McMule QED Corrections for Low-Energy Experiments
Yannick Ulrich

Abstract




\from

Deutschland

\committee

\member
Prof. Dr. Adrian Signer (Vorsitz)

\member
Prof. Dr. Stefano Pozzorini

\member
PD Dr. Michael Spira

\abstractE
We present
McMule
, a unified framework for the
calculation of NLO and NNLO corrections to many processes in QED with
massive fermions. This easily extendable program allows users to
calculate an arbitrary observable for any of the processes
implemented. These include various lepton decays as well as certain
low-energy scattering experiments such as
e\mu\to e\mu
and
\ell p\to\ell p
that can be measured to high enough a precision to warrant
QED corrections.

As part of our discussion, we will present a pedagogical introduction
to how these calculations are performed, focusing on technical aspects
supplemented with examples. Our goal is to provide a useful
introduction for those entering the field, covering all aspects
relevant for the practitioner.

This thesis is based on the following works to which the
author contributed to directly:

Chapter 1
Relevance of QED

A naive estimate of the size of radiative corrections in any theory is
generally driven by the size of its coupling. For quantum
electrodynamics (
) this is
\alpha\simeq 1/137
, implying that

corrections can often be safely ignored and are only ever
relevant for experiments with the highest precision. However, this
naive estimate overlooks two aspects.

•

corrections can easily become as large as ten percent if they
include large logarithms of widely different masses and kinematic
cuts.

•

The other aspect has to do with the experimental precision that
the theory has to ultimately match or even exceed. Current and
future experiments will be able to push the precision of event
rates – famously far more challenging to measure than shapes –
to well below the percent level, mandating next-to-leading order
(
) or even next-to-next-to-leading order (
)
calculations for many processes in
.

To facilitate the implementation of many
calculations (10 and
counting up to
at the time of this writing) we have
developed a unified framework called
McMule
(
M
onte
c
arlo for
Mu
ons and other
le
ptons). With it, new
processes can be added with relative ease, making
McMule
the
defining aspect of the thesis.

In what follows, we list some experiments that in some way or form are
relevant for
McMule
even though not all measure processes that can
be calculated with
McMule
. Next, we will discuss the implemented
processes sorted by order in perturbation theory.

The thesis-proper begins in Chapter
2
with a brief but
mostly standard introduction to
, defining some terminology
that we refer to later. Next, in Chapter
3
, we will discuss
different dimensional regularisation schemes with the practitioner in
mind, providing detailed examples. As a next big step, we will discuss
in Chapter
4
the infrared (
) subtraction schemes
used by
McMule
, the development of which was a corner stone of this
project. We will discuss practical aspects of a two-loop calculation
in Chapter
5
. For the technically inclined reader, we
will discuss aspects of
McMule
’s implementation in
Chapter
6
. Most of this will not be relevant for users
of
McMule
but serves as a guide on how
McMule
could be extended.
Finally, we will review some results obtained by
McMule
in
Chapter
7
before finally discussing future developments
in Chapter
8
.

1.1
Relevant experiments

As mentioned above, experimental progress requires more and more
theory support. While this is of course also true for the

experiments that certainly drove the development of technology, we
will focus exclusively on
here. Still, even though many
experiments have driven this development, an exhaustive list would not
be rewarding here. Instead, we will list some examples, mostly but not
exclusively, focussing on muonic physics that benefit from
fully-differential calculations:

•

Bhabha scattering has been used at various lepton colliders as a
standard candle for luminosity measurement. Hence, much
theoretical effort has been devoted to this process. Presently,

corrections, including leading electron mass effects,
are known and matched to
parton shower
(PS). For a review
of the state of Bhabha scattering, see for
example
[
9
]
.

•

The
g-2
experiment
[
10
]
at Brookhaven, its
successor at Fermilab
[
11
]
as well as a novel
experiment planed at J-PARC
[
12
]
are precisely
measuring the anomalous magnetic moment of the muon. This
observable is thought to be – due to its high precision – very
sensitive to
and indeed there is a tantalising
discrepancy between the measurement and the
prediction
(for example cf.
[
13
]
). The theoretical
prediction is plagued by uncertainties in the
hadronic vacuum
polarisation
(HVP) and the hadronic light-by-light
scattering.

However, as the
corrections to this process are known to
the five-loop level
[
14
]
and
g-2
is an
intrinsically inclusive observable, there is nothing further for

McMule
to directly add to the
calculation of
g-2
.
Hence, we will refrain from further commenting on the
determination of the
corrections to
g-2
.

•

The proposed MUonE experiment
[
15
,
16
,
17
]
plans to measure muon-electron
scattering to high precision in order to independently determine
the
contribution to the muon
g-2
through a novel
approach. For this to be competitive with the orthodox
methodology the relative systematic error needs to be under
control below
10^{-5}
. Aside from the obvious experimental
challenges connected to this, the
contributions should be
known to at least the
, level including mass effects and
matched to
.

•

The P2
[
18
]
, PRad
[
19
]
, and
MUSE
[
20
]
experiments are measuring elastic
electron-proton and muon-proton scattering, respectively. These
measurements help to determine the proton radius. However, PRad
uses Møller scattering (
ee\to ee
) for normalisation purposes,
the theory uncertainties of which are a leading systematic.

•

The MOLLER experiment
[
21
]
and the QWeak
experiment
[
22
]
measure the Weinberg angle at
low
Q^{2}
in electron-electron and electron-proton scattering,
respectively.

•

The MuLan experiment at the Paul Scherrer Institute (
)
has measured the muon lifetime to 1 ppm
[
23
]
.
This measurement was then, in combination with theoretical
calculations
[
24
,
25
]
, used to
extract the Fermi constant
G_{F}
.

•

The MEG experiment at

[
26
]
and its
successor MEG II
[
27
]
are searching for the
lepton-flavour violating (
) decay
\mu\to e\gamma
which
is predicted by many
scenarios. As any observation of
the
decay channel would constitute clear evidence of

physics, there is no pressing need for

corrections to this decay mode yet. However,
\mu\to e\gamma

becomes indistinguishable from the

\mu\to e\nu\bar{\nu}+\gamma
for small neutrino energies. Hence,
MEG is searching for a peak on a steeply falling background. It is
now unsurprising that precise knowledge of this background is
extremely helpful.

•

The Mu3e experiment at

[
28
,
29
]
is searching for the
decay
\mu\to eee
.
This is again difficult to disentangle from the

\mu\to e\nu\bar{\nu}+ee
for small neutrino energies.
Further, Mu3e is sensitive to light but weakly coupled

physics. These potential particles might not appear as a clear
bump over the falling background but as minute modifications to
certain differential observables. For these types of analyses,
radiative correction are essential.

•

The PADME experiment at the INFN National Laboratory of
Frascati
[
30
]
is searching for annihilation of

e^{+}e^{-}
pairs into a photon and a so-called dark photon. As such
the Standard Model process
ee\to\gamma\gamma
is of interest for
PADME.

The high experimental accuracy obtained or planned by these
experiments also requires a focussed theory support to make the best
use of their data. This means that from the theoretical side all
relevant processes need to be calculated

•

to the highest order in perturbation theory possible,

•

to be fully-differential, i.e. not just predicting inclusive cross
section but to instead being able to model the experimental
situation as closely as possible,

•

to include polarisation effects, should these matter
experimentally,

•

to include all necessary mass effects wherever possible, and

•

to include resummation where large logarithms are expected.

In the following sections we will comment on some of the processes in

McMule
, noticing some practical exceptions to the first point.

Even though
McMule
focusses on muonic processes, in some cases
tauonic (eg.
\tau\to e\nu\bar{\nu}\gamma
) or hadronic (eg.
\ell p\to\ell p
) processes can be included with only minor changes.

1.2
Processes at Leading order

While leading order (
) calculations are mostly trivial, that
does not necessarily make them futile. In fact, the polarised rare
muon decay
\mu\to e\nu\bar{\nu}+ee
was first calculated and made
available to the Mu3e collaboration in a predecessor of the
McMule

framework
[
31
]
. This was required by Mu3e to accurately
simulate their background including polarisation effects which heavily
influence angular distributions. While this was later superseded by a

calculation
[
1
,
32
]
, it was and
still is very helpful for the planning of the Mu3e experiment.

Additionally to their searches for
\mu\to e\gamma
, the MEG
collaboration also looks for the
decay of a muon into an
electron and a Majoron
J
, a Goldstone boson associated with a
hypothetical spontaneous breaking of lepton
number
[
33
,
34
]
(for a review of the
Majoron in the context of MEG see
[
35
,
36
]
, and
reference therein). This particle may decay promptly into

J\to\gamma\gamma

[
37
]
resulting in a
\mu\to e\gamma\gamma
signature. This becomes indistinguishable from the
double-radiative muon decay
\mu\to e\nu\bar{\nu}+\gamma\gamma
if
the neutrinos carry little energy. However, because the process is
heavily suppressed, a
study in
McMule
was sufficient to
model the relevant background.

1.3
Processes at next-to-leading order

For many background processes, a
study is sufficient to meet
the experimental requirements. Notable examples in
McMule
are the
radiative (
\mu\to e\nu\bar{\nu}+\gamma
) and rare (
\mu\to e\nu\bar{\nu}+ee
) muon decays. These processes serve as backgrounds to
MEG’s and Mu3e’s searches for
decays. As such, especially the
region of low neutrino energy is of particular interest.

studies conducted in

McMule

[
1
,
3
]
and
elsewhere
[
32
,
38
]
found relatively large
corrections, reaching up to ten percent in the relevant regimes. In
both cases, the
correction was driven through large
logarithms that somewhat spoil the perturbative expansion. As we will
see, this is a recurring theme in perturbative calculations in general
and
McMule
in particular. However, as in this case the corrections
are largely negative, the
background was generally
overestimated. Naturally this is preferable as it slightly increases
the actual efficiency.

From a theoretical point of view, an extension to the radiative tau
decay
\tau\to\ell\nu\bar{\nu}\gamma
seems natural. This was measured
by
BaBar

[
39
,
40
]
. In the electronic
case (
\ell=e
) the measured
branching ratio
(BR) was found to
be significantly above the
prediction
[
38
]
.
Using
McMule
we were able to study this discrepancy and found hints
towards a solution
[
3
,
4
]
.

With the high statistics of Belle and its successor, the rare
\tau

decays
\tau\to\nu\bar{\nu}\ell ll
become
accessible
[
41
]
. A
study that
merges
[
32
]
with
McMule

[
1
]
is
forthcoming
[
42
]
.

Finally, we should mention the
calculation of muon-electron
scattering
[
43
,
44
]
which was revisited
later in the context of the MUonE experiment
[
45
]

(shortly thereafter confirmed independently by
McMule

[
46
]

and
[
47
]
) as this allowed the first detailed study of the
situation that will be faced by the MUonE experiment.

1.4
Processes at next-to-next-to-leading order

Even though
is enough for many background studies, precision
measurements such as the measurement of the Fermi constant

G_{F}

[
23
]
, the extraction of the Michel parameters by
TWIST
[
48
]
, or the planned
fit by MUonE
require yet higher precision. In these cases we need to turn to

. While
corrections are essentially solved for
processes involving not too many particles (and no loops at
),
we are far from accomplishing the same feat for
. This is
mostly, but not exclusively, due to the lack of two-loop integrals.
Further complication arises from our wish to include mass effects
wherever possible as analytic solutions to integrals with multiple
masses quickly become impossible. In Chapter
5
we will
comment on this issue and potential shortcuts.

Currently,
McMule
implements the conventional muon
decay or

\mu\to\nu\bar{\nu}e

[
6
]

and
ee\to\nu\bar{\nu}
(which served as a test case) at
.
Further,
\mu
-
e
scattering can be split into gauge invariant
subsets by categorising which fermion radiates (cf.
Section
5.1
). Due to the lightness of the electron,
corrections associated to it are expected to be dominant. These
simpler contributions to
\mu
-
e
scattering are already implemented
in
McMule
at

[
7
]
. The

leptonic corrections to lepton-proton scattering, too, is implemented
because it can be obtained by tweaking
\mu
-
e
scattering.

1.5
Processes at next-to-next-to-next-to-leading order

While many observables were calculated at
for the

, only recently a select group of quantities reached

accuracy. Of these, only one – deep inelastic jet
production
[
49
]
– is fully-differential requiring a
subtraction scheme (cf. Chapter
4
).

The dominant contributions to muon-electron scattering would seem like
an ideal candidate to join this select group. It would also be the
first
calculation involving massive particles in initial and
final states as well as loops. While this calculation is not yet part
of
McMule
, progress is made towards its addition.

Chapter 2
Introduction to QED

Quantum Field Theories (
) have proven to be immensely
powerful tools to obtain evermore precise theoretical predictions for
the physics at the smallest scale. Usually this is understood in the
framework of the Standard Model (
) of electroweak and strong
interactions. However, we will not be discussing the full

with its strengths and weaknesses, suffice it to say that, while very
successful, we know that physics beyond the
(
) must
exist from a variety of evidence. When searching for

experimentally, it is crucial to have a precise understanding of the
background due to known physics – be that the
or one of its
subsets.

For all processes under consideration here, the background is
dominated by
, a particularly
simple part of the
. This
is defined through its
Lagrangian
1
1

1

Through this work, we will use upper Lorentz
indices regardless of whether an object is co- or contravariant. The
summation is still always implicit.

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}

\displaystyle=\sum_{i}\bar{\psi}_{i}(\mathrm{i}\gamma^{\mu}D^{\mu}-m_{i})\psi_{i}-\frac{1}{4}F^{\mu\nu}F^{\mu\nu}

(2.1a)

\displaystyle=\underbrace{\sum_{i}\bar{\psi}_{i}(\mathrm{i}\not{\partial}-m_{i})\psi_{i}-\frac{1}{4}\big(F^{\mu\nu}\big)^{2}}_{\text{free theory}}-e\sum_{i}\bar{\psi}_{i}\not{A}\psi_{i}\,,

(2.1b)

where
\psi_{i}
are the spinor fields of the leptons and
F^{\mu\nu}

the electromagnetic field tensor. In the second step we have
introduced some abbreviated notation, most notably the Feynman slash
notation for
\gamma^{\mu}a^{\mu}=\not{a}
.
D^{\mu}=\partial^{\mu}+\mathrm{i}eA^{\mu}
is called the gauge covariant derivative and is a compact
way to describe the interactions of leptons and photons.

Unfortunately,
– like all phenomenologically relevant

s – is not exactly solvable. However, the free theory, i.e.
the first two terms of (
2.1b
) are solvable. Hence, we
use perturbation theory to expand in the electromagnetic coupling

\displaystyle\alpha=\frac{e^{2}}{4\pi}\approx\frac{1}{137}\,.

(2.2)

This coupling is small enough to serve as an excellent expansion
parameter. Physical quantities like cross sections or decay rates are
now written as

\displaystyle\sigma=\sigma^{(0)}+\Big(\frac{\alpha}{\pi}\Big)^{1}\sigma^{(1)}+\Big(\frac{\alpha}{\pi}\Big)^{2}\sigma^{(2)}+\Big(\frac{\alpha}{\pi}\Big)^{3}\sigma^{(3)}+\mathcal{O}(\alpha^{4})\,,

(2.3)

where we refer to leading order (
,
\sigma^{(0)}
),
next-to-leading order (
,
\sigma^{(1)}
) etc. contributions.

When calculating the contributions
\sigma^{(i)}
we need to draw all
connected and amputated Feynman diagrams contributing to the same
observable including some fixed number of couplings. Here we
distinguish
and
.

Obtaining the
contribution
\sigma^{(0)}
(which itself can
contain further factors of
\alpha
) is in most cases relatively
straightforward. Note that
\sigma^{(0)}
could already contain
loops, i.e. a
process. We do not consider this
case here. Instead, we assume that the first order is always given
through a number of tree-level diagrams. Hence, we can use the number
of loops and the order in perturbation theory interchangeably.

Once we have the matrix element, we need to integrate over the phase
space to obtain a cross section or decay rate. At this stage, experimental
subtleties enter. Modelling these as closely as possible may require
us to include complicated cuts, making analytic integration over the
phase space quickly infeasible. Hence, we will do the integration
numerically. To facilitate the cuts, we define the so-called

[
50
]
. This function takes
as arguments the four-momenta of all particles involved in the
reaction and returns the experimentally measured quantity. The
measurement function has to fulfil certain criteria. We will comment
below on properties it has to fulfil beyond
. But even at

, an example for an invalid function would be to ask for a
number of photons without also specifying the minimum energy of these
photons. We call a calculation that can implement any measurement
function without renewed effort
.

We encounter our first loop diagram in
\sigma^{(1)}
. Because the
momenta of the particles in the loop is not fixed through the momenta
of the external particles, we have to integrate over them.
Unfortunately, these
can be divergent for large
momenta (ultraviolet,
) or soft or collinear momenta
(infrared,
). Hence, the first thing we need to do is to

these divergences. This is usually done by shifting
the dimension of space-time away from 4 to
d=4-2\epsilon

(dimensional regularisation,
). Both
and

singularities now appear as poles in
1/\epsilon
. We will explain how
to do this formally and mathematically consistent in
Chapter
3
.

The loop integrals required to solve practical processes tend to be
rather complicated. This complexity obviously increases the more loops
are included. Further, the problem is also made more complicated
through the inclusion of more external particles (with potentially
different masses) as this increases the number of relevant or

\mu_{i}
that enter in the actual loop integrals.
This is in contrast to other scales (inactive scales) that do not enter
loop integrals like the mass of spectator particles.

Further background information on these topics can be found in various
textbooks such as
[
51
,
52
,
53
]
.

2.1
Renormalisation

When computing scattering amplitudes with the
Lagrangian (
2.1
) beyond leading order, we encounter

singularities that are indicative of our ignorance of the physics at
very high scales. These
singularities are dealt with through

. The main idea is to express scattering
amplitudes in terms of renormalised fields and renormalised
parameters, rather than their bare counterparts, s.t. no

singularities are present. If to all orders in perturbation theory all

singularities can systematically be absorbed by a finite
number of

Z_{i}
, we call the theory

. It can be shown that
as well as the
full
are renormalisable.

At this stage we will start using
\psi_{0,i}
,
A_{0}^{\mu}
and

m_{0,i}
for the bare quantities of (
2.1
). The variables

\psi_{i}
etc. shall henceforth be reserved for the renormalised
quantities. In Section
2.1.1
we will be more specific
what is meant by that.

Relating the bare quantities
\psi_{0,i}
,
A_{0}^{\mu}
and
m_{0,i}
of
the Lagrangian (
2.1
) to the renormalised ones
2
2

2

In
the notation of
[
53
]

Z_{2}=Z_{\psi}
and

Z_{3}=Z_{A}=Z_{\alpha}^{-1}
.

\displaystyle\psi_{0,i}=Z_{2,i}^{1/2}\psi_{i}\,,\quad m_{0,i}=Z_{m,i}m_{i}\quad\text{and}\quad A_{0}^{\mu}=Z_{3}^{1/2}A^{\mu}\,,

(2.4a)

we obtain

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}

\displaystyle=\sum_{i}Z_{2,i}\ \bar{\psi}_{i}(\mathrm{i}\not{\partial}-Z_{m,i}m_{i})\psi_{i}-\frac{1}{4}Z_{3}\ \big(F^{\mu\nu}\big)^{2}-\sum_{i}Z_{2}Z_{3}^{1/2}\ e_{0}\bar{\psi}_{i}\not{A}\psi\,.

(2.4b)

Z_{2}
and
Z_{3}
are called the wave-function renormalisation factors,
whereas
Z_{m}
is the mass renormalisation. We also need to renormalise
the coupling
e_{0}
. This is usually expressed in terms of the
vertex-renormalisation factor
Z_{1}
as

\displaystyle e_{0}=e\frac{Z_{1}}{Z_{2}Z_{3}^{1/2}}=Z_{3}^{-1/2}\ e\,.

(2.4c)

In the last step we have used that to all orders in

Z_{1}=Z_{2}
, due to the
.

In (
2.1
) we have omitted the gauge-fixing terms, containing
the gauge parameter, usually called
\xi
. We will always set this
term to
\xi=1
, i.e. perform all calculations in Feynman gauge. In
general,
\xi
has to be renormalised as well. However, it can be
shown that, as long as one only considers on-shell scattering
amplitudes or renormalisation constants, this does not matter at any
order in

[
54
,
55
,
56
]

(also cf.
[
57
]
showing that this ceases to be true
in
at the three-loop level).

2.1.1
Renormalisation schemes

In
, the
poles are manifest as poles

1/\epsilon_{\text{UV}}
, where we temporarily use the
label to
distinguish
from
poles. At
n
loops, the highest

pole is of order
1/\epsilon^{n}_{\text{UV}}
. The
part
of the
Z_{i}=1+\delta Z_{i}
is uniquely fixed by the requirement that
all
singularities are absorbed. At one-loop accuracy they are

\displaystyle\begin{split}Z_{1}=Z_{2}&=1+\frac{\alpha}{4\pi}\frac{-1}{2\epsilon_{\text{UV}}}+\mathcal{O}\big(\epsilon_{\text{UV}}^{0},\alpha^{2}\big)\,,\\
Z_{m}&=1+\frac{\alpha}{4\pi}\frac{-3}{2\epsilon_{\text{UV}}}+\mathcal{O}\big(\epsilon_{\text{UV}}^{0},\alpha^{2}\big)\,,\\
Z_{3}&=1+\frac{\alpha}{4\pi}\frac{\beta_{0}}{\epsilon_{\text{UV}}}+\mathcal{O}\big(\epsilon_{\text{UV}}^{0},\alpha^{2}\big)\,,\end{split}

(2.5)

where
\beta_{0}=-4/3N_{F}
in a theory with
N_{F}
flavours. However,
there is quite some freedom in choosing a
, i.e. prescription how to fix the terms of the renormalisation
factors that are UV finite. Note that, to the loop order given in
(
2.5
), it does not matter whether
\alpha
has been
renormalised or not, as the difference would be

\mathcal{O}(\alpha^{2})
.

For most choices of the renormalisation scheme, the renormalised
parameters
\alpha
and
m
start to exhibit a behaviour known as

. These parameters become dependent on the

\mu
, the scale at which the

subtraction is made. In particular, this is encountered for the
coupling whose scale dependence is governed by the
\beta
function
as

\displaystyle\frac{\partial\alpha(\mu)}{\partial\log\mu}=2\beta(\alpha(\mu))\quad\text{with}\quad\beta(\alpha)=-\alpha\Bigg(\frac{\alpha}{4\pi}\ \beta_{0}+\mathcal{O}(\alpha^{2})\Bigg)\,.

(2.6)

This is a first example of what is called a
renormalisation
group equation
(RGE). By choosing the renormalisation scale at the
appropriate scale of the experiment,
Q^{2}
, one avoids large
logarithms
\log Q^{2}/\mu^{2}
that arise when integrating
(
2.6
).

The most common renormalisation schemes are the

scheme
, where the finite terms vanish up to some common factors, and
the
on-shell scheme
(OS). The latter will be the default in
this project, s.t. for example
m
and
\alpha
refer to the

mass and coupling. Hence, the
scheme deserves some further
elaboration.

The
scheme is constructed to most faithfully reproduce the
classical limit for the input parameters at
Q^{2}=0
without the
parameters ever experiencing running. For example, this means that the
electron mass really is
m_{e}\simeq 0.511\,{\rm MeV}
. To achieve this,
let us consider the one-loop corrections to the fermion propagator
as (following
[
53
]
)

\displaystyle\Sigma(p)=m_{0}\,\Sigma_{1}(p^{2})+(\not{p}-m_{0})\Sigma_{2}(p^{2})\,.

(2.7)

To get the physical propagator
S
from the bare propagator

S_{0}=1/(\not{p}-m_{0})
we have to sum an infinite number of

\Sigma

\displaystyle\begin{split}S(p)&=S_{0}(p)+S_{0}\Sigma(p)S_{0}+S_{0}\Sigma(p)S_{0}\Sigma(p)S_{0}+\cdots\\
&=\frac{1}{S_{0}^{-1}-\Sigma}=\frac{1}{\not{p}-m_{0}-\Sigma}\,.\end{split}

(2.8)

We now want to describe this in the renormalised quantities, i.e.

\displaystyle S(p)\frac{1}{\not{p}-m_{0}-\Sigma}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle!}}{{=}}\frac{Z_{2}}{\not{p}-m}+\text{regular}\,,

(2.9)

where regular refers to terms that do not contribute to the pole as

\not{p}\to m
. The
mass of the electron is now just
defined as the pole of the propagator. In principle we could just
plug (
2.7
) into (
2.9
) and obtain

\displaystyle Z_{m}=1-\Sigma(m)\quad\text{and}\quad Z_{2}=1+\frac{\mathrm{d}\Sigma}{\mathrm{d}\not{p}}\Big|_{p^{2}=m^{2}}\,.

(2.10)

However, calculating
\Sigma^{\prime}(m)
can be cumbersome, especially
beyond the one-loop level. Hence, we follow the method set out
by
[
58
]
: we begin by writing down the perturbative
expansion of
\Sigma
,
Z_{m}
, and
Z_{2}
with the most general
dependence of
p^{2}
and
m_{0}
allowed by the loop integration

\displaystyle\begin{split}Z_{2}&=1+\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}\bigg(\frac{\alpha_{0}}{m^{2\epsilon}}\bigg)^{n}F(n)\,,\\
Z_{m}&=1+\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}\bigg(\frac{\alpha_{0}}{m^{2\epsilon}}\bigg)^{n}M(n)\,,\\
\Sigma&=\phantom{1+}\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}\bigg(\frac{\alpha_{0}}{(p^{2})^{\epsilon}}\bigg)^{n}\Big(m_{0}\Sigma_{1}^{(n)}\big(\tfrac{m_{0}^{2}}{p^{2}}\big)+(\not{p}-m_{0})\Sigma_{2}^{(n)}\big(\tfrac{m_{0}^{2}}{p^{2}}\big)\Big)\,,\end{split}

(2.11)

where everything is expressed in the bare coupling
\alpha_{0}
. This
is now what we plug into (
2.9
) with
m=Z_{m}^{-1}m_{0}
,
expanding to the desired order in
\alpha
. At one-loop accuracy

\displaystyle M(1)=-\Sigma_{1}^{(1)}(1)\quad\text{and}\quad F(1)=\Sigma_{2}^{(1)}(1)-2\Sigma_{1}^{\prime(1)}(1)-2\epsilon\Sigma_{1}^{(1)}(1)\,.

(2.12)

It turns out that this way we still need to calculate
\Sigma_{i}(1)

and
\Sigma_{i}^{\prime}(1)
but we are allowed to set
p^{2}=m_{0}^{2}

before

the loop integration.

For the photon field – and by extension the coupling – we proceed
similarly, finding

\displaystyle Z_{3}=\frac{1}{1-\Pi(0)}\,,

(2.13)

where
\Pi
is the usual photon self energy, defined through

\displaystyle\Pi^{\mu\nu}(p)=(p^{2}g^{\mu\nu}-p^{\mu}p^{\nu})\Pi(p^{2})\,.

(2.14)

For a theory with only one massive fermion
\Pi(0)
depends only on
the mass of this flavour. One can easily calculate
that
[
53
]

\displaystyle\Pi^{(1)}(0)=-\frac{4}{3}\frac{e_{0}^{2}}{(4\pi)^{d/2}}\ {m^{-2\epsilon}}\ \Gamma(\epsilon)\,.

(2.15)

This way, we have a relation between the
coupling
\bar{\alpha}

and the
coupling
\alpha
at one-loop accuracy

\displaystyle\bar{\alpha}(\mu)=\alpha\bigg(1+\frac{4}{3}\frac{\alpha}{4\pi}\log\frac{\mu^{2}}{m^{2}}\bigg)\,.

(2.16)

In principle we are free to renormalise the masses and coupling in any
scheme we wish. For the fermion masses, we will always choose the
on-shell scheme. This mass is scale independent and corresponds
directly to the measured value of the lepton masses. Our standard
choice for the coupling is also the on-shell scheme. In this scheme
the coupling is scale independent and corresponds to the measured
value
\alpha\sim 1/137
in the Thomson limit. However, we
occasionally work with
\bar{\alpha}
, the coupling in the

-scheme. As mentioned above, this coupling depends on the
renormalisation scale
\mu
. If we consider processes at high energies

Q
(compared to the fermion masses) this scheme can be useful, as
setting
\mu\sim Q
allows to resum large logarithms.

All renormalisation constants required up to two-loop accuracy can be
found, expressed in the bare coupling, in Appendix
B
.

2.1.2
Practical renormalisation

In order to obtain scattering matrix elements at a particular order in
perturbation theory, we start by computing all connected and amputated
Feynman diagrams to the required order. Amputated means we do not
include diagrams with self-energy insertions on external
lines. According to the
reduction formula, such contributions
are properly included by multiplying the unrenormalised amplitude by

\sqrt{Z_{i}}
for each external line, where
Z_{i}
is the wave-function
renormalisation factor in the on-shell scheme. This results in the
renormalised scattering amplitude, but still expressed in terms of the
bare coupling, masses, and gauge parameter. To absorb all

singularities the bare parameters have to be expressed in terms of the
corresponding renormalised parameters.

Renormalisation beyond one-loop has certain subtleties, most of which
can be explained by pure counting of powers of the coupling
\alpha
.
At the one-loop level, the renormalisation constants
\delta Z_{i}^{(1)}
always
just multiply a tree-level amplitude
\mathcal{A}^{(0)}
. This ceases to be
sufficient at the two-loop level. Now, additionally to the product of
two-loop renormalisation constants
\delta Z_{i}^{(2)}
with the tree-level
amplitude
\mathcal{A}^{(0)}
, we need to include one-loop renormalisation of the
one-loop amplitude
\mathcal{A}^{(1)}\times\delta Z_{i}^{(1)}
. Further, the two-loop
renormalisation constants
\delta Z_{i}^{(2)}
themselves need to be renormalised
using constants
\delta Z_{i}^{(1)}
. This is called
.

Particular attention has to be given to the fermion-mass
renormalisation. Replacing
m_{0,i}=Z_{m,i}m_{i}=m_{i}+\delta m_{i}^{(1)}+\ldots
in the lower-order amplitudes and expanding in

\alpha
produces all mass counterterms, also those on external lines.
However, the latter have already been taken into account by the

reduction. Hence, in practical calculations it is
advantageous to perform mass renormalisation by explicitly computing
Feynman diagrams with mass counterterms
\delta m_{i}^{(l)}
on internal
lines only.

Hence, we arrive at the following practical procedure for two-loop
renormalisation:

1.

For every massive external particle, add the wave function
renormalisation for heavy fermions
Z_{h}

\displaystyle\Big(\frac{1}{2}\ \delta Z_{h}^{(2)}-\frac{1}{8}\ \big(\delta Z_{h}^{(1)}\big)^{2}\Big)\times\mathcal{A}^{(0)}\quad\text{and}\quad\frac{1}{2}\ \delta Z_{h}^{(1)}\times\mathcal{A}^{(1)}\,.

(2.17)

2.

For every massless external fermion, add
\tfrac{1}{2}\delta Z_{l}^{(2)}\times\mathcal{A}^{(0)}
,
keeping in mind that these contributions are induced through terms
proportional to the number of heavy flavours. This means that

Z_{l}=1
to all orders in theories without at least one massive
flavour.

3.

For every external photon, we have to add the corresponding

\tfrac{1}{2}\delta Z_{3}^{(1)}+...
as above.

4.

Perform the mass renormalisation of the fermions, i.e. add
counterterm diagrams obtained through the substitution

\displaystyle\frac{\mathrm{i}}{\not{p}+m}\to\frac{\mathrm{i}}{\not{p}+m}\delta m^{(l)}\frac{\mathrm{i}}{\not{p}+m}

(2.18)

for internal fermion lines at the amplitude level. We need
l=2

for tree-level diagrams and
l=1
for one-loop diagrams, as well
as double insertions with
l=1
for tree-level diagrams. Note that
this does not correspond to replacing
m_{0}=Z_{m}m
and
expanding again in
\alpha
at the matrix element level, as this
would lead to the double counting of the mass renormalisation of
external lines as discussed above.

5.

Perform the coupling renormalisation by shifting
\alpha_{0}\to(1+\delta Z_{\alpha}^{(1)}+\delta Z_{\alpha}^{(2)})\times\alpha
and sorting terms
according to the now renormalised coupling, dropping every term
with too high a power in
\alpha
.

If we have no internal photons at
, i.e. the number of
external photons coincides with the number of
vertices,
this step and Step 2 above cancel exactly thanks to the Ward
identity, meaning neither is necessary (cf. (
2.4c
)).

2.2
Effective theories and the muon decay

A recurring theme of this project is the muon decay as an example
process of high phenomenological relevance. However, the muon does not
decay in pure
as the only
changing
particle in the
is the
W
-boson. The amplitude for
\mu(p)\to e(q)\nu_{\mu}(q_{3})\bar{\nu}_{e}(q_{4})
in the
can be written as

\displaystyle{\cal A}=\frac{\mathrm{i}g}{\sqrt{2}}\ \Big[\bar{u}_{\nu_{\mu}}(q_{3})\gamma^{\alpha}P_{L}u_{\mu}(p)\Big]\ \frac{-\mathrm{i}}{q_{W}^{2}-m_{W}^{2}}\Bigg(g^{\alpha\beta}-\frac{q_{W}^{\alpha}q_{W}^{\beta}}{m_{W}^{2}}\Bigg)\frac{\mathrm{i}g}{\sqrt{2}}\ \Big[\bar{u}_{e}(q)\gamma^{\beta}P_{L}u_{\nu_{e}}(q_{4})\Big]\,,

(2.19)

with the
W
coupling
g
and the usual left-handed projector

P_{L}=\tfrac{1}{2}(1-\gamma_{5})
. While it is of course possible to perform
all calculations, including radiative corrections, in the full

, that is often unnecessary. Because the
W
momentum

q_{W}=p-q_{3}\sim m_{\mu}
is much smaller than its mass
m_{W}
, the
W

propagator simplifies to

\displaystyle\frac{-\mathrm{i}}{q_{W}^{2}-m_{W}^{2}}\Bigg(g^{\alpha\beta}-\frac{q_{W}^{\alpha}q_{W}^{\beta}}{m_{W}^{2}}\Bigg)=\frac{\mathrm{i}}{m_{W}^{2}}g^{\alpha\beta}+\mathcal{O}\Big(\frac{q_{W}^{2}}{m_{W}^{2}}\Big)\,,

(2.20)

resulting in

\displaystyle{\cal A}=-\mathrm{i}\frac{g^{2}}{2m_{W}^{2}}\ \Big[\bar{u}_{\nu_{\mu}}(q_{3})\gamma^{\alpha}P_{L}u_{\mu}(p)\Big]\ \Big[\bar{u}_{e}(q)\gamma^{\alpha}P_{L}u_{\nu_{e}}(q_{4})\Big]+\mathcal{O}\Big(\frac{q_{W}^{2}}{m_{W}^{2}}\Big)\,.

(2.21)

Further, because of the large
W
mass, radiative corrections due to
the
W
are also suppressed by
\mathcal{O}(m_{\mu}^{2}/m_{W}^{2})
. Hence,
instead of introducing a propagating
W
boson, we
augment (
2.1
) by

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}=\mathcal{L}_{\text{QED}}-\frac{4\,G_{F}}{\sqrt{2}}\left(\bar{\psi}_{\nu_{\mu}}\gamma^{\mu}P_{L}\psi_{\mu}\right)\left(\bar{\psi}_{e}\gamma^{\mu}P_{L}\psi_{\nu_{e}}\right)\,.

(2.22)

Here, we have introduced a
with a
dimensionful coupling
G_{F}
. At energies far below
m_{W}
, the exchange
of a
W
boson is described well by (
2.22
). This is a first
example of an
effective field theory
(EFT). We have encoded the
high-energy dynamics of the
W
into a so-called

G_{F}
. The relation of
G_{F}
with parameters of the full

is found through a
by calculating
a process both in the full
and in the
and then fixing

G_{F}
s.t. in the expansion of the
, i.e.
m_{W}\to\infty
,
both agree. In our case we find at LO

\displaystyle\frac{G_{F}}{\sqrt{2}}=\frac{g^{2}}{8m_{W}^{2}}=\frac{1}{2v^{2}}\,,

(2.23)

where
v
is the vacuum expectation value of the Higgs field in the
Standard Model.

There is one more simplification to be done in (
2.22
).
Since we cannot measure the neutrinos it is unfortunate that they take
such a prominent role in the calculation. Instead, we would prefer
everything related to neutrinos to factorise. Fortunately, there exist
so-called
to re-arrange spinor bilinears such
as the ones in (
2.22
). In our case we find

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}=\mathcal{L}_{\text{QED}}-\frac{4\,G_{F}}{\sqrt{2}}\left(\bar{\psi}_{e}\gamma^{\mu}P_{L}\psi_{\mu}\right)\left(\bar{\psi}_{\nu_{\mu}}\gamma^{\mu}P_{L}\psi_{\nu_{e}}\right)\,.

(2.24)

Because (
2.24
) is the theory we will be using to calculate
radiative corrections to the muon decay, we have to face the issue
that in the strict meaning of the word, (
2.24
) is not
renormalisable, requiring in general an infinite number of
Z_{i}
.
However, as long as we do not consider a perturbative expansion in

G_{F}
, we can maintain predictability by renormalising
G_{F}
as just
another coupling through a new
Z_{G_{F}}
which would usually be
assumed in the
scheme. However, it turns out that we do
not even have to do that as
Z_{G_{F}}=1
to all orders in
.

To see this, we first note that
\mathcal{L}
is invariant under the
exchange
\psi_{e}\to\gamma^{5}\psi_{e}
and

m_{e}\to-m_{e}

[
59
]
. However, because this exchanges the
vector and axial-vector current, we only really need to consider a
vectorial coupling. Further, because the neutrinos are uncharged,
there is no difference between
G_{F}
and the normal
coupling
from a renormalisation aspect. Hence, the
Ward identity

Z_{1}=Z_{2}
still holds. The only contribution left to influence

Z_{G_{F}}
is the equivalent of
Z_{3}
. The
contribution to
this quantity can be fixed by considering
corrections to

\nu\nu\to\nu\nu
. Because the neutrinos are uncharged under

, these vanish exactly. Of course, terms that are higher order
in
G_{F}
exist in principle.

To summarise, we will be using (
2.24
) for all calculations
involving the muon decay. As long as we only consider LO in
G_{F}
, the
results will be UV finite after
renormalisation. Higher-order
corrections in
G_{F}
have been considered in
[
60
]
.

2.3
Infrared safety

After the
renormalisation, our
matrix element
is unfortunately still
divergent. This is in so-far physical
that
singularities cannot just be absorbed through
redefinition of quantities. Instead, such fully

quantities are just not physical until they are combined with

matrix elements involving extra radiation. While it is of
course possible to distinguish events with extra hard radiation in an
appropriate detector, there always exist a physical cut-off
\Delta

below which radiation cannot be detected any more. As cross sections
usually scale like
\log\Delta
, the cross section would diverge when
integrating over the entire phase space including
\Delta\to 0
. This

divergence is exactly cancelled by the
divergence
of the virtual matrix element. Observables for which this is true are
called
. Totally inclusive cross sections like

\displaystyle\sigma(a+b\to c+d+\text{any number of}\ \gamma)\quad\text{where }c,d\neq\gamma

(2.25)

are examples for IR safe observables. The existence of these
observables is guaranteed by the Kinoshita-Lee-Nauenberg theorem
(
) that states that any sufficiently inclusive observable
(such as the total cross section) will always be finite. The
condition imposed by the
theorem can be translated into a
condition on the measurement function as we will see
later
[
50
]
.

As mentioned above, we would very much like to integrate over the
phase space numerically. However, we cannot do that in
d
dimensions.
Instead, we need special methods to treat these divergences in
d

dimensions without spoiling our ability to integrate numerically. We
will discuss one such method in detail in Chapter
4
.

In a theory with massless fermions there is an additional source of
singularities due to (hard) radiation becoming
with a
massless fermion. This is not an immediate problem as we will mostly be
dealing with massive particles where the mass
m
serves as a
regulator, giving rise to
\log m
. However, these

pseudo-collinear singularities
(PCS) cause a lot of numerical
instabilities making them difficult to integrate over as we will
discuss in Section
4.4.3
and again in Section
6.4.1
.

An unfortunate aspect of perturbative calculations is that, for
processes with very different scales
\mu_{i}
, logarithms of the form

L\sim\log\mu_{1}^{2}/\mu_{2}^{2}
become very large. Hence, each new loop
order not just brings a new power of
\alpha
but also often two
powers of
L
– one due to soft and one due to collinear emission. At
least in
, this can easily become large enough s.t.
\alpha_{s}L^{2}\sim 1
, spoiling the expansion completely. But even in

this is troublesome as it would require computations to an infeasibly
high order.

This means that we have to revise our counting (
2.3
),
assuming that we get two powers of
L
per loop order

\displaystyle\begin{split}\sigma=\sigma^{(0)}_{0}&+\Big(\frac{\alpha}{\pi}\Big)^{1}L^{2}\sigma^{(1)}_{2}+\Big(\frac{\alpha}{\pi}\Big)^{1}L^{1}\sigma^{(1)}_{1}+\Big(\frac{\alpha}{\pi}\Big)^{1}L^{0}\sigma^{(1)}_{0}\\
&+\Big(\frac{\alpha}{\pi}\Big)^{2}L^{4}\sigma^{(2)}_{4}+\Big(\frac{\alpha}{\pi}\Big)^{2}L^{3}\sigma^{(2)}_{3}+\Big(\frac{\alpha}{\pi}\Big)^{2}L^{2}\sigma^{(2)}_{2}+\Big(\frac{\alpha}{\pi}\Big)^{2}L^{1}\sigma^{(2)}_{1}+\Big(\frac{\alpha}{\pi}\Big)^{1}L^{0}\sigma^{(1)}_{0}\\
&+\Big(\frac{\alpha}{\pi}\Big)^{3}L^{6}\sigma^{(3)}_{6}+\Big(\frac{\alpha}{\pi}\Big)^{3}L^{5}\sigma^{(3)}_{5}+\Big(\frac{\alpha}{\pi}\Big)^{3}L^{4}\sigma^{(3)}_{4}+\cdots\,.\end{split}

(2.26)

The rows of this equation correspond to the
results
obtained above. However, we can use the fact that the terms

\sigma^{(i)}_{2i}
usually follow a predictable pattern. Hence, if we use

\alpha L^{2}/\pi
as the expansion parameter instead of
\alpha/\pi
we
can get control over these logarithms. This process is known as

. The first column is known as

leading-logarithm
(LL), the second as
next-to-leading
logarithm
(NLL) and so on.

A particularly efficient way to calculate the
contribution is
a
parton shower
(PS). This involves including a cascade of soft
and collinear radiation to all involved particles. This is
particularly interesting because
can be constructed
independent of the measurement function. Unfortunately, at the time of
this writing, no

has been presented though work is
ongoing towards a construction of such a method. Until then,

resummation must be done anew for each observable. However, much work
has been dedicated to obtaining results that are almost

accurate.

2.4
Infrared prediction

When performing multi-loop calculations, an important cross-check is
the cancellation of
singularities. However, to use this as a
practical tool, it is necessary to predict the
poles without
having to calculate the (potentially very difficult) real corrections.

For this discussion we assume that we work in
with (some)
massless flavours instead as the
structure will be much
richer. We will come back to massive
later.

Infrared predictions have been worked out for massless
in
dimensional regularisation
[
61
,
62
,
63
,
64
]
. This was extended to gauge theories
with massive fermions
[
65
]
.

To predict the
structure of
we remember that in an

, the Wilson coefficients need to be renormalised. However,
the
singularities removed this way were not present in the
full theory. This implies that the part of the calculation entering
the Wilson coefficient is
divergent. We now need to construct
a low-energy theory s.t. its
divergences match the

poles of
because we can predict
singularities using
renormalisation theory. The
in question is

soft-collinear effective theory
(SCET)
[
66
,
67
,
68
]
(for a pedagogical introduction, for
example cf.
[
69
]
) that splits soft and collinear
modes off from the full underlying theory, be it
or
.

While a full derivation of the
prediction is well beyond the
scope of this work, we can sketch the necessary concepts, especially
because we will encounter some of them later.

Let us define the, in principle, all-order renormalised
3
3

3

We
will assume that the coupling is renormalised in the
scheme
to be consistent with the literature
matrix element
\mathcal{M}
for
an arbitrary process as the sum of
\ell
-loop contributions

\mathcal{M}^{(\ell)}

\displaystyle\mathcal{M}=\sum_{\ell=0}^{\infty}\mathcal{M}^{(\ell)}=\mathcal{M}^{(0)}+\mathcal{M}^{(1)}+\mathcal{M}^{(2)}+...\,,

(2.27)

where each
\mathcal{M}^{(\ell)}
contains one power more of

\bar{\alpha}
. We now define the corresponding
{\bf Z}
s.t.

\displaystyle\mathcal{M}_{\text{sub}}=\big({\bf Z}\big)^{-1}\mathcal{M}\quad\text{with}\quad{\bf Z}=1+\delta{\bf Z}^{(1)}+\delta{\bf Z}^{(2)}+...

(2.28)

is finite in the limits
\epsilon\to 0
. We call
\mathcal{M}_{\text{sub}}

, because
{\bf Z}
is constructed to contain no
finite parts, up to trivial terms induced by the loop measure.
However, just like
renormalisation introduces a
renormalisation scale, the factorisation into
finite and

divergent quantities of (
2.28
) introduces a
new
.

It is important to note, that, while important for what follows, there
is nothing wrong with defining a different
{\bf Z}^{\prime}
that contains
finite parts but no factorisation scale (cf. Chapter
4
).
For now, however, we will stick to
-like subtraction and
re-write (
2.28
) to account for the new scale
\mu

\displaystyle\mathcal{M}_{\text{sub}}(\mu)=\big({\bf Z}(\mu)\big)^{-1}\mathcal{M}\,.

(2.29)

Next, we note that, even though
\mathcal{M}_{\text{sub}}
and
{\bf Z}

depend on the factorisation scale, the original matrix element

\mathcal{M}
does not. Hence, we can a obtain a
for

\mathcal{M}_{\text{sub}}(\mu)
by differentiating (
2.29
)
w.r.t.
\mu
, resulting in

\displaystyle\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}\log\mu}\mathcal{M}_{\text{sub}}(\mu)={\bf\Gamma}(\mu)\mathcal{M}_{\text{sub}}(\mu)\,.

(2.30a)

with

\displaystyle{\bf\Gamma}(\mu)=-\frac{\mathrm{d}\log{\bf Z}}{\mathrm{d}\log\mu}\,.

(2.30b)

Here,
\bf\Gamma(\mu)
is the
of the
process. This is very similar to how the anomalous dimension of, for
example, the fermion that is obtained by

\displaystyle\gamma_{f}=\frac{\mathrm{d}\log\bar{Z}_{2}}{\mathrm{d}\log\mu_{F}}\,,

(2.31)

with the
fermion wave function renormalisation
\bar{Z}_{2}
.

The formal solution of (
2.30b
) is
[
64
]

\displaystyle\log{\bf Z}(\mu)=\int_{\mu}^{\infty}\frac{\mathrm{d}\mu^{\prime}}{\mu^{\prime}}{\bf\Gamma}(\mu^{\prime})=\int_{\log\mu}^{\infty}\mathrm{d}(\log\mu^{\prime}){\bf\Gamma}(\mu^{\prime})\,.

(2.32)

Unfortunately, integrating (
2.30b
) is complicated by the fact
that
\bf\Gamma
is not just a function of
\mu
but also of the

coupling
\bar{\alpha}(\mu)
that has its own
RGE (
2.6
)
4
4

4

In
[
64
]
,
\beta
is
defined as
\beta_{\text{\cite[cite]{[\@@bibref{Number}{Becher:2009qa}{}{}]}}}=2\beta
.

\displaystyle\frac{\partial\alpha(\mu)}{\partial\log\mu}=2\beta(\alpha(\mu))\,.

(2.33)

Hence, we need to distinguish the explicit scale dependence from the
one induced by the running of
\bar{\alpha}
. We substitute

\mu^{\prime}\to\alpha^{\prime}(\mu^{\prime})
and write schematically

\displaystyle\log{\bf Z}(\mu)=\int_{0}^{\bar{\alpha}}\frac{\mathrm{d}\alpha^{\prime}}{-2\beta(\alpha^{\prime})}\Bigg({\bf\Gamma}(\alpha^{\prime})+\int_{0}^{\alpha}\frac{\mathrm{d}\alpha^{\prime\prime}}{-2\beta(\alpha^{\prime\prime})}\frac{\partial{\bf\Gamma}(\alpha^{\prime\prime})}{\partial(\log\mu)}\Bigg)\,,

(2.34)

where we have used that the only explicit dependency of
\log\mu
in

{\bf\Gamma}
is linear as we will see below. By identifying

{\bf\Gamma}^{\prime}
as

\displaystyle{\bf\Gamma}^{\prime}=\frac{\partial{\bf\Gamma}}{\partial\log\mu}\,,

(2.35)

we can solve this order-by-order
[
63
,
64
]

\displaystyle\log{\bf Z}=\Big(\frac{\bar{\alpha}}{4\pi}\Big)\Bigg(\frac{{\bf\Gamma}_{1}^{\prime}}{4\epsilon^{2}}+\frac{{\bf\Gamma}_{1}}{2\epsilon}\Bigg)+\Big(\frac{\bar{\alpha}}{4\pi}\Big)^{2}\Bigg(-\frac{3\beta\cdot{\bf\Gamma}_{1}^{\prime}}{16\epsilon^{3}}-\frac{\beta\cdot{\bf\Gamma}_{1}}{4\epsilon^{2}}+\frac{{\bf\Gamma}_{2}^{\prime}}{16\epsilon^{2}}+\frac{{\bf\Gamma}_{2}}{4\epsilon}\Bigg)+\mathcal{O}(\alpha^{3})\,,

(2.36)

where
{\bf\Gamma}_{i}
(
{\bf\Gamma}_{i}^{\prime}
) is the
\mathcal{O}(\alpha^{i})
coefficient of
{\bf\Gamma}
(
{\bf\Gamma}^{\prime}
) and

\beta\cdot{\bf\Gamma}_{1}=\beta_{0}\cdot{\bf\Gamma}_{1}
in the notation
of
[
70
]
and Appendix
B
.

It has been conjectured by
[
63
]
that, assuming a
theory without massive flavours, the anomalous dimension
{\bf\Gamma}

can be constructed to all orders by just considering two-particle
correlations. This ceases to be true in a theory with massive
particles
[
71
]
, requiring a more complicated
structure
[
65
]
that we will not reproduce here.

For the two-parton case
{\bf\Gamma}
is constructed from a

{\gamma}_{\text{cusp}}
relating two partons and quark anomalous
dimensions
{\gamma}_{i}
(or
{\gamma}_{I}
for massive quarks) that has to do with
just one parton. Assuming trivial colour-flow (as in
t\to Wb
or of
course any
calculation)

\displaystyle\begin{split}{\bf\Gamma}(\mu)&=\sum_{i,j}{\gamma}_{\text{cusp}}\log\frac{\mu^{2}}{-{\rm sign}_{ij}2p_{i}\cdot p_{j}}+\sum_{i}{\gamma}_{i}\\
&-\sum_{I,J}{\gamma}_{\text{cusp}}(\chi_{IJ})+\sum_{I}{\gamma}_{I}\\
&+\sum_{I,j}{\gamma}_{\text{cusp}}\log\frac{m_{I}\mu}{-{\rm sign}_{Ij}2p_{I}\cdot p_{j}}\,.\end{split}

(2.37)

We use capital letters
I
to indicate massive particles and
lower-case letters for massless particles. The signs in front of the
scalar product depend on the types of spinors
involved
[
63
]
. To be precise,
{\rm sign}_{ij}=(-1)^{n_{ij}+1}
, where
n_{ij}
is the number of incoming particles
or outgoing antiparticles among the particles
i
and
j
.

In a theory without massive particles, the first line of
(
2.37
) describes the anomalous dimension of any number of
particles with the sum going over all possible unordered pairs as
conjectured by
[
63
]
.

The angle
\chi_{IJ}
of the fully massive case is sometimes called

\displaystyle\chi_{IJ}={\rm arcosh}\frac{-{\rm sign}_{IJ}p_{I}\cdot p_{J}}{m_{I}m_{J}}\,.

(2.38)

A comprehensive list of the anomalous dimensions required at the
two-loop level can be found in Appendix
B.2
.

The procedure to cross-check
poles is now:

1.

Calculate the
-renormalised matrix element.

2.

In a theory with massive flavours, perform a
relating
parameters, in which heavy
fermions have been integrated out, and fields such as

\alpha_{\text{SCET}}
to those of the full
theory
[
72
]

\displaystyle\alpha_{\text{full}}=\zeta_{\alpha}\times\alpha_{\text{SCET}}\,,

(2.39)

where
\zeta_{\alpha}
is given in Appendix
B.2
.
In a theory without massive flavours there is no need for
decoupling.

3.

Calculate the anomalous dimension
{\bf\Gamma}
for the
process under consideration.

4.

Use (
2.36
) to calculate
{\bf Z}
and use (
2.29
)
to check whether
\mathcal{M}_{\text{sub}}
is finite.

We will see an example of this in Chapter
3
.

Even though the above discussion holds in
, there is a much
simpler way to predict
singularities in massive
.
This is done by noting that soft singularities exponentiate. This
means that
\log{\bf Z}
vanishes at all orders, except the first.

This can be re-formulated to all orders as

\displaystyle\sum_{\ell=0}^{\infty}\mathcal{M}^{(\ell)}=e^{-\alpha S}\times\text{finite}\,.

(2.40)

This was shown by Yennie, Frautschi, and Suura
(
)
[
73
]
. Only the pole of
S
is fixed by
this equation; its finite and
\mathcal{O}(\epsilon)
contribution can
be chosen at will. In Section
4.1
we will find a
particularly helpful choice of
S
.

Chapter 3
Regularisation schemes

As mentioned before, loop integrals are usually divergent and require
regularisation. The most common way to achieve this is to formally
shift the space-time dimension
[
74
,
75
,
76
,
77
,
78
]
(dimensional
regularisation,
) to

\displaystyle d=4-2\epsilon\,.

(3.1)

Correspondingly, we change the loop integration to
5
5

5

In many of
the original references,
\hat{k}
,
\tilde{k}
,
\bar{k}
etc. were used
with different meanings depending on paper, scheme, and context. We
avoid that by instead using the notation developed
in
[
2
]
.

\displaystyle\int\frac{\mathrm{d}^{4}k}{(2\pi)^{4}}\to\mu^{4-d}\int\frac{\mathrm{d}^{d}k_{[d]}}{(2\pi)^{d}}\equiv\int[\mathrm{d}k]\,,

(3.2)

where we have defined a convenient integral measure
[\mathrm{d}k]
(cf.
(
A.1
)). We use
k_{[\rm dim]}
to indicate a vector of
(quasi-)dimension
\rm dim
. We will specify what precisely is meant
by this in Section
3.1
.

and
singularities now manifest as poles of the form

1/\epsilon^{n}
.
is indeed a consistent prescription and
the resulting integrals still fulfil properties like linearity and
invariance under shifts
[
79
,
80
]
.

Note that (
3.2
) only specifies the dimensionality of the
integration momentum
k
. The dimensionality of other objects such as

\gamma
matrices are not yet constrained. In order to systematically
classify different approaches, one has to consider two questions

•

are all parts of a diagram regularised or only those leading to
divergences?

•

are algebraic objects like metric tensors or
\gamma
matrices
regularised in
d
dimensions or in a different dimensionality?

In Section
3.1
we will introduce a unified framework for
the discussion of (dimensional)
regularisation schemes
(
).
Using this, we will briefly discuss
\gamma_{5}
in
in
Section
3.2
. In Section
3.3
we will use our
unified framework to discuss the muon decay in the common schemes

[
75
]
,

[
80
]
, and

[
81
,
82
]
. In particular, in
Section
3.3.5
we will provide a practitioner’s guide to a
particularly simple formulation of
, the

scheme
[
83
]
by once again calculating the muon decay.
Finally, we will discuss how we can use IR prediction (cf.
Section
2.4
) to predict the regularisation scheme
dependence, both generally and on the example of the muon decay.

3.1
Formal aspects

To study the questions asked above and to elegantly unify all common
variations of
, we need to introduce a series of vector
spaces
[
79
,
80
,
84
]
: the
strictly four-dimensional Minkowski space
{\rm S}_{[4]}
as well as the
infinite-dimensional spaces
{\rm QS}_{[d_{s}]}
,
{\rm QS}_{[d]}
, and
{\rm QS}_{[n_{\epsilon}]}
. The
infinite-dimensional spaces are equipped with the correct
quasi-dimensionality, s.t. the metric tensor for each space fulfils

\displaystyle\big(g_{[{\rm dim}]}^{\mu\nu}\big)^{2}={\rm dim}\,.

(3.3)

Most aspects of
can be understood from
the hierarchy between these spaces

\displaystyle{\rm S}_{[4]}\subset{\rm QS}_{[d]}\quad\text{and}\quad{\rm QS}_{[d_{s}]}={\rm QS}_{[d]}\oplus{\rm QS}_{[n_{\epsilon}]}\,.

(3.4)

The space
{\rm QS}_{[d]}
is the space in which
k_{[d]}
exist. It is

enlarged
to the bigger space
{\rm QS}_{[d_{s}]}
by the orthogonal sum with

{\rm QS}_{[n_{\epsilon}]}
. The dimensionality
d_{s}
is

\displaystyle d_{s}=d+n_{\epsilon}=4-2\epsilon+n_{\epsilon}\,.

(3.5)

Note that for many actual calculations we will be setting

d_{s}=4
and
n_{\epsilon}=2\epsilon
. For now, however, we will keep all
values independent.

Using (
3.4
) we can now construct all necessary

d_{s}
-dimensional objects

\displaystyle g_{[d_{s}]}^{\mu\nu}=g_{[d]}^{\mu\nu}+g_{[n_{\epsilon}]}^{\mu\nu}\quad\text{and}\quad\gamma_{[d_{s}]}^{\mu}=\gamma_{[d]}^{\mu}+\gamma_{[n_{\epsilon}]}^{\mu}\,.

(3.6)

Of course these objects have no finite-dimensional representation. To
practically work with them, we rely on their algebraic properties

\displaystyle(g_{[d]}g_{[n_{\epsilon}]})^{\mu\nu}=0\,,\quad\{\gamma_{[\rm dim]}^{\mu},\gamma_{[\rm dim]}^{\nu}\}=2g_{[\rm dim]}^{\mu\nu}\,,\quad\{\gamma_{[d]}^{\mu},\gamma_{[n_{\epsilon}]}^{\nu}\}=0\,.

(3.7)

Finally, we need to distinguish between two types of vector
fields
6
6

6

Again, we refer to the notation of
[
2
]
instead of using the old names internal and
external, respectively.
:

•

Fields associated with particles in one-particle irreducible
(
) diagrams or with soft and collinear radiation are
called
,

•

all other fields are
.

In general, there is no need to regularise regular fields so that
there is some freedom regarding their treatment. We can now identify
the four flavours of
through their answers
to the questions considered above, i.e. what particles are treated in
which of the three spaces.

Singular fields

{\rm QS}_{[d]}

{\rm QS}_{[d]}

{\rm QS}_{[d_{s}]}

{\rm QS}_{[d_{s}]}

Regular fields

{\rm QS}_{[d]}

{\rm S}_{[4]}

{\rm S}_{[4]}

{\rm QS}_{[d_{s}]}

‘dim. reg.’

‘dim. red.’

and
belong to a class of schemes that used to be referred
to as ‘dimensional regularisation’ while
and
belong to
what was called ‘dimensional reduction’. We will not be using these
terms further to avoid confusion and refer to all four schemes as

.

This seems to suggest that
is ideal for the calculation of
multi-loop contributions because all quantities are either strictly or
quasi four-dimensional, keeping the algebra simple without introducing
too many new problems (cf. Section
3.3.4
). Similarly,

is ideal for any type of real corrections because it does not
distinguish between singular and regular fields while still minimising
the nightmare that are the
\epsilon/\epsilon

contributions
[
85
]
.

3.2
\gamma_{5}
in dimensional schemes

In
{\rm S}_{[4]}
,
\gamma_{5}
is defined through two equivalent relations

\displaystyle\{\gamma_{[4]}^{\mu},\gamma_{5}\}=0\quad\text{or}\quad\mathrm{tr}\big(\underbrace{\gamma_{[4]}^{\mu}\gamma_{[4]}^{\nu}\gamma_{[4]}^{\rho}\gamma_{[4]}^{\sigma}}_{\Gamma_{[4]}}\gamma_{5}\big)=4\mathrm{i}\varepsilon_{[4]}^{\mu\nu\rho\sigma}\equiv 4\mathrm{i}\varepsilon^{\mu\nu\rho\sigma}\,,

(3.8)

where we have defined

\displaystyle\Gamma_{[{\rm dim}]}=\gamma_{[{\rm dim}]}^{\mu}\gamma_{[{\rm dim}]}^{\nu}\gamma_{[{\rm dim}]}^{\rho}\gamma_{[{\rm dim}]}^{\sigma}

(3.9)

in an arbitrary dimension
\rm dim
. However, these definitions are
equivalent only on
{\rm S}_{[4]}
. In any of the other spaces, they are
mutually exclusive if we want to keep the cyclicity of traces. The
proof of this is simple but lengthy
[
86
]
but
results in

\displaystyle 2\Big(\big(g_{[d]}^{\alpha\beta}\big)^{2}-4\Big)\,\mathrm{tr}\big(\Gamma_{[d]}\gamma_{5}\big)+\mathrm{tr}\big(\Gamma_{[d]}\,\gamma_{[d]}^{\alpha}\{\gamma_{5},\gamma_{[d]}^{\alpha}\}\big)=0\,,

(3.10)

which is only valid if
d=\big(g_{[d]}^{\alpha\beta}\big)^{2}=4
. A
similar proof can be found in
[
87
]
, resulting in

\displaystyle 0=(d-4)(d-3)^{2}(d-2)^{2}(d-1)^{2}d\,.

(3.11)

This suggests that the two definitions are only equivalent in for
integer
d
.

There are two commonly used solutions to this problem that change the
definition of
\gamma_{5}
that maintain cyclic traces

•

\gamma_{5}
is constructed to fulfil the trace relation as done in
the original
scheme
[
75
]
and later picked up
by Breitenlohner and Maison (
)
[
88
]

\displaystyle\gamma_{5}^{\textsc{bm}}=\frac{\mathrm{i}}{4!}\Big(\varepsilon^{\mu\nu\rho\sigma}\Gamma\Big)_{[4]}=\frac{\mathrm{i}}{4!}\varepsilon_{[4]}^{\mu\nu\rho\sigma}\Gamma_{[d]}\,.

(3.12)

This way, we still have the
\gamma
-algebra in
{\rm QS}_{[d]}
but also
generate many more
\gamma
-matrices, complicating traces.

In this scheme, we find for
and

\displaystyle\{\gamma_{5}^{\textsc{bm}},\gamma_{[4]}^{\mu}\}=0\quad\text{and}\quad[\gamma_{5}^{\textsc{bm}},\gamma_{[n_{\epsilon}]}^{\mu}]=[\gamma_{5}^{\textsc{bm}},\gamma_{[-2\epsilon]}^{\mu}]=0\,.

(3.13)

This also implies that
[
88
]

\displaystyle\{\gamma_{5}^{\textsc{bm}},\gamma_{[d]}^{\mu}\}=2\gamma_{[-2\epsilon]}^{\mu}\gamma_{5}^{\textsc{bm}}

(3.14)

and similarly for
\{\gamma_{5}^{\textsc{bm}},\gamma_{[d_{s}]}^{\mu}\}
.

Unfortunately, in combination with

breaks the chiral
symmetry because for
P_{L,R}=\tfrac{1}{2}(1\pm\gamma_{5})
to be a
chiral projectors for both
\psi
and
\bar{\psi}
,

\{\gamma^{0},\gamma^{5}\}=0
is required. This no longer naively
works meaning that chiral symmetry is broken. Hence, we require
another finite renormalisation
[
89
,
90
]

\displaystyle\gamma_{5}^{\textsc{bm}}\to Z_{5}\gamma_{5}^{\textsc{bm}}\quad\text{with}\quad Z_{5}=1+\frac{\alpha}{4\pi}\Big(\frac{n_{\epsilon}}{\epsilon}-4\Big)\,.

(3.15)

We will later see an explicit example of this.

•

Alternatively we can define
\gamma_{5}
algebraically s.t. the
anti-commutator vanishes
[
90
,
91
,
92
]

\displaystyle\{\gamma_{5}^{\textsc{ac}},\gamma_{[d]}^{\mu}\}=\{\gamma_{5}^{\textsc{ac}},\gamma_{[n_{\epsilon}]}^{\mu}\}=\{\gamma_{5}^{\textsc{ac}},\gamma_{[d_{s}]}^{\mu}\}=0\,.

(3.16)

This scheme is workable but not in the strict sense consistent as
it fails to reproduce the Adler-Bell-Jackiw or triangle
anomaly
[
93
,
94
,
95
]
. Despite
this, it was proposed by
[
86
]
that we can use

if we restore the anomaly by hand afterwards wherever
necessary (though it often is not). One way to make
ac

consistent is by giving up the cyclicity of the
trace
[
91
,
92
]
.

Both methods, if used properly, lead to consistent results. A more
complete review of
\gamma_{5}
in
and
can be found
in
[
90
]
.

3.3
The muon decay in all schemes

\mu^{-}
e^{-}

p

(p-k)

(q-k)

q

k

Figure 3.1:
The Feynman diagram contributing to the muon decay at the
one-loop level with momentum routing. The dimensionality of the
momenta will depend on the scheme used.

As an illustration of the various aspects of the different schemes, we
will calculate the muon decay

\displaystyle\mu(p)\to\nu(q_{3})\bar{\nu}(q_{4})e(q)\,,

(3.17)

with
p^{2}=M^{2}\equiv m_{\mu}^{2}
and
q^{2}=0
in the three schemes
,

, and
. In particular, we will set the electron mass to
zero in this section because it will result in a more interesting
singularity structure – helping us to understand the different
schemes better. Note that we keep all mass-effects in the
phenomenological discussion later.

To simplify this discussion, we will perform the computation in the
Fierz rearranged effective theory of the muon
decay (
2.24
). As we have already discussed in
Section
2.2
,
\mathcal{L}
is invariant under the exchange

\psi_{e}\to\gamma^{5}\psi_{e}
(and
m_{e}\to-m_{e}
had we not assumed

m_{e}=0
)
[
59
]
, allowing us to relate the axial-vector
current to the vector current. Hence, we can calculate the matrix
elements without needing to worry about
\gamma_{5}
.

When considering this process, we need to compute one diagram at tree
level and one diagram at one-loop, the latter is shown in
Figure
3.1
. As aids, we will be using the
Mathematica programs
TRACER

[
96
]
for the Dirac
algebra and Package-X
[
97
]
for the one-loop calculus.

All renormalisation constants necessary for this calculation can be
found in Appendix
B.1
.

3.3.1
Neutrino average

As a first step that is universal to all schemes, we will deal with
the neutrinos, realising that we cannot actually measure them. Hence,
we would like to remove them as much from the calculation as possible.
To do this we note that when we calculate any observable
using (
2.24
), a term corresponding to the neutrino current

\displaystyle\mathcal{N}^{\mu}=\bar{u}(q_{3})\gamma^{\mu}u(q_{4})

(3.18)

will be present in the amplitudes. We can factor out the neutrino
tensor
\mathcal{N}^{\mu}\mathcal{N}^{*\nu}
that appears in the
squared amplitude by averaging over all possible neutrino momenta. To
do this, we note that

\displaystyle\mathcal{N}^{\mu}\mathcal{N}^{*\nu}=4q_{3}^{\mu}q_{4}^{\nu}+4q_{3}^{\nu}q_{4}^{\mu}+4q_{3}\cdot q_{4}g^{\mu\nu}\,,

(3.19)

where
q_{3}
and
q_{4}
are the momenta of the neutrinos. Here and
henceforth, the sum over spin states is implicit.
Had we not removed the
\gamma_{5}
earlier, there would also be
anti-symmetric terms that would not change the discussion below. We
now define the average of an arbitrary function
f(q_{3},q_{4})
as the
normalised
1\to 2
phase space integration

\displaystyle\langle f(q_{3},q_{4})\rangle=\frac{\int\mathrm{d}\Phi\ f(q_{3},q_{4})}{\int\mathrm{d}\Phi\ 1}=8\pi{\int\mathrm{d}\Phi\ f(q_{3},q_{4})}\,.

(3.20)

However, it turns out that, as long as
\langle 1\rangle=1
, it does
not matter how the phase space is defined as long as it is Lorentz
invariant and integrates over the neutrinos.

When we average over
q_{3}
and
q_{4}
, the result can only depend on

Q=q_{3}+q_{4}
. Hence, the most general ansatz for
\langle q_{3}^{\mu}q_{4}^{\nu}\rangle
is

\displaystyle\langle q_{3}^{\mu}q_{4}^{\nu}\rangle=A\frac{Q^{\mu}Q^{\nu}}{Q^{2}}+Bg^{\mu\nu}\,.

(3.21)

By applying the projectors
g^{\mu\nu}
and
Q^{\mu}Q^{\nu}
we find

\displaystyle\begin{split}g^{\mu\nu}\langle q_{3}^{\mu}q_{4}^{\nu}\rangle&=\langle q_{3}\cdot q_{4}\rangle=A+B\,d\\
Q^{\mu}Q^{\nu}\langle q_{3}^{\mu}q_{4}^{\nu}\rangle&=\langle Q\cdot q_{3}\,Q\cdot q_{4}\rangle=Q^{2}(A+B)\,.\end{split}

(3.22)

Using that
q_{3}^{2}=q_{4}^{2}=0
, i.e.
Q^{2}=2q_{3}\cdot q_{4}
, we can re-write
these equations

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}Q^{2}\langle 1\rangle=A+B\,d\quad\text{and}\quad\frac{1}{4}\big(Q^{2}\big)^{2}\langle 1\rangle=Q^{2}(A+B)\,,

(3.23)

allowing us to determine
A
and
B

\displaystyle A=\frac{d-2}{d-1}\frac{Q^{2}}{4}\langle 1\rangle\quad\text{and}\quad B=\frac{1}{d-1}\frac{Q^{2}}{4}\langle 1\rangle\,.

(3.24)

And hence with
\langle 1\rangle=1

\displaystyle\mathcal{N}^{\mu\nu}=\langle\mathcal{N}^{\mu}\mathcal{N}^{*\nu}\rangle=2\frac{d-2}{d-1}Q^{2}\bigg(\frac{Q^{\mu}Q^{\nu}}{Q^{2}}-g^{\mu\nu}\bigg)\,.

(3.25)

Note that the neutrino tensor
\mathcal{N}^{\mu\nu}
will be the same
in all parts of the calculation (both real and virtual) as a global
pre-factor. While the dimensionality of
\mathcal{N}
certainly
influences intermediary results, any physical quantity must be
independent of its dimensionality as it will only influence terms

\mathcal{O}(\epsilon)
that vanish in the limit
\epsilon\to 0
. This
means that we could choose its dimensionality independently of the
scheme under consideration.

3.3.2
Conventional dimensional regularisation (
cdr
)

In
all quantities are considered
d
-dimensional, even the
external momenta. However, because
{\rm S}_{[4]}\subset{\rm QS}_{[d]}
the dimension of
the external fermion momenta does not matter and they could in
principle be chosen from either space. Nevertheless, for consistency
we will still keep them in the space they would be in if they were
internal momenta.

The tree-level amplitude is

\displaystyle\mathcal{A}_{\textsc{cdr}}^{(0)}=\frac{1}{2}\times\frac{4G_{F}}{\sqrt{2}}\Big(-\bar{u}(q_{[d]})\gamma_{[d]}^{\mu}u(p_{[d]})\ \mathcal{N}_{[d]}^{\mu}\Big)\,.

(3.26)

Here, the factor
1/2
arises from the projector
P_{L}=(1-\gamma^{5})/2
.
The matrix element
7
7

7

By ‘matrix element’ we denote the result
of squaring the amplitude. In particular, we refrain from calling it
the matrix element squared
is

\displaystyle\begin{split}\mathcal{M}^{(0)}_{\textsc{cdr}}&=\frac{1}{2}\Big|\mathcal{A}_{\textsc{cdr}}^{(0)}\Big|^{2}=\frac{1}{2}\times\frac{4G_{F}^{2}}{2}\mathrm{tr}\Big[\not{q}_{[d]}\ \gamma_{[d]}^{\mu}\ \big(\not{p}_{[d]}+M\big)\ \gamma_{[d]}^{\nu}\Big]\times\mathcal{N}_{[d]}^{\mu\nu}\\
&=\frac{8}{3}G_{F}^{2}M^{4}x(3+2x(\epsilon-1)-2\epsilon)\,,\end{split}

(3.27)

with
x=2p\cdot q/M^{2}
the dimensionless quantity describing the
process. To obtain this result we have used standard
d
-dimensional
trace techniques as implemented in
TRACER
.

At the one-loop level, we have to calculate

\displaystyle\begin{split}\mathcal{M}^{(1)}_{\textsc{cdr}}\Big|_{\text{bare}}&=\frac{1}{2}\times 2\Re\big(\mathcal{A}_{\textsc{cdr}}^{(1)}\times\mathcal{A}_{\textsc{cdr}}^{(0)}\big)\\
&=\frac{\alpha_{0}G_{F}^{2}}{\pi}\int[\mathrm{d}k]\frac{\mathrm{tr}\Big[\not{q}_{[d]}\,\,\gamma_{[d]}^{\sigma}\big(\not{k}_{[d]}-\not{q}_{[d]}\big)\gamma_{[d]}^{\mu}\big(\not{k}_{[d]}-\not{p}_{[d]}-M\big)\gamma_{[d]}^{\sigma}\,\,\big(\not{p}_{[d]}+M\big)\,\,\gamma_{[d]}^{\nu}\Big]}{\mathcal{D}_{1}\mathcal{D}_{2}\mathcal{D}_{3}}\mathcal{N}_{[d]}^{\mu\nu}\,,\end{split}

(3.28)

with

\displaystyle\mathcal{D}_{1}=k_{[d]}^{2}\,,\quad\mathcal{D}_{2}=(k_{[d]}+p_{[d]})^{2}-M^{2}\,,\quad\mathcal{D}_{3}=(k_{[d]}-q_{[d]})^{2}\,.

(3.29)

(
3.28
) can be evaluated using standard techniques,
obtaining the unrenormalised
result

\displaystyle\begin{split}\mathcal{M}^{(1)}_{\textsc{cdr}}\Big|_{\text{bare}}=\frac{\alpha_{0}}{\pi}\bigg(\frac{\mu^{2}}{M^{2}x^{2}}\bigg)^{\epsilon}\mathcal{M}^{(0)}_{\textsc{cdr}}\Bigg(&-\frac{1}{2\epsilon^{2}}-\frac{1}{2\epsilon}-2-\frac{3}{2}\zeta_{2}+\frac{x}{2x-3}\log(x)\\
&\qquad+\log(1-x)\log(x)+{\rm Li}_{2}(x)\Bigg)\,,\end{split}

(3.30)

with the dilogarithm
{\rm Li}_{2}(x)
, the first polylogarithm with
order
o=2
, and
\zeta(2)=\pi^{2}/6
the Riemann
\zeta
-function.
This and many more expressions we will encounter can be compactly
written by using so-called
harmonic polylogarithms
(HPL),
introduced in
[
98
]
and implemented for Mathematica
in
[
99
]
. These functions extend the notion of
polylogarithms by generalising the order
o
to a weight vector
\vec{w}
. In particular, introducing
\vec{w}=\{0\}
and
\vec{w}=\{1,0\}

\displaystyle H_{0}(x)=\log(x)\quad\text{and}\quad H_{1,0}(x)=-\log(1-x)\log(x)-{\rm Li}_{2}(x)\,,

(3.31)

we find

\displaystyle\mathcal{M}^{(1)}_{\textsc{cdr}}\Big|_{\text{bare}}=\frac{\alpha_{0}}{\pi}\bigg(\frac{\mu^{2}}{M^{2}x^{2}}\bigg)^{\epsilon}\mathcal{M}^{(0)}_{\textsc{cdr}}\Bigg(-\frac{1}{2\epsilon^{2}}-\frac{1}{2\epsilon}-2-\frac{3}{2}\zeta_{2}+\frac{x}{2x-3}H_{0}(x)-H_{1,0}(x)\Bigg)\,,

(3.32)

We now need to renormalise this quantity. No mass renormalisation is
necessary because
\mathcal{A}^{(0)}
does not contain
M
. Masses
from the spin-sum are taken care through the renormalisation of the
wave function in the
scheme as mandated by the

formula. For the muon, this means we have to multiply with (in
Feynman gauge)

\displaystyle\sqrt{Z_{2,\textsc{cdr}}^{\mu}}=1+\frac{\alpha_{0}}{2\pi}\bigg(-\frac{3}{2\epsilon}+\frac{3}{2}\log\frac{M^{2}}{\mu^{2}}-\frac{4}{2}\bigg)\,.

(3.33)

Technically, the
\alpha_{0}
here is unrenormalised. However, at the
current loop order there is no difference because

\alpha=\alpha_{0}+\mathcal{O}(\alpha^{2})
. The corresponding factor

Z_{2}^{e}=1+\mathcal{O}(\alpha^{2})
because we treat the electron
massless. Hence, we have

\displaystyle\mathcal{M}^{(1)}_{\textsc{cdr}}=\frac{\alpha}{\pi}\bigg(\frac{\mu^{2}}{M^{2}x^{2}}\bigg)^{\epsilon}\mathcal{M}^{(0)}_{\textsc{cdr}}\Bigg(-\frac{1}{2\epsilon^{2}}-\frac{5}{4\epsilon}-3-\frac{3}{2}\zeta_{2}-\frac{9-4x}{6-4x}H_{0}(x)-H_{1,0}(x)\Bigg)\,.

(3.34)

In particular we do not renormalise the electromagnetic coupling

\alpha
because no
vertex is present at
(cf.
Section
2.1.2
).

3.3.3
The original scheme (
hv
)

In
, we treat the regular fields four dimensionally. This means
that at tree level we do a strictly four-dimensional calculation

\displaystyle\begin{split}\mathcal{M}^{(0)}_{\textsc{hv}}&=\frac{1}{2}\times\frac{4G_{F}^{2}}{2}\mathrm{tr}\Big[\not{q}_{[4]}\ \gamma_{[4]}^{\mu}\ \big(\not{p}_{[4]}+M\big)\ \gamma_{[4]}^{\nu}\Big]\times\mathcal{N}_{[4]}^{\mu\nu}=\frac{8G_{F}^{2}}{3}M^{4}x(3-2x)\\
&=\mathcal{M}^{(0)}_{\textsc{cdr}}\Big|_{d=4}\,.\end{split}

(3.35)

At one-loop we have to be more careful as we have objects of
different dimensions in one trace

\displaystyle\mathcal{M}^{(1)}_{\textsc{hv}}\Big|_{\text{bare}}=\frac{\alpha_{0}G_{F}^{2}}{\pi}\int[\mathrm{d}k]\frac{\mathrm{tr}\Big[\not{q}_{[4]}\,\,\gamma_{[d]}^{\sigma}\big(\not{k}_{[d]}-\not{q}_{[d]}\big)\gamma_{[4]}^{\mu}\big(\not{k}_{[d]}-\not{p}_{[d]}-M\big)\gamma_{[d]}^{\sigma}\,\,\big(\not{p}_{[4]}+M\big)\,\,\gamma_{[4]}^{\nu}\Big]}{\mathcal{D}_{1}\mathcal{D}_{2}\mathcal{D}_{3}}\mathcal{N}_{[4]}^{\mu\nu}\,.

(3.36)

Note that the only meaningful difference to the
discussion is
the dimensionality of the
\gamma^{\mu}
and
\gamma^{\sigma}
. When
calculating in
, we need to utilise that
{\rm S}_{[4]}\subset{\rm QS}_{[d]}
. This
is a rather powerful statement because it allows us to calculate the
product of two vectors in different spaces as

\displaystyle a_{[d]}\cdot b_{[4]}=a_{[4]}\cdot b_{[4]}\,.

(3.37)

This relation goes both ways. After using standard trace techniques
(taking care of the dimensionality of each
\gamma
matrix) we can use
it the other way around to write the numerator again in terms of the
familiar
k_{[d]}\cdot p_{[d]}
,
k_{[d]}\cdot q_{[d]}
, and
k_{[d]}\cdot k_{[d]}
. However, we also have a new type numerator with
k_{[4]}\cdot k_{[4]}

from

\displaystyle\mathrm{tr}\Big[k_{[d]}\,\,\gamma_{[4]}^{\mu}\,\,k_{[d]}\,\,\gamma_{[4]}^{\mu}\Big]=8k_{[4]}\cdot k_{[4]}-16k_{[d]}\cdot k_{[d]}\,.

(3.38)

Using these relations, we find

\displaystyle\begin{split}\mathcal{M}^{(1)}_{\textsc{hv}}\Big|_{\text{bare}}=\frac{\alpha_{0}}{\pi}\frac{8G_{F}^{2}}{3}\int[\mathrm{d}k]\ &\frac{1}{\mathcal{D}_{1}\mathcal{D}_{2}\mathcal{D}_{3}}\bigg(2(3M^{2}-2s)s^{2}\\
&+4(3M^{4}-4M^{2}s+2s^{2})(k_{[d]}\cdot q_{[d]})-4(3M^{2}-2s)s(k_{[d]}\cdot p_{[d]})\\
&+4(d-2)M^{2}(k_{[d]}\cdot q_{[d]})^{2}+4(d-2)(M^{2}-2s)(k_{[d]}\cdot p_{[d]})(k_{[d]}\cdot q_{[d]})\\
&-(d-2)(2s-3M^{2})s(k_{[d]}\cdot k_{[d]})-2(M^{2}-s)s(d-2)(k_{[4]}\cdot k_{[4]})\bigg)\,.\end{split}

(3.39)

With the
(k_{[4]}\cdot k_{[4]})
in the numerator, we have in principle a
new class of integrals to discuss. These will be related to the

\mu
-integrals of Section
3.3.5
. However, for now we can just
solve these integrals using Passarino-Veltman
decomposition
[
100
]
(for a didactic introduction
cf.
[
101
]
)

\displaystyle\int[\mathrm{d}k]\frac{k_{[4]}\cdot k_{[4]}}{\mathcal{D}_{1}\mathcal{D}_{2}\mathcal{D}_{3}}=g_{[4]}^{\mu\nu}\int[\mathrm{d}k]\frac{k_{[d]}^{\mu}k_{[d]}^{\nu}}{\mathcal{D}_{1}\mathcal{D}_{2}\mathcal{D}_{3}}\,,

(3.40)

because
{\rm S}_{[4]}\subset{\rm QS}_{[d]}
.

Solving the loop integral and renormalising with
Z_{2,\textsc{hv}}^{\mu}=Z_{2,\textsc{cdr}}^{\mu}
we find a familiar result

\displaystyle\mathcal{M}^{(1)}_{\textsc{hv}}=\frac{\alpha}{\pi}\bigg(\frac{\mu^{2}}{M^{2}x^{2}}\bigg)^{\epsilon}\mathcal{M}^{(0)}_{\textsc{hv}}\Bigg(-\frac{1}{2\epsilon^{2}}-\frac{5}{4\epsilon}-3-\frac{3}{2}\zeta_{2}-\frac{9-4x}{6-4x}H_{0}(x)-H_{1,0}(x)\Bigg)\,.

(3.41)

Note that here we pulled out a factor
\mathcal{M}^{(0)}_{\textsc{hv}}
instead of

\mathcal{M}^{(0)}_{\textsc{cdr}}
. This makes the fully expanded result simpler in
comparison with the fully expanded result of
\mathcal{M}^{(1)}_{\textsc{cdr}}
as there are
no terms
\epsilon/\epsilon
from the poles with the linear parts of
the tree level result. We refer to this as
because nothing relevant has changed. This is similar to
the scheme dependence due to the neutrino tensor. The
\mathcal{M}^{(0)}_{\textsc{rs}}
term
will appear in all parts of the calculations, i.e. both real and
virtual. Intermediary results will have to be different due to the
trivial scheme dependence but any physical, i.e. finite, result will
be independent because
\mathcal{M}^{(0)}_{\textsc{rs}}
acts as a pre-factor.

3.3.4
The four-dimensional helicity scheme (
fdh
)

The goal of
is to treat as many objects in
d_{s}\equiv 4

dimensions as possible. While this simplifies things a lot, ‘there
ain’t no such thing as a free lunch’. This popular saying manifests
itself in the existence of so-called
\epsilon
-scalars.

We treat singular vector fields in
{\rm QS}_{[d_{s}]}={\rm QS}_{[d]}\oplus{\rm QS}_{[n_{\epsilon}]}
which means
that we have to write the covariant derivative as

\displaystyle D^{\mu}_{[d_{s}]}\psi_{0}=\partial_{[d]}^{\mu}\psi_{0}+\mathrm{i}\big(e_{0}(A_{0})^{\mu}_{[d]}+e_{e,0}(A_{0})^{\mu}_{[n_{\epsilon}]}\big)\psi_{0}\,,

(3.42)

with a bare
\epsilon
-scalar
(A_{0})^{\mu}_{[n_{\epsilon}]}
with an evanescent
coupling
e_{e,0}
to fermions. This split, that spoils
’s
simplicity, is necessary as
e_{e,0}
is not protected by the

d
-dimensional gauge symmetry and is renormalised differently. In

, the corresponding
\beta
-functions
are
[
102
]
(cf. Appendix
B.1
)

\displaystyle\begin{split}\beta&=-\alpha\Bigg(\Big(\frac{\alpha}{4\pi}\Big)\underbrace{\Big[-\frac{4}{3}N_{F}\Big]}_{\beta_{0}\equiv\beta_{20}}+\mathcal{O}(\alpha^{2})\Bigg)\,,\\
\beta_{e}&=-\alpha_{e}\Bigg(\Big(\frac{\alpha_{e}}{4\pi}\Big)\underbrace{\Big[-4-2N_{F}\Big]}_{\beta_{02}}+\Big(\frac{\alpha}{4\pi}\Big)\underbrace{\Big[+6\Big]}_{\beta_{11}}+\mathcal{O}(\alpha_{i}^{2})\Bigg)\,.\end{split}

(3.43)

Therefore, one would have to perform any
\ell
-loop calculation with
both
\epsilon
-scalars and normal gauge bosons, keeping the couplings

e
and
e_{e}
different. After renormalisation one can safely set

e_{e}\to e
. This increases the number of diagrams by

\mathcal{O}(2^{\ell})
.

Fortunately, there is a silver lining: because the effect of

\epsilon
-scalars is limited the their coupling’s renormalisation,
there is actually no need to use (
3.42
) at the
\ell
-loop
level. This gives us the following prescription for an
\ell
-loop
calculation

•

Use (
3.42
) for anything up to the
(\ell-1)
-level and
renormalise correctly.

•

At the
\ell
-loop level, perform the calculation using only
quasi-four-dimensional objects.

•

Add everything up and set
e_{e}\to e
and
n_{\epsilon}\to 2\epsilon
. If
necessary, convert to a different renormalisation scheme for the
coupling now.

Especially for one-loop
\epsilon
-scalars are not needed at all
because the renormalisation could only influence tree level

\epsilon
-scalars that do not exist because there are no singular
fields at leading order. Hence, one-loop calculations can be performed
without worrying about
\epsilon
-scalars (for a particular efficient
way to exploit this, cf. Section
3.3.5
). This would still work
in QCD when including
s
[
82
]
.

At tree level in
, we obtain the same result as in
because
both schemes treat regular fields in
{\rm S}_{[4]}
. For illustration, we will
calculate the one-loop
result twice: once carefully
differentiating
\epsilon
-scalar contributions with

\epsilon
-scalars and once ignoring
\epsilon
-scalars at one-loop,
while instead working in
d_{s}\equiv 4
dimensions.

Calculation with
\epsilon
-scalars

\mu^{-}
e^{-}

p

(p-k)

(q-k)

q

k

(a)
\mathcal{M}^{(1)}_{\textsc{fdh},\gamma}

\mu^{-}
e^{-}

p

(p-k)

(q-k)

q

k

(b)
\mathcal{M}^{(1)}_{\textsc{fdh},\epsilon}

Figure 3.2:
The two diagrams in full
. The first diagram is
identical to Figure
3.1
.

We have to calculate the two diagrams of Figure
3.2
: one
diagram with a virtual photon, Figure
3.2a
,

\displaystyle\mathcal{M}^{(1)}_{\textsc{fdh},\gamma}=\frac{\alpha_{0}G_{F}^{2}}{\pi}\int[\mathrm{d}k]\frac{\mathrm{tr}\Big[\not{q}_{[4]}\,\,\gamma_{[d_{s}]}^{\sigma}\big(\not{k}_{[d]}-\not{q}_{[d]}\big)\gamma_{[4]}^{\mu}\big(\not{k}_{[d]}-\not{p}_{[d]}-M\big)\gamma_{[d_{s}]}^{\sigma}\,\,\big(\not{p}_{[4]}+M\big)\,\,\gamma_{[4]}^{\nu}\Big]}{\mathcal{D}_{1}\mathcal{D}_{2}\mathcal{D}_{3}}\mathcal{N}_{[4]}^{\mu\nu}\,,

(3.44a)

and one with a virtual
\epsilon
-scalar,
Figure
3.2b
,

\displaystyle\mathcal{M}^{(1)}_{\textsc{fdh},\epsilon}=\frac{\alpha_{e,0}G_{F}^{2}}{\pi}\int[\mathrm{d}k]\frac{\mathrm{tr}\Big[\not{q}_{[4]}\,\,\gamma_{[n_{\epsilon}]}^{\sigma}\big(\not{k}_{[d]}-\not{q}_{[d]}\big)\gamma_{[4]}^{\mu}\big(\not{k}_{[d]}-\not{p}_{[d]}-M\big)\gamma_{[n_{\epsilon}]}^{\sigma}\,\,\big(\not{p}_{[4]}+M\big)\,\,\gamma_{[4]}^{\nu}\Big]}{\mathcal{D}_{1}\mathcal{D}_{2}\mathcal{D}_{3}}\mathcal{N}_{[4]}^{\mu\nu}\,,

(3.44b)

where
\alpha_{e,0}=e_{e,0}^{2}/(4\pi)
in accordance with the
definition of
\alpha_{0}
. Because
\not{k}_{[d]}=\gamma_{[d]}^{\mu}k_{[d]}^{\mu}=\gamma_{[d_{s}]}^{\mu}k_{[d]}^{\mu}
, we can do most of the algebra
of
\mathcal{M}^{(1)}_{\textsc{fdh},\gamma}
in
d_{s}
dimensions as long as we keep track
of the dimensionality of the
k
.

By employing the same tricks as above for the Dirac algebra, we find
without specifying
d_{s}
for the bare matrix element

\displaystyle\begin{split}\mathcal{M}^{(1)}_{\textsc{fdh},\gamma}\Big|_{\text{bare}}&=\frac{\alpha_{0}}{\pi}\bigg(\frac{\mu^{2}}{M^{2}x^{2}}\bigg)^{\epsilon}\mathcal{M}^{(0)}_{\textsc{fdh}}\Bigg(-\frac{1}{2\epsilon^{2}}-\frac{6-d_{s}}{4\epsilon}-\frac{10-d_{s}}{4}-\frac{3}{2}\zeta_{2}\\
&\qquad\qquad+\frac{(d_{s}-4)(3-x)-2x}{6-4x}H_{0}(x)-H_{1,0}(x)\Bigg)\end{split}\,,

(3.45a)

\displaystyle\mathcal{M}^{(1)}_{\textsc{fdh},\epsilon}\Big|_{\text{bare}}

\displaystyle=\frac{\alpha_{e,0}}{\pi}n_{\epsilon}\bigg(\frac{\mu^{2}}{M^{2}x^{2}}\bigg)^{\epsilon}\mathcal{M}^{(0)}_{\textsc{fdh}}\Bigg(\frac{1}{4\epsilon}+\frac{1}{4}+\frac{3-x}{6-4x}H_{0}(x)\Bigg)\,.

(3.45b)

For the renormalisation we need to consider the effect of

\epsilon
-scalars to
Z_{2}^{\mu}

\displaystyle\sqrt{Z_{2,\textsc{fdh}}^{\mu}}=1+\frac{\alpha_{0}}{2\pi}\bigg(-\frac{3}{2\epsilon}+\frac{3}{2}\log\frac{M^{2}}{\mu^{2}}-\frac{4}{2}\bigg)+\frac{\alpha_{e,0}}{2\pi}n_{\epsilon}\bigg(-\frac{1}{4\epsilon}+\frac{1}{4}\log\frac{M^{2}}{\mu^{2}}-\frac{1}{4}\bigg)\,.

(3.46)

Our renormalised
result is therefore

\displaystyle\begin{split}\mathcal{M}^{(1)}_{\textsc{fdh}}&=\mathcal{M}^{(1)}_{\textsc{fdh},\gamma}+\mathcal{M}^{(1)}_{\textsc{fdh},\epsilon}\\
&=\bigg(\frac{\mu^{2}}{M^{2}x^{2}}\bigg)^{\epsilon}\mathcal{M}^{(0)}_{\textsc{fdh}}\Bigg[\frac{\alpha}{\pi}\Bigg(-\frac{1}{2\epsilon^{2}}+\frac{d_{s}-9}{4\epsilon}-\frac{10+(4-d_{s})}{4}-\frac{3}{2}\zeta_{2}\\
&\hskip 128.0374pt+\frac{(d_{s}-4)(3-x)-9+4x}{6-4x}H_{0}(x)-H_{1,0}(x)\Bigg)\\
