&\hskip 85.35826pt+\frac{\alpha_{e}}{\pi}\frac{n_{\epsilon}}{4}\Bigg(\frac{1}{2\epsilon}+\frac{1}{2}-\frac{3}{2x-3}H_{0}(x)\Bigg)\Bigg]\,.\end{split}

(3.47)

This result is what we refer to as
. It has the
explicit dependence on the
\epsilon
-scalars so that we could – and
in Chapter
5
will – perform the two-loop calculation
in
with the correct renormalisation of
\alpha_{e}
.

Calculation without
\epsilon
-scalars

Assuming we do not actually want to perform a two-loop calculation, we
can simplify the calculation by just setting
d_{s}\equiv 4
and

n_{\epsilon}=0
in the one-loop calculation from the get-go with

\alpha_{e}=\alpha
. However, we still need to keep in mind that

{\rm S}_{[4]}\subset{\rm QS}_{[d_{s}]}\equiv{\rm QS}_{[4]}
, i.e. that
a_{[d]}\cdot b_{[d_{s}]}=a_{[d]}\cdot b_{[d]}
, allowing us to perform the algebra
d_{s}\equiv 4

dimensionally. For complicate processes this can simplify the algebra
massively as there is no need to keep track of
\epsilon
-terms
induced by the algebra. We of course lose the generality of two-loop
readiness.

Our one-loop bare result is just (
3.45a
) with
d_{s}=4
.
However, we need to keep the
n_{\epsilon}
in
Z_{2}^{\mu}
resulting in

\displaystyle\mathcal{M}^{(1)}_{\textsc{fdh}}\Big|_{\text{bare}}

\displaystyle=\frac{\alpha}{\pi}\bigg(\frac{\mu^{2}}{M^{2}x^{2}}\bigg)^{\epsilon}\mathcal{M}^{(0)}_{\textsc{fdh}}\Bigg(-\frac{1}{2\epsilon^{2}}-\frac{1}{2\epsilon}-\frac{3}{2}-\frac{3}{2}\zeta_{2}-\frac{2x}{6-4x}H_{0}(x)-H_{1,0}(x)\Bigg)\,,

(3.48a)

\displaystyle Z_{2,\textsc{fdh}}^{\mu}

\displaystyle=1+\frac{\alpha}{2\pi}\bigg(-\frac{3}{2\epsilon}+\frac{3}{2}\log\frac{M^{2}}{\mu^{2}}-\frac{5}{2}\bigg)\,.

(3.48b)

This directly results in what we find if we set
d_{s}=4-2\epsilon
and

n_{\epsilon}=2\epsilon
in (
3.47
)

\displaystyle\mathcal{M}^{(1)}_{\textsc{fdh}}=\frac{\alpha}{\pi}\bigg(\frac{\mu^{2}}{M^{2}x^{2}}\bigg)^{\epsilon}\mathcal{M}^{(0)}_{\textsc{fdh}}\Bigg(-\frac{1}{2\epsilon^{2}}-\frac{5}{4\epsilon}-\frac{11}{4}-\frac{3}{2}\zeta_{2}-\frac{9-4x}{6-4x}H_{0}(x)-H_{1,0}(x)\Bigg)\,.

(3.49)

Compare this result to the
result from (
3.41
). The
only difference is the rational number in the finite part that changes
from
-3\to-11/4
. This the first time we have encountered

(this is in contrast to the
trivial scheme dependence between
and
). As we will see
in Section
3.4
, we can predict the scheme dependence
without having to calculate the different contributions.

It is important that all non-trivial scheme dependence will cancel as
soon as
\mathcal{M}^{(1)}_{\textsc{rs}}
is combined with the real correction in the same
scheme
rs
. This is crucial as otherwise the different schemes would
not be consistent.

Renormalisation of
fdh
beyond leading order

Beyond what we have discussed in Section
2.1
, the
renormalisation in
is complicated by the presence of

\epsilon
-scalars at the one-loop level. Additionally to the issue of
the different coupling we have already discussed, there is one more
subtlety in a theory with massive flavours. In contrast to the vector
boson propagator, there is no symmetry that protects the propagator of
the
\epsilon
-scalar from acquiring a mass term
\propto m^{2}g^{\mu\nu}_{[n_{\epsilon}]}

[
103
,
104
]
. This
effectively shifts the scalar’s mass from zero, requiring an
appropriate counter term to restore a vanishing
\epsilon
-scalar
mass. Hence, we add the following steps in the discussion of
Section
2.1.2

5.

Maintain the masslessness of the
\epsilon
-scalars by
substituting

\displaystyle\frac{-\mathrm{i}g^{\mu\nu}_{[n_{\epsilon}]}}{p^{2}}\to\delta m_{\epsilon}^{(l)}\ \frac{-\mathrm{i}g^{\mu\nu}_{[n_{\epsilon}]}}{p^{2}}

(3.50)

at tree level and one-loop.

6.

Perform the coupling renormalisation by shifting
\alpha_{0,i}\to(1+\delta Z_{\alpha_{i}}^{(1)}+\delta Z_{\alpha_{i}}^{(2)})\times\alpha_{i}
and sorting
terms according to the now renormalised coupling, dropping every
term with too high a power in
\alpha_{i}
.

7.

Identify
\alpha_{e}\equiv\alpha
and set
n_{\epsilon}=2\epsilon
.

3.3.5
Four-dimensional formulation of
fdh
(
fdf
)

At the one-loop level,
is seemingly complicated by the presence
of objects with different dimensions in the traces and the need to
include
\epsilon
-scalars at tree level. There is also still the
problem of
\gamma^{5}
which we have ignored so-far. The
four-dimensional formulation of
(
) solves both problems at
one-loop
[
83
]
.

Originally, the
scheme was constructed to best use unitarity
methods for one-loop calculation. This is done by essentially
constructing one-loop amplitudes by sewing together tree-level
amplitudes, allowing for extremely efficent numerical evaluation of
one-loop amplitudes as done by e.g. GoSam
[
105
]
.
However, for this to work all momenta including the loop momentum must
be in
{\rm S}_{[4]}
. We will not be discussing these methods further as

is for us just a particularly efficient way to calculate complicated
one-loop amplitudes.

We can simplify
by realising that all our problems arise
because of the
k_{[4]}^{2}
-terms in numerators of integrals that we have
postponed in our discussion of
. To understand these terms better,
we will introduce another space
{\rm QS}_{[-2\epsilon]}

\displaystyle{\rm QS}_{[d]}={\rm S}_{[4]}\oplus{\rm QS}_{[-2\epsilon]}\,.

(3.51a)

We can now write
k_{[4]}^{2}
as

\displaystyle k_{[d]}^{2}=k_{[4]}^{2}-\mu^{2}\,,

(3.51b)

where
\mu^{2}
is the remnant of the part of the loop momentum from

{\rm QS}_{[-2\epsilon]}
. However, we can realise this already at the level of
\gamma

matrices by setting

\displaystyle\not{k}_{[d]}=\not{k}_{[4]}+\mathrm{i}\gamma^{5}\mu\,.

(Rule I)

One can easily verify that this definition
satisfies (
3.51b
). We can now perform the entire
calculation in
{\rm S}_{[4]}
, up to terms
\mu\in{\rm QS}_{[-2\epsilon]}
for which we use

\displaystyle\text{Odd powers of $\mu$ are set to zero}\,.

(Rule II)

In our case, we write (cf. (
3.36
))

\displaystyle\begin{split}\mathcal{M}^{(1)}_{\textsc{fdf}}\Big|_{\text{bare}}=\frac{\alpha}{\pi}\frac{8G_{F}^{2}}{3}\Bigg[&\int[\mathrm{d}k]\frac{1}{k_{[d]}^{2}\,\mathcal{D}_{2}\mathcal{D}_{3}}\Bigg(2(3M^{2}-2s)s^{2}\\
&\qquad+4(3M^{4}-4M^{2}s+2s^{2})(k_{[4]}\cdot q_{[4]})-4(3M^{2}-2s)s(k_{[4]}\cdot p_{[4]})\\
&\qquad+8M^{2}(k_{[4]}\cdot q_{[4]})^{2}+8(M^{2}-2s)(k_{[4]}\cdot p_{[4]})(k_{[4]}\cdot q_{[4]})\\
&\qquad+2M^{2}sk_{[4]}^{2}\Bigg)-2\int[\mathrm{d}k]\frac{\mu^{2}}{k_{[d]}^{2}\,\mathcal{D}_{2}\mathcal{D}_{3}}s(3M^{2}-2s)\Bigg]\,.\end{split}

(3.52)

Obtaining a result such as this was the original goal of
. All
momenta are strictly four-dimensional, allowing to use (numerical)
unitarity. We, however, are not interested in unitarity, instead
wanting to use standard tools to calculate these integrals. For this
we need to be able to cancel the
k_{[4]}^{2}
-terms against denominators
containing
k_{[d]}^{2}
. Hence, we reverse (
3.51b
),
properly implementing (
3.40
)

\displaystyle\begin{split}&\text{After completing the algebra, use}\\
&\int[\mathrm{d}k]\frac{k_{[4]}^{2}}{k_{[d]}^{2}\mathcal{D}_{2}\cdots\mathcal{D}_{n}}=\int[\mathrm{d}k]\frac{1}{\mathcal{D}_{2}\cdots\mathcal{D}_{n}}+\int[\mathrm{d}k]\frac{\mu^{2}}{k_{[d]}^{2}\mathcal{D}_{2}\cdots\mathcal{D}_{n}}\,.\end{split}

(Rule III)

Now only terms of the form
k_{[4]}\cdot p_{[4]}
remain in the numerators.
These terms map directly to
k_{[d]}\cdot p_{[4]}
because external momenta
have no contribution in
{\rm QS}_{[-2\epsilon]}
. Hence, we can now safely set
k_{[4]}\to k_{[d]}
and solve the
\mu^{2}
-independent integrals using standard
one-loop calculus as implemented in Package-X. For the

\mu
-integrals, one can show that
[
106
]

\displaystyle\int[\mathrm{d}^{d}k]\frac{(\mu^{2})^{r}}{\mathcal{D}_{1}\cdots\mathcal{D}_{n}}=-\epsilon(4\pi)^{r}\frac{\Gamma(r-\epsilon)}{\Gamma(1-\epsilon)}\int[\mathrm{d}^{d+2r}]\frac{1}{\mathcal{D}_{1}\cdots\mathcal{D}_{n}}\,.

(Rule IV)

As these dimensionally shifted integrals are at most

-divergent, we only ever need their
pole which is
generally very simple to obtain. In our case,

\displaystyle\mathcal{M}^{(1)}_{\textsc{fdf}}\Big|_{\text{$\mu$-integral}}=-\frac{16G_{F}^{2}}{3}M^{4}(x-1)x\epsilon\times\Big(-\frac{1}{2\epsilon}+\mathcal{O}(\epsilon^{0})\Big)\,.

(3.53)

Thus, we have reproduced the bare
result (
3.45a
)
with techniques more amenable for automated calculations without
introducing unnecessary terms such as in
. Unfortunately, the

scheme has only been shown to work at one-loop. Worse yet, the
simplest extension to the two-loop level is known to be incorrect.

\gamma^{5}
in
fdf

We have avoided the
\gamma^{5}
problem by using the

\psi_{e}\to\gamma^{5}\psi_{e}
symmetry. However, a side effect of

Rule I
is that
comes with a hard-coded
\gamma^{5}

scheme
[
90
]
. Because all objects except
\mu
are
in
{\rm S}_{[4]}
, our
\gamma^{5}
is anti-commuting in practice. However, due
to the implementation of the
{\rm QS}_{[-2\epsilon]}
terms as
\sim\gamma^{5}\mu
, these
terms effectively commute with
\gamma^{5}
. This means that we have
implemented the
scheme. Hence,
requires the additional

Z_{5}
renormalisation.

In practice the Born matrix element is

\displaystyle\mathcal{M}^{(0)}_{\textsc{fdf}}

\displaystyle=\frac{4G_{F}^{2}}{2}\mathrm{tr}\Big[\big(\not{p}_{[4]}+M\big)\ P_{L}\gamma_{[4]}^{\mu}\ \not{q}_{[4]}\ P_{L}\gamma_{[4]}^{\nu}\Big]\times\mathcal{N}_{[4]}^{\mu\nu}\,,

(3.54)

where we set

\displaystyle P_{L}=\frac{1}{2}(1-Z_{5}\gamma^{5})\,,

(3.55)

with
Z_{5}
as in (
3.15
).

3.4
Regularisation-scheme dependence and IR prediction

As mentioned in the prologue to this chapter, we ideally want to
compute every part of the calculation in the best suited scheme. For
this it is important to understand how to convert from one scheme to
another.

For this we distinguish two different types of scheme dependence: a
trivial scheme dependence that is due to the dimensionality of the
Born matrix element and non-trivial scheme dependence. The trivial
scheme dependence is best described between
and
. As
suggested in (
3.41
) this is just

\displaystyle\mathcal{M}^{(\ell)}_{\textsc{cdr}}=\frac{\mathcal{M}^{(0)}_{\textsc{cdr}}}{\mathcal{M}^{(0)}_{\textsc{hv}}}\mathcal{M}^{(\ell)}_{\textsc{hv}}\,.

(3.56)

The scheme dependence between
and
is more interesting.
It is encapsulated by divergent diagrams involving
\epsilon
-scalars.
At one-loop we have

\displaystyle\mathcal{M}^{(1)}_{\textsc{fdh}}-\mathcal{M}^{(1)}_{\textsc{hv}}=2\epsilon\times\mathcal{M}^{(1)}_{\textsc{fdh}}\Big|_{\text{$n_{\epsilon}$ coefficient}}=\Delta_{\textsc{rs}}\,.

(3.57)

In our case,
\Delta_{\textsc{rs}}=M^{4}x(3-2x)/6+\mathcal{O}(\epsilon)
. Had we assumed a finite electron mass instead,
we would have found
\Delta_{\textsc{rs}}^{m_{e}>0}\propto\epsilon
and
hence no non-trivial scheme dependence. At
, scheme
dependence is induced by collinear
singularities
[
107
]
of which there are non for
finite electron masses. Beyond
, also soft singularities
contribute to the scheme dependence, i.e. even in the case of
non-vanishing electron mass there is a non-trivial scheme dependence.

To formalise the scheme dependence, we need to extend our discussion
of
predictions from Section
2.4
to
in

. The relevant results are given in
Appendix
B
. We begin by noting that the

8
8

8

The terms light and heavy quark are
universally used to describe massless and massive quark,
respectively.
anomalous dimension
{\gamma}_{i}
has terms proportional to

n_{\epsilon}

\displaystyle{\gamma}_{i}

\displaystyle=\Big(\frac{\alpha}{4\pi}\Big)(-3C_{F})+\Big(\frac{\alpha_{e}}{4\pi}\Big)n_{\epsilon}\frac{C_{F}}{2}+\mathcal{O}(\alpha_{i}^{2})\,.

(3.58)

The
anomalous dimension
{\gamma}_{I}
on the other hand has
no scheme dependence

\displaystyle{\gamma}_{I}

\displaystyle=\Big(\frac{\alpha}{4\pi}\Big)(-2C_{F})\,,

(3.59)

meaning that non-trivial scheme dependence is due collinear
singularities at one-loop. The cusp-anomalous dimension

\displaystyle{\gamma}_{\text{cusp}}

\displaystyle=\Big(\frac{\alpha}{4\pi}\Big)(4)

(3.60)

also has no scheme dependence at one-loop but develops a term
\propto\alpha^{2}C_{A}n_{\epsilon}
at two-loop. Up to at least two-loop,
{\gamma}_{\text{cusp}}
has

, i.e.
{\gamma}_{\text{cusp}}^{q}/C_{F}={\gamma}_{\text{cusp}}^{g}/C_{A}={\gamma}_{\text{cusp}}
.

A further source of scheme dependence in
{\bf Z}
is that we now have
to include terms induced by the
\epsilon
-scalar coupling (cf.
Appendix
B.2
). This makes
\log{\bf Z}_{\textsc{rs}}

regularisation scheme dependent as indicated by the subscript
rs
.
Note that, because
{\bf Z}_{\textsc{rs}}
matches the
poles exactly, the

\overline{\text{MS}}
-like subtracted matrix element
\mathcal{M}_{\text{sub}}(\mu)
is
regularisation scheme independent in the limit
n_{\epsilon}\to 2\epsilon
and

\epsilon\to 0
. This allows us to predict the regularisation scheme
dependence of any matrix element by predicting the scheme dependence

\log{\bf Z}_{\textsc{rs}}
using the
prediction discussed in
Section
2.4
.

To illustrate this method, let us predict the
pole of

\mathcal{M}^{(0)}_{\textsc{rs}}
. For this, we first need to write down
\bf\Gamma
as

\displaystyle\begin{split}{\bf\Gamma}_{1}(\mu)&={\gamma}_{i}+{\gamma}_{I}-{\gamma}_{\text{cusp}}\log\frac{M\mu}{2p\cdot q}\\
&=-\frac{5\alpha}{4\pi}+\frac{\alpha_{e}}{8\pi}n_{\epsilon}-\frac{\alpha}{\pi}\log\frac{\mu}{Mx}\,.\end{split}

(3.61)

We now can construct
\log{\bf Z}_{\textsc{rs}}
as in (
B.10
)

\displaystyle\begin{split}\log{\bf Z}_{\textsc{rs}}&=\frac{{\bf\Gamma}_{1}^{\prime}}{2\epsilon^{2}}+\frac{{\bf\Gamma}_{1}}{\epsilon}+\mathcal{O}(\alpha_{i}^{2})\\
&=\frac{\alpha}{\pi}\Bigg(-\frac{1}{2\epsilon^{2}}-\frac{1}{\epsilon}\bigg[\frac{5}{4}+2\log\frac{\mu^{2}}{M^{2}}-\log x\bigg]\Bigg)+\frac{\alpha_{e}}{\pi}n_{\epsilon}\Bigg(\frac{1}{8\epsilon}\Bigg)+\mathcal{O}(\alpha_{i}^{2})\,,\end{split}

(3.62)

where we have used that
{\bf\Gamma}_{1}^{\prime}=\partial_{\log\mu}{\bf\Gamma}={\gamma}_{\text{cusp}}
. By exponentiating

\log{\bf Z}_{\textsc{rs}}
we can obtain
{\bf Z}_{\textsc{rs}}^{-1}
as required
by (
2.29
)

\displaystyle{\bf Z}_{\textsc{rs}}^{-1}=1+\frac{\alpha}{\pi}\Bigg(-\frac{1}{2\epsilon^{2}}-\frac{1}{\epsilon}\bigg[\frac{5}{4}+2\log\frac{\mu^{2}}{M^{2}}-\log x\bigg]\Bigg)+\frac{\alpha_{e}}{\pi}n_{\epsilon}\Bigg(\frac{1}{8\epsilon}\Bigg)+\mathcal{O}(\alpha_{i}^{2})\,.

(3.63)

This, once multiplied with
\mathcal{M}^{(0)}_{\textsc{rs}}+\mathcal{M}^{(1)}_{\textsc{rs}}
, produces a finite,
scheme independent result in the limit
d_{s}\to 4-2\epsilon
,

n_{\epsilon}\to 2\epsilon
,
\alpha_{e}\to\alpha
, and finally
\epsilon\to 0

\displaystyle\begin{split}{\bf Z}_{\textsc{rs}}^{-1}\Big(\mathcal{M}^{(0)}_{\textsc{rs}}+\mathcal{M}^{(1)}_{\textsc{rs}}\Big)=\mathcal{M}^{(0)}_{\textsc{rs}}\Bigg(1+\frac{\alpha}{\pi}\bigg[&\Big\{3\frac{x-1}{2x-3}+L\Big\}-\frac{5}{4}L-\frac{1}{4}L^{2}-3\\
&-\frac{3}{2}\zeta_{2}-2H_{0,0}(x)-H_{1,0}(x)\Bigg]+\mathcal{O}(\alpha^{2})\Bigg)\,,\end{split}

(3.64)

with
L=\log(\mu^{2}/M^{2})
for all schemes
\textsc{rs}\in\{\textsc{cdr},\textsc{hv},\textsc{fdh}\}
.

After calculating
{\bf Z}_{\textsc{rs}}^{-1}\Big(\mathcal{M}^{(0)}_{\textsc{rs}}+\mathcal{M}^{(1)}_{\textsc{rs}}\Big)

in any scheme
rs
we can obtain
\mathcal{M}^{(1)}_{\textsc{rs}^{\prime}}
in any other scheme

\textsc{rs}^{\prime}
by multiplying with the corresponding
{\bf Z}_{\textsc{rs}^{\prime}}
. This is
a very powerful statement, allowing us to perform any part of any one-
and two-loop calculation in any scheme we wish and convert to any
other scheme. In calculations for the LHC this is particularly
important because the parton distribution functions (
s) are
usually only available in
.

Chapter 4
The FKS
2
scheme

As already discussed in Section
2.3
, cross sections
beyond
are constructed of several
divergent parts.
In this chapter, we will focus on the
divergences arising
during the phase-space integration of real corrections. As already
mentioned, we would like to do this integration numerically. However,
we cannot do this in
d
dimensions. A common way to circumvent this
problem is called a
. The basic idea is to
write the divergent integrand over the extra emission as

\displaystyle\int_{n+1}\mathrm{d}\sigma_{n+1}=\int_{n+1}\big(\mathrm{d}\sigma_{n+1}-\mathrm{d}{\rm CT}\big)+\int_{n}\int_{1}\mathrm{d}{\rm CT}\,,

(4.1)

where the subscript refers to the number of particles integrated over.

\mathrm{d}{\rm CT}
is constructed to ensure that the first integral is
finite while being easy enough so that the integral over
the one-particle phase space can be done analytically in
d

dimensions.

In the case of massive
the only
singularity is due
to soft photon emission; collinear divergences are regulated by the
presence of fermion masses. Hence,
\mathrm{d}{\rm CT}
can be quite simple as
we will see below.

In this chapter we will review one of the central pieces of this
project, the
subtraction scheme (Section
4.2
),
as well as its predecessor, the
scheme
(Section
4.1
). Next, we will comment on the possibility of
extending the scheme beyond
in Section
4.3
.
Finally, we will comment on properties of
in
Section
4.4
.

4.1
FKS for soft singularities at NLO

In this section we will briefly summarise the necessary aspects of the

scheme at
. Because we only treat soft
singularities,
is dramatically simplified. The

correction to a cross section is split into virtual and real parts

\displaystyle\sigma^{(1)}=\int\Big(\mathrm{d}\sigma^{(1)}_{v}+\mathrm{d}\sigma^{(1)}_{r}\Big)=\int\mathrm{d}\Phi_{n}\,\mathcal{M}_{n}^{(1)}+\int\mathrm{d}\Phi_{n+1}\,\mathcal{M}_{n+1}^{(0)}\,.

(4.2)

In (
4.2
) we implicitly assume the presence of the flux
factor (or the analogous factor for a decay rate) as well as a
measurement function that defines the observable in terms of the
particle momenta. The measurement function has to respect infrared
safety, i.e. the observable it defines must not depend on whether or
not one or more additional soft photons are present as arguments of
this function.

The real corrections

\displaystyle\mathrm{d}\sigma^{(1)}_{r}=\mathrm{d}\Phi_{n+1}\,\mathcal{M}_{n+1}^{(0)}\,

(4.3)

are obtained by integrating the tree-level matrix element
\mathcal{M}_{n+1}^{(0)}

over the phase space
\mathrm{d}\Phi_{n+1}
. To simplify the discussion we
assume that in the tree-level process described by
\mathcal{M}_{n}^{(0)}
no
final-state photons are present. Hence, in
\mathcal{M}_{n+1}^{(0)}
only the
particle (photon) with label
n+1
can potentially become soft. If
there are additional photons (i.e. photons in the
process)
the measurement function and combinatorics become slightly more
involved, but the essential part of the discussion is not affected.

When computing a cross section in the centre-of-mass frame, we choose
coordinates where the beam axis is in
z
direction. Further, we
denote the (partonic) centre-of-mass energy by
\sqrt{s}
. When
computing a decay width we instead parametrise one of the outgoing
particles in
z
direction and, if necessary, rotate the coordinate
system afterwards.

Following
[
108
]
we parametrise the momentum of
the additionally radiated particle
n+1
as
9
9

9

Note that this
parametrisation could also tackle initial-state collinear
singularities because
y_{1}
corresponds to the angle between the photon
and the incoming particles. However, a different parametrisation may
be sensible (and is allowed here) to better account for
from
light particles (cf. Section
4.4.3
and Section
6.4.1
).
What is important in the following is that the scaled energy
\xi_{1}

is chosen as a variable in the parametrisation to ensure a consistent
implementation of the distributions defined in (
4.8
).

\displaystyle k_{1}=p_{n+1}=\frac{\sqrt{s}}{2}\xi_{1}(1,\sqrt{1-y_{1}^{2}}\vec{e}_{\perp},y_{1})\,,

(4.4)

where
\vec{e}_{\perp}
is a
(d-2)
dimensional unit vector and the
ranges of
y_{1}
(the cosine of the angle) and
\xi_{1}
(the scaled
energy) are
-1\leq y_{1}\leq 1
and
0\leq\xi_{1}\leq\xi_{\text{max}}
,
respectively. The upper bound
\xi_{\text{max}}
depends on the masses
of the outgoing particles. Following
[
109
]
we
find

\displaystyle\xi_{\text{max}}=1-\frac{\Big(\sum_{i}m_{i}\Big)^{2}}{s}\,.

(4.5)

Further kinematic constraints are assumed to be implemented through
the measurement function. We write the single-particle phase-space
measure for particle
n+1
as

\displaystyle\mathrm{d}\phi_{1}

\displaystyle\equiv\mu^{4-d}\frac{\mathrm{d}^{d-1}k_{1}}{(2\pi)^{d-1}\,2k_{1}^{0}}=\frac{\mu^{2\epsilon}}{2(2\pi)^{d-1}}\left(\frac{\sqrt{s}}{2}\right)^{d-2}\xi_{1}^{1-2\epsilon}(1-y_{1}^{2})^{-\epsilon}\,\mathrm{d}\xi_{1}\,\mathrm{d}y_{1}\,\mathrm{d}\Omega_{1}^{(d-2)}=\mathrm{d}\Upsilon_{1}\mathrm{d}\xi_{1}\ \xi_{1}^{1-2\epsilon}\,,

(4.6)

where the angular integrations and other trivial factors are collected
in
\mathrm{d}\Upsilon_{1}
. Denoting by
\mathrm{d}\Phi_{n,1}
the remainder of the

(n+1)
-parton phase space, i.e.
\mathrm{d}\Phi_{n+1}=\mathrm{d}\Phi_{n,1}\mathrm{d}\phi_{1}
, we write the real part of the
differential cross
section as

\displaystyle\mathrm{d}\sigma^{(1)}_{r}

\displaystyle=\mathrm{d}\Phi_{n,1}\,\mathrm{d}\phi_{1}\,\mathcal{M}_{n+1}^{(0)}=\mathrm{d}\Upsilon_{1}\mathrm{d}\Phi_{n,1}\,\mathrm{d}\xi_{1}\ \xi_{1}^{2}\mathcal{M}_{n+1}^{(0)}\xi_{1}^{-1-2\epsilon}\,.

(4.7)

To isolate the soft singularities in the phase-space integration we
use the identity

\displaystyle\begin{split}\xi^{-1-2\epsilon}&=-\frac{\xi_{c}^{-2\epsilon}}{2\epsilon}\delta(\xi)+\left(\frac{1}{\xi^{1+2\epsilon}}\right)_{\hskip-3.0ptc}\,,\\
\left\langle\left(\frac{1}{\xi^{n}}\right)_{\hskip-3.0ptc},f\right\rangle&=\int_{0}^{1}\mathrm{d}\xi\,\frac{f(\xi)-f(0)\theta(\xi_{c}-\xi)}{\xi^{n}}\,,\end{split}

(4.8)

to expand
\xi_{1}^{-1-2\epsilon}
in terms of a

. Here we have introduced an unphysical free
parameter
\xi_{c}
that can be chosen
arbitrarily
[
108
,
109
]
as
long as

\displaystyle 0<\xi_{c}\leq\xi_{\text{max}}\,.

(4.9)

The dependence of
\xi_{c}
has to drop out exactly since no approximation
was made. Therefore, any fixed value could be chosen. However,
keeping it variable is useful to test the implementation of the
scheme.

Using (
4.8
) we split the real cross section into a hard
and a soft part
10
10

10

In
[
108
]
the second
term is called
\mathrm{d}\sigma^{(ns)}
for ‘non-soft’. We will label it
h
(for
‘hard’) instead to avoid confusion when we need more than one such
label later.

\displaystyle\mathrm{d}\sigma^{(1)}_{r}\phantom{(\xi_{c})}

\displaystyle=\mathrm{d}\sigma^{(1)}_{s}(\xi_{c})+\mathrm{d}\sigma^{(1)}_{h}(\xi_{c})\,,

(4.10a)

\displaystyle\mathrm{d}\sigma^{(1)}_{s}(\xi_{c})

\displaystyle=-\mathrm{d}\Upsilon_{1}\mathrm{d}\Phi_{n,1}\ \frac{\xi_{c}^{-2\epsilon}}{2\epsilon}\ \delta(\xi_{1})\,\mathrm{d}\xi_{1}\,\Big(\xi_{1}^{2}\mathcal{M}_{n+1}^{(0)}\Big)\,,

(4.10b)

\displaystyle\mathrm{d}\sigma^{(1)}_{h}(\xi_{c})

\displaystyle=+\mathrm{d}\Upsilon_{1}\mathrm{d}\Phi_{n,1}\ \left(\frac{1}{\xi_{1}^{1+2\epsilon}}\right)_{\hskip-3.0ptc}\mathrm{d}\xi_{1}\Big(\xi_{1}^{2}\mathcal{M}_{n+1}^{(0)}\Big)\,.

(4.10c)

In
\mathrm{d}\sigma^{(1)}_{s}
we can now (trivially) perform the
\xi_{1}

integration. To do this systematically, we define for photons the
general soft limit
\mathcal{S}_{i}
of the
i
-th particle

\displaystyle\mathcal{S}_{i}\mathcal{M}_{m}^{(0)}\equiv\lim_{\xi_{i}\to 0}\xi_{i}^{2}\mathcal{M}_{m}^{(0)}=\mathcal{E}_{i}\mathcal{M}_{m-1}^{(0)}\qquad\text{with}\qquad\xi_{i}=\frac{2E_{i}}{\sqrt{s}}\,,

(4.11)

where
\mathcal{M}_{m-1}^{(0)}
is the matrix element for the process without
particle
i
. The

\displaystyle\mathcal{E}_{i}\equiv 4\pi\alpha\,\sum_{j,k}\frac{p_{j}\cdot p_{k}}{p_{j}\cdot n_{i}\,p_{k}\cdot n_{i}}\ {\rm sign}_{jk}\qquad\text{with}\qquad p_{i}=\xi_{i}n_{i}\,,

(4.12)

is assembled from self- and mixed-eikonals.
{\rm sign}_{jk}=(-1)^{n_{jk}+1}
as in Section
2.4
, where
n_{jk}
is the
number of incoming particles or outgoing antiparticles among the
particles
j
and
l
. Further, we define the

\displaystyle\hat{\mathcal{E}}(\xi_{c})\equiv-\frac{\xi_{c}^{-2\epsilon}}{2\epsilon}\int\mathrm{d}\Upsilon_{i}\ \mathcal{E}_{i}=\xi_{c}^{-2\epsilon}\ \hat{\mathcal{E}}(1)=\sum_{j,k}\hat{\mathcal{E}}_{jk}(\xi_{c})\,.

(4.13)

\hat{\mathcal{E}}
has been computed for example in
[
108
,
109
]
and can be found in Appendix
C
.
This definition of
\hat{\mathcal{E}}
completes the definition of the

split (
2.40
) with
\alpha S=\hat{\mathcal{E}}
. After
\mathrm{d}\Upsilon_{1}
and

\mathrm{d}\xi_{1}
integration (under which
\mathrm{d}\Phi_{n,1}\to\mathrm{d}\Phi_{n}
) we
obtain

\displaystyle\mathrm{d}\sigma^{(1)}_{s}(\xi_{c})\

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\int\mathrm{d}\Upsilon_{1}\mathrm{d}\xi_{1}}}{{\longrightarrow}}\ \mathrm{d}\Phi_{n}\ \hat{\mathcal{E}}(\xi_{c})\,\mathcal{M}_{n}^{(0)}\,.

(4.14)

This part now contains explicit
1/\epsilon
poles that cancel against
poles in the virtual cross section. The second term of the real
corrections,
\mathrm{d}\sigma^{(1)}_{h}
given in (
4.10c
), is finite and can
be integrated numerically after setting
d=4
. Combining the real and
virtual corrections, the
correction is given by

\displaystyle\sigma^{(1)}

\displaystyle=\sigma^{(1)}_{n}(\xi_{c})+\sigma^{(1)}_{n+1}(\xi_{c})\,,

(4.15a)

\displaystyle\sigma^{(1)}_{n}(\xi_{c})

\displaystyle=\int\ \mathrm{d}\Phi_{n}^{d=4}\,\Bigg(\mathcal{M}_{n}^{(1)}+\hat{\mathcal{E}}(\xi_{c})\,\mathcal{M}_{n}^{(0)}\Bigg)=\int\ \mathrm{d}\Phi_{n}^{d=4}\,\mathcal{M}_{n}^{(1)f}\,,

(4.15b)

\displaystyle\sigma^{(1)}_{n+1}(\xi_{c})

\displaystyle=\int\ \mathrm{d}\Phi^{d=4}_{n+1}\left(\frac{1}{\xi_{1}}\right)_{\hskip-3.0ptc}\big(\xi_{1}\,\mathcal{M}_{n+1}^{(0)f}\big)\,.

(4.15c)

We have defined
\mathcal{M}_{n+1}^{(0)f}=\mathcal{M}_{n+1}^{(0)}
and absorbed one of the
\xi_{1}

factors multiplying
\mathcal{M}_{n+1}^{(0)}
in (
4.10c
) in the phase space

\mathrm{d}\Phi^{d=4}_{n+1}
. Contrary to (
4.2
), there are no
soft singularities present in (
4.15
). According to
(
2.40
) the explicit
1/\epsilon
poles cancel between the two
terms in the integrand of (
4.15b
) and the phase-space
integration in (
4.15c
) is also manifestly finite.

In (
4.15
) we see first terms of the build-up of the YFS
split (
2.40
)

\displaystyle e^{\alpha\hat{\mathcal{E}}}\,\sum_{\ell=0}^{\infty}\mathcal{M}_{n}^{(\ell)}=\sum_{\ell=0}^{\infty}\mathcal{M}_{n}^{(\ell)f}=\mathcal{M}_{n}^{(1)}+\hat{\mathcal{E}}(\xi_{c})\,\mathcal{M}_{n}^{(0)}+\mathcal{O}(\alpha^{2})\,.

(4.16)

Finally, we note that
\mathcal{S}_{i}
is invariant under rotations,
but not Lorentz invariant, because it contains the explicit energy

E_{i}
. Hence, also
\mathcal{E}_{i}
and
\hat{\mathcal{E}}
are only invariant under
rotations but not under general Lorentz transformations. The
integrated eikonal
\hat{\mathcal{E}}_{jk}
has been computed
in
[
109
]
, dropping terms of

\mathcal{O}(\epsilon)
. As we will see this is sufficient even beyond

. The expression is given in Appendix
C
, using our
conventions.

4.2
FKS
2
: NNLO extension

In the following, we discuss the extension of
to
,
while still limiting ourselves to massive
. To simplify the
discussion in this section, we assume that all (suitably renormalised)
matrix elements are known to sufficient order in the coupling and
expansion in
\epsilon
. In Section
4.4.1
we will state
what precisely is needed for a
computation.

We write the
cross section
\sigma^{(2)}
as

\displaystyle\sigma^{(2)}=\int\Big(\mathrm{d}\sigma^{(2)}_{vv}+\mathrm{d}\sigma^{(2)}_{rv}+\mathrm{d}\sigma^{(2)}_{rr}\Big)=\int\mathrm{d}\Phi_{n}\,\mathcal{M}_{n}^{(2)}+\int\mathrm{d}\Phi_{n+1}\,\mathcal{M}_{n+1}^{(1)}+\int\mathrm{d}\Phi_{n+2}\,\mathcal{M}_{n+2}^{(0)}\,.

(4.17)

The double-virtual corrections are obtained by integrating
\mathcal{M}_{n}^{(2)}

over the Born phase space
\mathrm{d}\Phi_{n}
. Here
\mathcal{M}_{n}^{(2)}
contains all terms
of the
n
-particle (renormalised) matrix element with two
additional powers of the coupling
\alpha
. This includes the
interference term of the two-loop amplitude with the tree-level
amplitude as well as the one-loop amplitude squared. Similarly, the
real-virtual contribution is obtained by integration of
\mathcal{M}_{n+1}^{(1)}
,
the interference of the (renormalised)
(n+1)
-particle one-loop
amplitude with the corresponding tree-level amplitude, over the

(n+1)
-particle phase space
\mathrm{d}\Phi_{n+1}
. Finally, for the
double-real contribution the tree-level matrix element with two
additional particles,
\mathcal{M}_{n+2}^{(0)}
, is integrated over the corresponding
phase space.

4.2.1
Real-virtual correction

The treatment of the real-virtual contribution

\displaystyle\mathrm{d}\sigma^{(2)}_{rv}=\mathrm{d}\Phi_{n+1}\,\mathcal{M}_{n+1}^{(1)}

(4.18)

proceeds along the lines of normal
because it is a

(n+1)
-particle contribution. Again we assume that there is only one
external particle, with label
n+1
, that can potentially become
soft. We use (
4.8
) with another unphysical cut-parameter

\xi_{c_{A}}
to split the real-virtual cross section into a soft and a
hard part

\displaystyle\mathrm{d}\sigma^{(2)}_{rv}=\mathrm{d}\sigma^{(2)}_{s}(\xi_{c_{A}})+\mathrm{d}\sigma^{(2)}_{h}(\xi_{c_{A}})\,.

(4.19)

For
\mathrm{d}\sigma^{(2)}_{s}
the analogy to the
case is particularly
strong because there is no genuine one-loop eikonal
contribution
[
110
,
111
]
, i.e. the soft
limit of the real-virtual matrix element is

\displaystyle\mathcal{S}_{n+1}\mathcal{M}_{n+1}^{(1)}=\mathcal{E}_{n+1}\mathcal{M}_{n}^{(1)}\,,

(4.20)

with the same
\mathcal{E}_{n+1}
as in (
4.12
). Therefore,
compared to (
4.14
) the definition of the soft part remains
essentially unchanged

\displaystyle\mathrm{d}\sigma^{(2)}_{s}(\xi_{c_{A}})\

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\int\mathrm{d}\Upsilon_{1}\mathrm{d}\xi_{1}}}{{\longrightarrow}}\ \mathrm{d}\Phi_{n}\ \hat{\mathcal{E}}(\xi_{c_{A}})\,\mathcal{M}_{n}^{(1)}\,.

(4.21)

However,
\mathrm{d}\sigma^{(2)}_{s}
has a double-soft
1/\epsilon^{2}
pole from the
overlap of the soft
1/\epsilon
poles of
\hat{\mathcal{E}}
and
\mathcal{M}_{n}^{(1)}
.

Unfortunately,
\mathrm{d}\sigma^{(2)}_{h}
is not yet finite as it contains an
explicit
1/\epsilon
pole from the loop integration. With the

\overline{\text{MS}}
-like
subtraction of Section
2.4
, we
already found one way to remove this pole by defining

{\bf Z}=1+\alpha\delta{\bf Z}
s.t.

\displaystyle\mathcal{M}_{\text{sub}}(\mu)=\mathcal{M}_{n+1}^{(1)}-\delta{\bf Z}(\mu)\mathcal{M}_{n+1}^{(0)}

(4.22)

is finite. This is (
2.29
) expanded in
\alpha
and
applied to our discussion. However, it turns out that a different
subtraction, called
, is more advantageous.
We split the real-virtual matrix element according to

\displaystyle\mathcal{M}_{n+1}^{(1)f}=\mathcal{M}_{n+1}^{(1)}(\xi_{c_{B}})+\hat{\mathcal{E}}(\xi_{c_{B}})\,\mathcal{M}_{n+1}^{(0)}

(4.23)

into a finite and a divergent piece. The pole of
\mathcal{M}_{n+1}^{(1)}
is now
contained in the integrated eikonal of
\hat{\mathcal{E}}(\xi_{c_{B}})\,\mathcal{M}_{n+1}^{(0)}
,
whereas the eikonal-subtracted matrix element
\mathcal{M}_{n+1}^{(1)f}
is free from
poles. This is again the
split, mentioned in (
2.40
)
and (
4.16
). In (
4.23
) we have introduced yet
another initially independent cut-parameter
\xi_{c_{B}}
.

With the help of (
4.23
) we can now write

\displaystyle\begin{split}\mathrm{d}\sigma^{(2)}_{h}(\xi_{c_{A}})&=\mathrm{d}\Upsilon_{1}\mathrm{d}\Phi_{n,1}\mathrm{d}\xi_{1}\,\left(\frac{1}{\xi_{1}^{1+2\epsilon}}\right)_{\hskip-3.0ptc_{A}}\big(\xi_{1}^{2}\mathcal{M}_{n+1}^{(1)}\big)\\
&=\mathrm{d}\sigma^{(2)}_{f}(\xi_{c_{A}},\xi_{c_{B}})+\mathrm{d}\sigma^{(2)}_{d}(\xi_{c_{A}},\xi_{c_{B}})\,,\end{split}

(4.24a)

where
c_{A}
indicates that the subtraction should be performed with
the cut parameter
\xi_{c_{A}}
. The finite piece

\displaystyle\mathrm{d}\sigma^{(2)}_{f}(\xi_{c_{A}},\xi_{c_{B}})

\displaystyle=\mathrm{d}\Upsilon_{1}\mathrm{d}\Phi_{n,1}\mathrm{d}\xi_{1}\,\left(\frac{1}{\xi_{1}^{1+2\epsilon}}\right)_{\hskip-3.0ptc_{A}}\big(\xi_{1}^{2}\mathcal{M}_{n+1}^{(1)f}(\xi_{c_{B}})\big)

(4.24b)

can be integrated numerically with
\epsilon=0
. Integrating the
divergent piece,
\mathrm{d}\sigma^{(2)}_{d}
, over the complete phase space we
obtain

\displaystyle\begin{split}\int\mathrm{d}\sigma^{(2)}_{d}(\xi_{c_{A}},\xi_{c_{B}})&=-\int\mathrm{d}\Upsilon_{1}\mathrm{d}\Phi_{n,1}\mathrm{d}\xi_{1}\,\left(\frac{1}{\xi_{1}^{1+2\epsilon}}\right)_{\hskip-3.0ptc_{A}}\big(\hat{\mathcal{E}}(\xi_{c_{B}})\,\xi_{1}^{2}\,\mathcal{M}_{n+1}^{(0)}\big)\equiv-\mathcal{I}(\xi_{c_{A}},\xi_{c_{B}})\,,\end{split}

(4.24c)

where in
\mathcal{I}
the first argument refers to the cut-parameter
of the
\xi
integration and the second to the argument of

\hat{\mathcal{E}}
. This process- and observable-dependent function
is not finite and generally very tedious to compute. Even for the
simplest cases such as the muon decay it gives rise to complicated
analytic expressions including for example Appell’s
F_{i}
functions.
However, as we will see it is possible to cancel its contribution
exactly with the double-real emission.

To summarise, the real-virtual corrections are given by

\displaystyle\mathrm{d}\sigma^{(2)}_{rv}

\displaystyle=\mathrm{d}\sigma^{(2)}_{s}(\xi_{c_{A}})+\mathrm{d}\sigma^{(2)}_{f}(\xi_{c_{A}},\xi_{c_{B}})+\mathrm{d}\sigma^{(2)}_{d}(\xi_{c_{A}},\xi_{c_{B}})\,,

(4.25)

where the expressions for
\mathrm{d}\sigma^{(2)}_{s}
,
\mathrm{d}\sigma^{(2)}_{f}
, and

\mathrm{d}\sigma^{(2)}_{d}
can be read off from (
4.21
),
(
4.24b
), and (
4.24c
), respectively. We point
out that
\mathrm{d}\sigma^{(2)}_{rv}
is independent of both
\xi_{c_{A}}
and

\xi_{c_{B}}
.

4.2.2
Double-real correction

For the double-real contribution

\displaystyle\mathrm{d}\sigma^{(2)}_{rr}=\mathrm{d}\Phi_{n+2}\,\mathcal{M}_{n+2}^{(0)}

(4.26)

we have to consider
\mathcal{M}_{n+2}^{(0)}
, the matrix element for the process
with two additional photons (with labels
n+1
and
n+2
) w.r.t. the
tree-level process. We extend the parametrisation (
4.4
)
accordingly to

\displaystyle k_{1}=p_{n+1}=\frac{\sqrt{s}}{2}\xi_{1}(1,\sqrt{1-y_{1}^{2}}\vec{e}_{\perp},y_{1})\,,

\displaystyle\qquad k_{2}=p_{n+2}=\frac{\sqrt{s}}{2}\xi_{2}R_{\phi}(1,\sqrt{1-y_{2}^{2}}\vec{e}_{\perp},y_{2})\,,

(4.27)

with
-1\leq y_{i}\leq 1
,
0\leq\xi_{i}\leq\xi_{\text{max}}
and a

(d-2)
-dimensional rotation matrix
R_{\phi}
. Writing the phase space
as
\mathrm{d}\Phi_{n+2}=\mathrm{d}\Phi_{n,2}\mathrm{d}\phi_{1}\mathrm{d}\phi_{2}
, the double-real
contribution becomes

\displaystyle\begin{split}\mathrm{d}\sigma^{(2)}_{rr}&=\mathrm{d}\Phi_{n,2}\mathrm{d}\phi_{1}\mathrm{d}\phi_{2}\,\ \frac{1}{2!}\mathcal{M}_{n+2}^{(0)}\\
&=\mathrm{d}\Upsilon_{1}\mathrm{d}\Upsilon_{2}\mathrm{d}\Phi_{n,2}\ \mathrm{d}\xi_{1}\,\mathrm{d}\xi_{2}\,\frac{1}{2!}\big(\xi_{1}^{2}\xi_{2}^{2}\mathcal{M}_{n+2}^{(0)}\big)\ \xi_{1}^{-1-2\epsilon}\,\xi_{2}^{-1-2\epsilon}\,,\end{split}

(4.28)

where we have used analogous definitions as in (
4.6
) and
(
4.7
). The only difference between
\mathrm{d}\Phi_{n,1}
and

\mathrm{d}\Phi_{n,2}
is in the argument of the
\delta
function that
ensures momentum conservation. Note that the factor
1/2!
is
the symmetry factor due to two identical particles.

Again, we use (
4.8
) with two new cut parameters

\xi_{c_{1}}
and
\xi_{c_{2}}
to expand
\mathrm{d}\sigma^{(2)}_{rr}
in terms of
distributions as

\displaystyle\qquad\mathrm{d}\sigma^{(2)}_{rr}=\mathrm{d}\sigma^{(2)}_{ss}(\xi_{c_{1}},\xi_{c_{2}})+\mathrm{d}\sigma^{(2)}_{sh}(\xi_{c_{1}},\xi_{c_{2}})+\mathrm{d}\sigma^{(2)}_{hs}(\xi_{c_{1}},\xi_{c_{2}})+\mathrm{d}\sigma^{(2)}_{hh}(\xi_{c_{1}},\xi_{c_{2}})\,,

(4.29)

\displaystyle\left\{\begin{array}[]{c}\mathrm{d}\sigma^{(2)}_{ss}(\xi_{c_{1}},\xi_{c_{2}})\\[5.0pt]
\mathrm{d}\sigma^{(2)}_{hs}(\xi_{c_{1}},\xi_{c_{2}})\\[5.0pt]
\mathrm{d}\sigma^{(2)}_{sh}(\xi_{c_{1}},\xi_{c_{2}})\\[5.0pt]
\mathrm{d}\sigma^{(2)}_{hh}(\xi_{c_{1}},\xi_{c_{2}})\end{array}\right\}=\mathrm{d}\Upsilon_{1}\mathrm{d}\Upsilon_{2}\mathrm{d}\Phi_{n,2}\ \frac{1}{2!}\ \left\{\begin{array}[]{c}\frac{\xi_{c_{1}}^{-2\epsilon}}{2\epsilon}\delta(\xi_{1})\,\frac{\xi_{c_{2}}^{-2\epsilon}}{2\epsilon}\delta(\xi_{2})\\
-\frac{\xi_{c_{2}}^{-2\epsilon}}{2\epsilon}\delta(\xi_{2})\,\left(\frac{1}{\xi_{1}^{1+2\epsilon}}\right)_{\hskip-3.0ptc_{1}}\\
-\frac{\xi_{c_{1}}^{-2\epsilon}}{2\epsilon}\delta(\xi_{1})\,\left(\frac{1}{\xi_{2}^{1+2\epsilon}}\right)_{\hskip-3.0ptc_{2}}\\
\left(\frac{1}{\xi_{1}^{1+2\epsilon}}\right)_{\hskip-3.0ptc_{1}}\,\left(\frac{1}{\xi_{2}^{1+2\epsilon}}\right)_{\hskip-3.0ptc_{2}}\end{array}\right\}\,\mathrm{d}\xi_{1}\,\mathrm{d}\xi_{2}\ \xi_{1}^{2}\xi_{2}^{2}\mathcal{M}_{n+2}^{(0)}\,.

We note that for
\xi_{c_{1}}=\xi_{c_{2}}\equiv\xi_{c}
we have

\int\mathrm{d}\sigma^{(2)}_{sh}(\xi_{c},\xi_{c})=\int\mathrm{d}\sigma^{(2)}_{hs}(\xi_{c},\xi_{c})
.

The contribution from
\mathrm{d}\sigma^{(2)}_{hh}
can be integrated numerically
with
\epsilon=0
because it is finite everywhere.

For the mixed contributions
\mathrm{d}\sigma^{(2)}_{hs}
and
\mathrm{d}\sigma^{(2)}_{sh}
we use

\displaystyle\mathcal{S}_{i}\mathcal{M}_{n+2}^{(0)}=\mathcal{E}_{i}\mathcal{M}_{n+1}^{(0)}\qquad\text{with}\qquad i\in\{n+1,n+2\}\,.

(4.38)

Considering first
\mathrm{d}\sigma^{(2)}_{hs}
, we perform the
\xi_{2}
integration
(under which
\mathrm{d}\Phi_{n,2}\to\mathrm{d}\Phi_{n,1}
) and use
(
4.13
) to do the
\mathrm{d}\Upsilon_{2}
integration to obtain

\displaystyle\int\mathrm{d}\sigma^{(2)}_{hs}(\xi_{c_{1}},\xi_{c_{2}})

\displaystyle=\int\mathrm{d}\Upsilon_{1}\mathrm{d}\Phi_{n,1}\ \frac{1}{2!}\ \int\mathrm{d}\xi_{1}\ \left(\frac{1}{\xi_{1}^{1+2\epsilon}}\right)_{\hskip-3.0ptc_{1}}\big(\xi_{1}^{2}\mathcal{M}_{n+1}^{(0)}\big)\hat{\mathcal{E}}(\xi_{c_{2}})=\frac{1}{2!}\mathcal{I}(\xi_{c_{1}},\xi_{c_{2}})\,.

(4.39a)

Similarly, we get

\displaystyle\int\mathrm{d}\sigma^{(2)}_{sh}(\xi_{c_{1}},\xi_{c_{2}})

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2!}\mathcal{I}(\xi_{c_{2}},\xi_{c_{1}})\,.

(4.39b)

Thus, we find again the integral
\mathcal{I}
of (
4.24c
).

Finally, we turn to the double-soft contribution
\mathrm{d}\sigma^{(2)}_{ss}
. Since

\displaystyle\Big(\mathcal{S}_{i}\circ\mathcal{S}_{j}\Big)\mathcal{M}_{n+2}^{(0)}

\displaystyle=\Big(\mathcal{S}_{j}\circ\mathcal{S}_{i}\Big)\mathcal{M}_{n+2}^{(0)}=\mathcal{E}_{i}\mathcal{E}_{j}\,\mathcal{M}_{n}^{(0)}\qquad\text{with}\qquad i\neq j\in\{n+1,n+2\}\,,

(4.40)

the
\xi
integrals in
\mathrm{d}\sigma^{(2)}_{ss}
factorise. Therefore, we can do
the
\mathrm{d}\xi_{1}\mathrm{d}\Upsilon_{1}
integrations independently from the

\mathrm{d}\xi_{2}\mathrm{d}\Upsilon_{2}
integrations and obtain

\displaystyle\begin{split}\mathrm{d}\sigma^{(2)}_{ss}(\xi_{c_{1}},\xi_{c_{2}})\ &\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\int\mathrm{d}\Upsilon_{1,2}\mathrm{d}\xi_{1,2}}}{{\longrightarrow}}\ \mathrm{d}\Phi_{n}\ \frac{1}{2!}\ \hat{\mathcal{E}}(\xi_{c_{1}})\hat{\mathcal{E}}(\xi_{c_{2}})\,\mathcal{M}_{n}^{(0)}\,.\end{split}

(4.41)

It is clear that the simplicity of the infrared structure of

with massive fermions is crucial for reducing the complexity of the
procedure described in the steps above.

4.2.3
Combination

At this stage we have introduced four different cutting parameters

\xi_{c_{A}}
and
\xi_{c_{B}}
as well as
\xi_{c_{1}}
and
\xi_{c_{2}}
.
All of these are unphysical, arbitrary parameters that can take any
value
0<\xi_{c_{i}}\leq\xi_{\text{max}}
. In total we have to deal with
seven different contributions. Two of them,
\mathrm{d}\sigma^{(2)}_{s}
and

\mathrm{d}\sigma^{(2)}_{ss}
, are very simple as they just depend on the
eikonal. Another two contributions
\mathrm{d}\sigma^{(2)}_{f}
and
\mathrm{d}\sigma^{(2)}_{hh}

can be calculated numerically with
\epsilon=0
.

The sum of the three remaining

\mathrm{d}\sigma^{(2)}_{d}
,
\mathrm{d}\sigma^{(2)}_{sh}
, and
\mathrm{d}\sigma^{(2)}_{hs}
, only depend on the
function
\mathcal{I}
defined above

\displaystyle\int\mathrm{d}\sigma^{(2)}_{aux}(\{\xi_{c_{i}}\})

\displaystyle\equiv\int\Big(\mathrm{d}\sigma^{(2)}_{d}(\xi_{c_{A}},\xi_{c_{B}})+\mathrm{d}\sigma^{(2)}_{hs}(\xi_{c_{1}},\xi_{c_{2}})+\mathrm{d}\sigma^{(2)}_{sh}(\xi_{c_{1}},\xi_{c_{2}})\Big)

\displaystyle=-\mathcal{I}(\xi_{c_{A}},\xi_{c_{B}})+\frac{1}{2!}\mathcal{I}(\xi_{c_{1}},\xi_{c_{2}})+\frac{1}{2!}\mathcal{I}(\xi_{c_{2}},\xi_{c_{1}})\,.

(4.42)

Note that, due to the sign difference and the symmetry factor,

\mathrm{d}\sigma^{(2)}_{aux}
vanishes if we choose

\displaystyle\xi_{c}\equiv\xi_{c_{A}}=\xi_{c_{B}}=\xi_{c_{1}}=\xi_{c_{2}}\,.

(4.43)

This cancellation will not be affected by the measurement
function. Thus, in what follows we will make the choice
(
4.43
), avoiding the computation of the potentially
difficult
\mathcal{I}
function.

It is possible to compute the auxiliary contribution
\mathrm{d}\sigma^{(2)}_{aux}

numerically keeping all
\xi_{c_{i}}
different by implementing the

d
-dimensional phase space mapping explicitly. While this complicates
the implementation of the scheme it can be helpful to validate the
code by confirming that physical quantities are in fact
\xi_{c}

independent. We have indeed done that by calculating

\mathcal{I}(\xi_{c_{1}},\xi_{c_{2}})
for the muon decay.

We can now collect the non-vanishing contributions, sorted by
remaining integrations

\displaystyle\sigma^{(2)}\phantom{(\xi_{c})}

\displaystyle=\sigma^{(2)}_{n}(\xi_{c})+\sigma^{(2)}_{n+1}(\xi_{c})+\sigma^{(2)}_{n+2}(\xi_{c})\,,

(4.44a)

\displaystyle\sigma^{(2)}_{n}(\xi_{c})

\displaystyle=\int\Big(\mathrm{d}\Phi_{n}\,\mathcal{M}_{n}^{(2)}+\mathrm{d}\sigma^{(2)}_{s}+\mathrm{d}\sigma^{(2)}_{ss}\Big)\,,

(4.44b)

\displaystyle\sigma^{(2)}_{n+1}(\xi_{c})

\displaystyle=\int\mathrm{d}\sigma^{(2)}_{f}=\int\mathrm{d}\Upsilon_{1}\mathrm{d}\Phi_{n,1}\mathrm{d}\xi\,\left(\frac{1}{\xi^{1+2\epsilon}}\right)_{\hskip-3.0ptc}\,\xi^{2}\mathcal{M}_{n+1}^{(1)f}(\xi_{c})\,,

(4.44c)

\displaystyle\sigma^{(2)}_{n+2}(\xi_{c})

\displaystyle=\int\mathrm{d}\sigma^{(2)}_{hh}=\int\mathrm{d}\Upsilon_{1}\mathrm{d}\Upsilon_{2}\mathrm{d}\Phi_{n,2}\mathrm{d}\xi_{1}\mathrm{d}\xi_{2}\frac{1}{2!}\left(\frac{1}{\xi_{1}^{1+2\epsilon}}\right)_{\hskip-3.0ptc}\,\left(\frac{1}{\xi_{2}^{1+2\epsilon}}\right)_{\hskip-3.0ptc}\,\Big(\xi_{1}^{2}\xi_{2}^{2}\mathcal{M}_{n+2}^{(0)}\Big)\,.

(4.44d)

The three terms of the integrand of
\sigma^{(2)}_{n}
are separately
divergent. However, in the sum the
1/\epsilon
poles cancel. The
other parts,
\sigma^{(2)}_{n+1}
and
\sigma^{(2)}_{n+2}
, are finite
by construction. Hence, we can set
d=4
everywhere (except in the
individual pieces of the integrand of
\sigma^{(2)}_{n}
) and obtain

\displaystyle\begin{split}\sigma^{(2)}_{n}(\xi_{c})&=\int\ \mathrm{d}\Phi_{n}^{d=4}\,\bigg(\mathcal{M}_{n}^{(2)}+\hat{\mathcal{E}}(\xi_{c})\,\mathcal{M}_{n}^{(1)}+\frac{1}{2!}\mathcal{M}_{n}^{(0)}\hat{\mathcal{E}}(\xi_{c})^{2}\bigg)=\int\ \mathrm{d}\Phi_{n}^{d=4}\,\mathcal{M}_{n}^{(2)f}\,,\end{split}

(4.45a)

\displaystyle\sigma^{(2)}_{n+1}(\xi_{c})

\displaystyle=\int\ \mathrm{d}\Phi^{d=4}_{n+1}\left(\frac{1}{\xi}\right)_{\hskip-3.0ptc}\Big(\xi\,\mathcal{M}_{n+1}^{(1)f}(\xi_{c})\Big)\,,

(4.45b)

\displaystyle\sigma^{(2)}_{n+2}(\xi_{c})

\displaystyle=\int\ \mathrm{d}\Phi_{n+2}^{d=4}\left(\frac{1}{\xi_{1}}\right)_{\hskip-3.0ptc}\,\left(\frac{1}{\xi_{2}}\right)_{\hskip-3.0ptc}\,\Big(\xi_{1}\xi_{2}\,\mathcal{M}_{n+2}^{(0)f}\Big)\,.

(4.45c)

This is the generalisation of (
4.15
) to
. In the
integrand of (
4.45a
) the build-up of the exponentiated
singular part
e^{\hat{\mathcal{E}}}
is recognisable (cf. (
2.40
) and
(
4.16
)). For
\mathcal{M}_{n}^{(\ell)f}
to be finite,
\hat{\mathcal{E}}
has to
contain the soft
1/\epsilon
pole. However, any choice of the finite
part is possible in principle. We have chosen to define the finite
matrix elements through eikonal subtraction, (
4.23
). This
ensures that the auxiliary contributions cancel and the remaining
parts
\sigma^{(2)}_{n+1}
and
\sigma^{(2)}_{n+2}
have a very simple
form. Terms of
\mathcal{O}(\epsilon)
in
\hat{\mathcal{E}}
have no effect since
they do not modify
\mathcal{M}_{n}^{(\ell)f}
after setting
d=4
. This means we can
set them to zero and there is no need to compute the integral
(
4.13
) beyond finite terms.

4.3
Beyond NNLO

4.3.1
FKS
3
: extension to N
3
LO

First steps towards extending universal schemes beyond
have
been made in

[
49
]
. The simplicity of

suggests that this paradigm is a promising starting point
for further extension to
in massive
, provided
that all matrix elements are known.

At
, we have four terms

\displaystyle\sigma^{(3)}=\int\mathrm{d}\Phi_{n}\mathcal{M}_{n}^{(3)}+\int\mathrm{d}\Phi_{n+1}\mathcal{M}_{n+1}^{(2)}+\int\mathrm{d}\Phi_{n+2}\mathcal{M}_{n+2}^{(1)}+\int\mathrm{d}\Phi_{n+3}\mathcal{M}_{n+3}^{(0)}\,,

(4.46)

which are separately divergent. In order to reorganise these four
terms into individually finite terms, we repeatedly use
(
4.8
) to split the phase-space integrations into hard and
soft and (
4.23
) to split the matrix element into finite
and divergent parts. In principle we could choose many different

\xi_{c}
parameters. However, from the experience of
we expect
decisive simplifications if we choose them all to be the same. Indeed,
as is detailed in Appendix
D
, there are now at least three
different auxiliary integrals that enter in intermediate
steps. However, if all
\xi_{c}
parameters are chosen to be equal, their
contributions cancel for any cross section, similar to (
4.42
).
Hence, writing

\displaystyle\mathrm{d}\sigma^{(3)}

\displaystyle=\mathrm{d}\sigma^{(3)}_{n}(\xi_{c})+\mathrm{d}\sigma^{(3)}_{n+1}(\xi_{c})+\mathrm{d}\sigma^{(3)}_{n+2}(\xi_{c})+\mathrm{d}\sigma^{(3)}_{n+3}(\xi_{c})\,,

(4.47)

all terms are separately finite and, as discussed in detail in
Appendix
D
, given by

\displaystyle\mathrm{d}\sigma^{(3)}_{n}(\xi_{c})

\displaystyle=\mathrm{d}\Phi_{n}^{d=4}\mathcal{M}_{n}^{(3)f}\,,

(4.48a)

\displaystyle\mathrm{d}\sigma^{(3)}_{n+1}(\xi_{c})

\displaystyle=\mathrm{d}\Phi_{n+1}\,\left(\frac{1}{\xi_{1}}\right)_{\hskip-3.0ptc}\Big(\xi_{1}\,\mathcal{M}_{n+1}^{(2)f}(\xi_{c})\Big)\,,

(4.48b)

\displaystyle\mathrm{d}\sigma^{(3)}_{n+2}(\xi_{c})

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2!}\,\mathrm{d}\Phi_{n+2}\,\left(\frac{1}{\xi_{1}}\right)_{\hskip-3.0ptc}\,\left(\frac{1}{\xi_{2}}\right)_{\hskip-3.0ptc}\,\Big(\xi_{1}\xi_{2}\,\mathcal{M}_{n+2}^{(1)f}(\xi_{c})\Big)\,,

(4.48c)

\displaystyle\mathrm{d}\sigma^{(3)}_{n+3}(\xi_{c})

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{3!}\,\mathrm{d}\Phi_{n+3}\,\left(\frac{1}{\xi_{1}}\right)_{\hskip-3.0ptc}\,\left(\frac{1}{\xi_{2}}\right)_{\hskip-3.0ptc}\,\left(\frac{1}{\xi_{3}}\right)_{\hskip-3.0ptc}\ \Big(\xi_{1}\xi_{2}\xi_{3}\,\mathcal{M}_{n+3}^{(0)f}(\xi_{c})\Big)\,.

(4.48d)

Once more we have used the fact that for tree-level amplitudes

\mathcal{M}_{n+3}^{(0)}=\mathcal{M}_{n+3}^{(0)f}
. As always, the
\xi_{c}
dependence cancels
between the various parts s.t.
\mathrm{d}\sigma^{(3)}
is independent of this
unphysical parameter.

4.3.2
FKS
ℓ
: extension to N
ℓ
LO

The pattern that has emerged in the previous cases leads to the
following extension to an arbitrary order
\ell
in perturbation
theory:

\displaystyle\mathrm{d}\sigma^{(\ell)}

\displaystyle=\sum_{j=0}^{\ell}\mathrm{d}\sigma^{(\ell)}_{n+j}(\xi_{c})\,,

(4.49a)

\displaystyle\mathrm{d}\sigma^{(\ell)}_{n+j}(\xi_{c})

\displaystyle=\mathrm{d}\Phi_{n+j}^{d=4}\,\frac{1}{j!}\,\bigg(\prod_{i=1}^{j}\left(\frac{1}{\xi_{i}}\right)_{\hskip-3.0ptc}\xi_{i}\bigg)\,\mathcal{M}_{n+j}^{(\ell-j)f}(\xi_{c})\,.

(4.49b)

The eikonal subtracted matrix elements

\displaystyle\mathcal{M}_{m}^{(\ell)f}

\displaystyle=\sum_{j=0}^{\ell}\frac{\hat{\mathcal{E}}^{j}}{j!}\mathcal{M}_{m}^{(\ell-j)}\,,

(4.50)

(with the special case
\mathcal{M}_{m}^{(0)f}=\mathcal{M}_{m}^{(0)}
included) are free from

1/\epsilon
poles, as indicated in (
2.40
). Furthermore, the
phase-space integrations are manifestly finite.

4.4
Comments on and properties of FKS
ℓ

With the scheme now established, let us discuss a few non-trivial
properties that are helpful during implementation and testing.

4.4.1
Regularisation-scheme and scale dependence

As we have explained in Section
2.1
, it is advantageous
to calculate the matrix elements
\mathcal{M}_{n}^{(\ell)}
in the on-shell scheme
for
\alpha
(and the masses) because it best exploits the Ward
identity. This way the only
\mu
dependence is in a global prefactor

\mu^{2\epsilon}
induced through the integral measure. The same holds
for the integrated eikonal. Hence, for the finite matrix elements

\mathcal{M}_{n}^{(\ell)f}
there is no
\mu
dependence after setting
d=4
.

A similar argument can be made for the regularisation-scheme
dependence. As discussed in Section
3.4
, the
renormalised and
\overline{\text{MS}}
-like
subtracted

\mathcal{M}_{\text{sub}}
is scheme independent for
\epsilon\to 0

because it is free of terms
\propto n_{\epsilon}/\epsilon
. The same argument
can also be made for the eikonal subtracted matrix element
\mathcal{M}_{n}^{(\ell)f}

because the integrated eikonal
\hat{\mathcal{E}}
is scheme independent, dealing
only with singular vector fields. Of course, this hinges on there
being no collinear singularities.

4.4.2
Ingredients required at NNLO

To be concrete, we list the input that is required for a computation
of a physical cross section at
in
. The important
point is that once the final expressions for a
cross
section, (
4.45
), or beyond, (
4.49
), are
obtained, we can set
d=4
everywhere.

•

The two-loop matrix element
\mathcal{M}_{n}^{(2)}
is known with non-vanishing
masses up to
\mathcal{O}(\epsilon^{0})
. In general this is a
bottleneck because the necessary master integrals are only known
for a very select class of processes, not to mention the algebraic
complexity. However, it is possible to approximate
\mathrm{d}\sigma^{(2)}
using
‘massification’ of
\mathcal{M}_{n}^{(2)}

[
5
,
112
,
113
]
(see Section
5.5
).

•

The renormalised one-loop matrix element
\mathcal{M}_{n}^{(1)}
of the

n
-particle process is known including
\mathcal{O}(\epsilon^{1})

terms. This is usually the case for
calculation as it
is needed for the sub-renormalisation
\mathcal{M}_{n}^{(2)}\supset\delta Z\times\mathcal{M}_{n}^{(1)}
as well as the one-loop amplitude squared, which is
part of
\mathcal{M}_{n}^{(2)}
. Once these pieces are assembled to
\mathcal{M}_{n}^{(2)f}
, the

\mathcal{O}(\epsilon)
terms can be dropped.

•

The renormalised real-virtual matrix element
\mathcal{M}_{n+1}^{(1)}
is known
with non-vanishing masses. Terms
\mathcal{O}(\epsilon)
are not
required.

•

\mathcal{M}_{n+2}^{(0)}
is known in four dimensions. In intermediate steps, the
matrix elements
\mathcal{M}_{n}^{(0)}
and
\mathcal{M}_{n+1}^{(0)}
are required to

\mathcal{O}(\epsilon^{2})
and
\mathcal{O}(\epsilon)
,
respectively. However, depending on the regularisation scheme,
such terms might actually be absent. In any scheme, once
\mathcal{M}_{n}^{(2)f}

and
\mathcal{M}_{n+1}^{(1)f}
is assembled, the
\mathcal{O}(\epsilon)
terms
can be dropped.

4.4.3
Phase-space parametrisation

A further issue in connection with small lepton masses is related to
the phase-space parametrisation. The phase space has to be
constructed in any way that allows the distributions to be
implemented. The easiest way to do this is to ensure that
\xi_{i}
is
as an integration variable of the numerical integrator. In addition,
for small
m
there are potentially numerical problems due to

. In fact, these regions produce precisely the
\log(m)
terms
that correspond to the collinear ‘singularities’ of the real part.
These
\log(m)
terms will cancel the virtual collinear
‘singularities’ of similar origin. Hence, for small
m
there is a
numerically delicate cancellation. This requires a dedicated tuning
of the phase-space parametrisation. We will discuss this in detail in
Section
6.4.1
.

Chapter 5
Two-loop calculation

The major bottleneck in most higher-order calculations is the
evaluation of the
n
-particle amplitude to the required number of
loops. In our case of
calculations these are two-loop
diagrams. The difficulty originates in part from the algebraic
complexity, though this can sometimes be reduced by the choice of
the regularisation scheme. The biggest problem is in any case the lack of
analytic results for the so-called master integrals. This is because
– especially in massive
– we have often a lot of active
scales some of which are internal and external masses.

There is a traditional procedure for multi-loop calculations that was
developed over the last decade for the analytic
calculations
for the
which we have adopted for massive
:

1.

Generate all Feynman diagrams contributing to the process. While this
is straightforward to do by hand for amplitudes with few external
particles and few loops, it quickly becomes a daunting task as the
number of diagrams grows factorially at higher loops or
multiplicities. Hence, this step is usually performed with a
dedicated computer program such as
Qgraf

[
114
]
.

2.

Apply the Feynman rules and perform algebraic simplification. This
also includes simplifying the Dirac and Lorentz structure of the
expression to obtain scalar quantities that can be treated later.
Common ways to do this include

•

The reduction to helicity amplitudes, i.e. fixing the helicity and
polarisation of every external particle and then employ
completeness relations and Fierz identities to simplify the
result. This works very well for tree and one-loop diagrams
involving few massless particles as the number of helicity
combinations is small. For massive particles, this is still
possible but in practice a lot more involved.

We will not be using helicity amplitudes in this project.

•

The standard approach of squaring the amplitude
\mathcal{A}
to
obtain the matrix element
\mathcal{M}=|\mathcal{A}|^{2}
and using
the completeness relation of spinors to convert the expression of

\mathcal{M}
to traces.

•

The projection onto form factors. This is done by writing the most
general expression that satisfies all symmetries of the theory
with arbitrary coefficients and fixing those by applying
projectors onto the amplitude. This procedure has the advantage of
being completely general and independent of the observable to be
calculated. However, this mechanism also falls short when too many
particles are involved because too many form factors need to be
defined.

3.

Take stock of all integrals appearing and try to find relations
between them.

4.

Calculate all remaining master integrals using various methods.

In this chapter, we will briefly discuss all these steps as a short
tutorial on two-loop calculations. As an example we will be using the
calculations in
McMule
but the discussion is far more general. In
fact, the relevant calculations were performed in
first and
only later was the abelian limit taken. We begin by discussing ways of
organising a calculation in a gauge-invariant fashion in
Section
5.1
. Next, we will discuss various aspects of the
actual loop integration, focussing on reductions to scalar
(Section
5.2
) and master integrals
(Section
5.3
) as well as the eventual calculation of these
integrals in Section
5.4
. Finally, we will discuss a
method of coping with massive fermions in
Section
5.5
.

5.1
Gauge-invariant splitting of amplitudes

Often we would like to be able to decompose our expressions into
simpler contributions. If we do not want to break gauge invariance, we
cannot use Feynman diagrams for this. Instead, we are forced to find a
different strategy to classify the contributions.

A natural strategy in
is to sort the expression by

, the result of solving the colour algebra.
While public codes are available for this (for
example
[
115
]
), it is often easier to just implement
the colour algebra directly. After solving the traces in colour space,
one is left with combinations of the different colour factors of
Table
5.1
.

When adapting results from
for
, the limit of the
Casimir operators
C_{F}\to 1
and
C_{A}\to 0
is trivial. However, the
factor associated to closed fermion loops
T_{R}n_{i}
is often written
assuming
T_{R}=1/2
s.t. adapting amplitudes may require
n_{i}\to 2n_{i}

before use.

Note that even for
without colour structure it may make
sense to separate purely photonic contributions (
\propto C_{F}^{\ell}
)
from fermionic contributions with their
n_{i}
.

Unfortunately, sorting the contributions by colour factor is often not
sufficient in
as there are just not enough different colour
factors. Assuming we have multiple flavours of leptons, as is the case
in most
McMule
processes, we can exploit this as a new
strategy
[
45
,
116
]
by assigning
(formally) different charges to each flavour. For example, the
amplitude for
\mu

e
scattering at
can be written as
