Chapter 8
Outlook

McMule
supports various phenomenologically relevant processes at

and
in
with massive fermions. The code’s
development was driven by and implemented in close cooperation with
the experiments that it will continue to serve.

When designing
McMule
we have two more or less distinct groups of
users in mind: those who just wish to calculate tailored observables
and those who wish to extend it by adding new processes. The code’s
structure serves both groups.

Defining new observables is as easy as changing a single file; in fact
we will provide a library of legacy results
[
160
]
with
the user files to reproduce all previous works by (or related to)

McMule

[
1
,
3
,
4
,
6
,
7
]
.

Thanks to the technical development of massification and
,
adding new processes at
is also relatively painless.
This is good too because the demand for massive
calculation
will not abate. If anything, it will grow as more low-energy
experiments push for higher and higher accuracy.

The bottleneck in the computation of cross sections for massive QED at

is the availability of the matrix element
\mathcal{M}_{n}^{(2)}
. These
computations are usually much simpler if some (or all) fermion masses

m
are set to zero. Unfortunately, this also spoils
.
However, if
m
is small compared to the other kinematic quantities,
an option is to start from the massless case and subsequently massify

\mathcal{M}_{n}^{(2)}
. As we have seen, this converts the collinear
1/\epsilon

singularities of
\mathcal{M}_{n}^{(2)}(m=0)
into
\log(m)
terms that will cancel
against corresponding ‘singularities’ of the real corrections. In
addition, it retains the finite
\log(m)
terms in
\mathrm{d}\sigma^{(2)}_{n}
that
are present in differential distributions. However, terms
m\log(m)

that vanish in the limit
m\to 0
will be neglected. Using full
m

dependence in
\mathrm{d}\sigma^{(2)}_{n+1}
, but only partial
m
dependence in

\mathrm{d}\sigma^{(2)}_{n}
through a massified
\mathcal{M}_{n}^{(2)}
results in a mismatch in
terms
m\log(m)
. Since the whole procedure of massification is
anyway only correct up to such terms, the mismatch should not cause
additional problems, as the terms relevant to the
\xi_{c}
independence
can be included exactly.

It should be noted that a similar procedure in
\mathrm{d}\sigma^{(2)}_{n+1}
is less
straightforward. It is not possible to naively use massification for

\mathcal{M}_{n+1}^{(1)}
. The remaining phase-space integration over the
additional particle requires a non-vanishing
m
to avoid a collinear
singularity. While this could be patched, massification relies on the
fact that the small mass is the smallest scale of the process. While
this is certainly often the case, it ceases to be true once we allow
for soft or collinear photon emission. Hence, a crucial step will be
working out the massification for real-emission matrix elements.

The extension of massification is closely connected to the problem of
collinear stabilisation, i.e. finding an efficient numerical treatment
of
s. So far we have solved this problem by dedicated tuning
of the phase-space. However, this ceases to be feasible for
high-multiplicity processes. In fact, both problems – numerical
stabilisation and massification of real-emissions matrix elements
might be solved with the same method. The idea is to subtract
pseudo-collinear regions from the integrand and add them back in
integrated form
[
208
]
. However, care must be taken
when integrating these terms analytically to retain a
fully-differential code. This is because, in contrast to

where collinear emission cannot be resolved, collinear photon emission
of an electron can very much be resolved experimentally.

To actually compute
\mathcal{M}_{n+1}^{(1)}
, we use
in conjunction with
COLLIER. The usage of
over
is already a major
simplification. However, maybe a much better strategy exists.
Fundamentally, we are not interested in
\mathcal{M}_{n+j}^{(\ell)}
but in the
eikonal-subtracted
\mathcal{M}_{n+j}^{(\ell)f}
that we traditionally obtain from

\mathcal{M}_{n+j}^{(\ell)}
. However,
\mathcal{M}_{n+j}^{(\ell)f}
is
finite
. This seems to
suggest that it should be possible to calculate it without ever
leaving
{\rm S}_{[4]}
using numerical methods. Schematically, this is similar
to the
scheme (four-dimensional unsubtraction)
[
209
,
210
,
211
,
212
]
that directly combines real and virtual corrections
using only four-dimensional quantities.

Unfortunately, just adding new processes at
– Møller
scattering and photon pair production come to mind – is not enough.
Already now,
accuracy fails to be good enough for some
applications. To reach the required accuracy for MUonE, it may become
necessary to calculate the electronic
corrections. While we
do have a suitable subtraction scheme with FKS
3
, the relevant
matrix elements are presently not known and most likely will not
become known with the full mass dependence in time. Further, even if
they were available, they would likely be very complicated analytic
functions that are not directly suited for numerical integration. The
big bottleneck here is the real-virtual-virtual contribution
\mathcal{M}_{n+1}^{(2)}
.
The electronic corrections can be constructed from
\gamma^{*}\to q\bar{q}g

[
213
,
214
]
that are only known for
vanishing quark (or in our case, electron) masses. This makes the need
for massification of real-emission matrix element even more pressing.

Whatever happens with the
calculation, it presently seems
exceedingly unlikely that we could go beyond even that to N
4
LO.
Unfortunately, a naive extrapolation of the trend observed so-far in
the radiative corrections to
\mu
-
e
scattering seem to suggest that
we need exactly that. Luckily, resummation provides a way out as most
of the corrections come from a single source: large – and predictable
– logarithms. In the framework of
McMule
, the only way to
implement resummation is adding a
that will resum the full

tower. This way, we can capture the largest contributions of
all orders without sacrificing our ability to calculate arbitrary
observables.

To summarise,
McMule
has allowed for relatively easy implementation
of
calculations in QED with massive fermions while paving
the way to
calculations. In the near future, these will be
matched to
in order to resum the
tower. Further,
more exotic technical development, such as the numerical and direct
evaluation
\mathcal{M}_{n+j}^{(\ell)f}
, is being proposed.

Appendix A
Conventions

When calculating loop integrals one often encounters factors of

\log(4\pi)
and
\gamma_{E}
. These are artefacts of expanding the

d
-dimensional spherical integration in
\epsilon
and hence not
physical. Because they will drop out in any physical result, there is
no need to include them to begin with. Hence, we remove the
relevant factor directly by defining the loop measure as
[
215
]

\displaystyle\mu^{2\epsilon}\frac{\mathrm{d}^{d}k}{(2\pi)^{d}}\to[\mathrm{d}k]=\mu^{2\epsilon}\Gamma(1-\epsilon)\frac{\mathrm{d}^{d}k}{\mathrm{i}(4\pi)^{-d/2}}

(A.1)

With this conventions, the tadpole integral reads

\displaystyle\int[\mathrm{d}k]\frac{1}{(k^{2}-\Delta)^{\alpha}}=(-1)^{\alpha}\Delta^{2-\epsilon-\alpha}\times\frac{\Gamma(1-\epsilon)\Gamma(\alpha-2+\epsilon)}{\Gamma(\alpha)}

(A.2)

Here we also have removed a factor
16\pi^{2}
that is physical but
cumbersome to write. What factors to include is somewhat arbitrary as
long as this is done consistently for virtual and real correction and
all factors that remain for
\epsilon\to 0
are added back. In fact,
Package-X
[
97
]
uses a different convention

\displaystyle[\mathrm{d}k]_{X}=\Bigg(\frac{\mathrm{i}\mathrm{e}^{-\gamma_{E}\epsilon}}{(4\pi)^{d/2}}\Bigg)^{-1}\mathrm{d}^{d}k\,.

(A.3)

Both conventions have the effect of removing unwanted and distracting
constants and are equally valid. However, they do differ at

\mathcal{O}(\epsilon^{2})
so attention must be paid for massless
calculations where the highest pole is
\epsilon^{-2}
or when
requiring the
\mathcal{O}(\epsilon)
terms of a massive one-loop
amplitude. The relevant conversion factor is

\displaystyle\frac{[\mathrm{d}k]}{[\mathrm{d}k]_{X}}=\frac{\Gamma(1-\epsilon)}{\mathrm{e}^{\epsilon\gamma_{E}}}=1+\frac{\zeta_{2}}{2}\epsilon^{2}+\frac{\zeta_{3}}{3}\epsilon^{3}+\mathcal{O}(\epsilon^{4})\,.

(A.4)

Of course other conventions exist as well.

Appendix B
Constants in
fdh

For the benefit of the reader, we will collect all relevant constants
for
calculations in this chapter. These include
renormalisation constants (Section
B.1
), the anomalous
dimensions required for the
prediction
(Section
B.2
) and the massification constants
(Section
B.3
). In all cases, the
or

limit can be obtained by setting
n_{\epsilon}\to 0
.

All results present here were previously published in
[
102
,
103
,
58
]
for the
renormalisation constants,
[
102
,
103
]

for the
constants and
[
5
]
for the massification
constants. Some results in different schemes have obviously been
published before.

All results presented use Feynman gauge.

B.1
Renormalisation constants

In this section we present the renormalisation constants
Z_{2}
and

Z_{m}
up to
\mathcal{O}(\alpha^{2})
as well as
Z_{\alpha}
and

Z_{m_{\epsilon}}
up to
\mathcal{O}(\alpha)
. With this we can
calculate processes to the muon decay as well as the electronic
corrections to
\mu
-
e
scattering.

All results (except
Z_{\alpha_{i}}
) in this section are given in the

unrenormalised
coupling in accordance to the procedure set out
in Section
2.1.2
. Further, the scale
\mu
of the
integration is set to the muon mass
\mu=M
, assuming we are
renormalising the muon field and mass. Obtaining the results for the
electron is straightforward by re-introducing logarithms from

(m^{2}/\mu^{2})^{\epsilon}
per coupling.

Using the technology we set out in Chapter
3
we find for

\delta m_{\epsilon}

\displaystyle\begin{split}\delta{m_{\epsilon}}&=\Big(\frac{\alpha_{0,e}}{4\pi}\Big)\big(2M^{2}n_{h}\big)\Gamma(1-\epsilon)\Gamma(\epsilon-1)+\mathcal{O}(\alpha^{2})\\
&=\Big(\frac{\alpha_{0,e}}{4\pi}\Big)\big(-2M^{2}n_{h}\big)\Bigg[\frac{1}{\epsilon}+1+(1+\zeta_{2})\epsilon+(1+\zeta_{2})\epsilon^{2}\Bigg]+\mathcal{O}(\alpha^{2},\epsilon^{3})\end{split}

(B.1)

A similar term is required for
n_{m}
and
m^{2}
.

The mass renormalisation is

\displaystyle Z_{m}=1

\displaystyle+\Big(\frac{\alpha_{0}}{4\pi}\Big)C_{F}\left[-\frac{3}{\epsilon}-4+(-3\zeta_{2}-8)\epsilon+(-4\zeta_{2}-16)\epsilon^{2}+\mathcal{O}(\epsilon^{3})\right]

\displaystyle+\Big(\frac{\alpha_{0,e}}{4\pi}\Big)C_{F}n_{\epsilon}\left[-\frac{1}{2\epsilon}-\frac{1}{2}-\frac{1}{2}(\zeta_{2}+1)\epsilon-\frac{1}{2}(\zeta_{2}-1)\epsilon^{2}+\mathcal{O}(\epsilon^{3})\right]

\displaystyle+\Big(\frac{\alpha_{0}}{4\pi}\Big)^{2}\Bigg\{C_{F}^{2}\left[\frac{9}{2\epsilon^{2}}+\frac{45}{4\epsilon}-21\zeta_{2}-12\zeta_{3}+48\zeta_{2}\log 2+\frac{199}{8}+\mathcal{O}(\epsilon)\right]

\displaystyle\quad+C_{A}C_{F}\left[-\frac{11}{2\epsilon^{2}}-\frac{91}{4\epsilon}-3\zeta_{2}+6\zeta_{3}-24\zeta_{2}\log 2-\frac{605}{8}+\mathcal{O}(\epsilon)\right]

\displaystyle\quad+C_{A}C_{F}n_{\epsilon}\left[\frac{1}{4\epsilon^{2}}+\frac{9}{8\epsilon}+\frac{3}{2}\zeta_{2}+\frac{63}{16}+\mathcal{O}(\epsilon)\right]

\displaystyle\quad+n_{f}C_{F}\left[\frac{1}{\epsilon^{2}}+\frac{7}{2\epsilon}+6\zeta_{2}+\frac{45}{4}+\mathcal{O}(\epsilon)\right]+n_{h}C_{F}\left[\frac{1}{\epsilon^{2}}+\frac{7}{2\epsilon}-6\zeta_{2}+\frac{69}{4}+\mathcal{O}(\epsilon)\right]

\displaystyle\quad+n_{m}C_{F}\Bigg[\frac{1}{\epsilon^{2}}+\frac{7}{2\epsilon}+\frac{11}{16}-\frac{1}{2}z+\Big(\frac{3}{2}-2z^{2}+z^{4}\Big)\zeta_{2}+8z^{4}H_{0,0}(z)+4z^{2}H_{0}(z)

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad+4(z-1)^{2}(z^{2}+z+1)H_{1,0}(z)-4(z^{4}+z^{3}+z+1)H_{-1,0}(z)+\mathcal{O}(\epsilon)\Bigg]\Bigg\}

\displaystyle+\Big(\frac{\alpha_{0}}{4\pi}\Big)\Big(\frac{\alpha_{0,e}}{4\pi}\Big)n_{\epsilon}\Bigg\{C_{F}^{2}\left[-\frac{3}{2\epsilon}+6\zeta_{2}-\frac{23}{4}+\mathcal{O}(\epsilon)\right]+C_{A}C_{F}\left[-\frac{3}{4\epsilon}-3\zeta_{2}-\frac{11}{8}+\mathcal{O}(\epsilon)\right]\Bigg\}

\displaystyle+\Big(\frac{\alpha_{0,e}}{4\pi}\Big)^{2}n_{\epsilon}\Bigg\{C_{F}^{2}\left[\frac{1}{\epsilon^{2}}+\frac{3}{\epsilon}+6+\mathcal{O}(\epsilon)\right]+C_{F}^{2}n_{\epsilon}\left[-\frac{1}{8\epsilon^{2}}-\frac{13}{16\epsilon}+\frac{5}{4}\zeta_{2}-\frac{75}{32}+\mathcal{O}(\epsilon)\right]

\displaystyle\quad C_{A}C_{F}\left[-\frac{1}{2\epsilon^{2}}-\frac{3}{2\epsilon}-3+\mathcal{O}(\epsilon)\right]+C_{A}C_{F}n_{\epsilon}\left[\frac{1}{4\epsilon^{2}}+\frac{3}{4\epsilon}+\frac{3}{2}+\mathcal{O}(\epsilon)\right]

\displaystyle\quad+C_{F}n_{f}\left[\frac{1}{4\epsilon^{2}}+\frac{5}{8\epsilon}+\frac{3}{2}\zeta_{2}+\frac{11}{16}+\mathcal{O}(\epsilon)\right]+C_{F}n_{h}\left[\frac{1}{4\epsilon^{2}}+\frac{5}{8\epsilon}+\frac{1}{2}\zeta_{2}+\frac{3}{16}+\mathcal{O}(\epsilon)\right]

\displaystyle\quad+C_{F}n_{m}\Bigg[\frac{1}{4\epsilon^{2}}+\frac{5}{8\epsilon}+\frac{11}{16}-\frac{1}{2}z^{2}+(\frac{3}{2}-2z^{2}+z^{4})\zeta_{2}+2(z^{2}-2)z^{2}H_{0,0}(z)-z^{2}H_{0}(z)

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad-(z^{2}-1)^{2}H_{-1,0}(z)+(z^{2}-1)^{2}H_{1,0}(z)+\mathcal{O}(\epsilon)\Bigg]\Bigg\}+\mathcal{O}(\alpha^{3})\,.

(B.2)

Similarly, the wave-function is renormalised through

\displaystyle Z_{2}=1

\displaystyle+\Big(\frac{\alpha_{0}}{4\pi}\Big)C_{F}\left[-\frac{3}{\epsilon}-4+(-3\zeta_{2}-8)\epsilon+(-4\zeta_{2}-16)\epsilon^{2}+\mathcal{O}(\epsilon^{3})\right]

\displaystyle+\Big(\frac{\alpha_{0,e}}{4\pi}\Big)C_{F}n_{\epsilon}\left[-\frac{1}{2\epsilon}-\frac{1}{2}-\frac{1}{2}(\zeta_{2}+1)\epsilon-\frac{1}{2}(\zeta_{2}-1)\epsilon^{2}+\mathcal{O}(\epsilon^{3})\right]

\displaystyle+\Big(\frac{\alpha_{0}}{4\pi}\Big)^{2}\Bigg\{C_{F}^{2}\left[\frac{9}{2\epsilon^{2}}+\frac{51}{4\epsilon}-69\zeta_{2}-24\zeta_{3}+96\zeta_{2}\log 2+\frac{433}{8}+\mathcal{O}(\epsilon)\right]

\displaystyle\quad C_{A}C_{F}\left[-\frac{11}{2\epsilon^{2}}-\frac{101}{4\epsilon}+19\zeta_{2}+12\zeta_{3}-48\zeta_{2}\log 2-\frac{803}{8}+\mathcal{O}(\epsilon)\right]

\displaystyle\quad+C_{A}C_{F}n_{\epsilon}\left[\frac{1}{4\epsilon^{2}}+\frac{11}{8\epsilon}+\frac{3}{2}\zeta_{2}+\frac{81}{16}+\mathcal{O}(\epsilon)\right]

\displaystyle\quad+C_{F}n_{f}\left[\frac{1}{\epsilon^{2}}+\frac{9}{2\epsilon}+6\zeta_{2}+\frac{59}{4}+\mathcal{O}(\epsilon)\right]+C_{F}n_{h}\left[\frac{2}{\epsilon^{2}}+\frac{19}{6\epsilon}+\frac{1139}{36}-12\zeta_{2}+\mathcal{O}(\epsilon)\right]

\displaystyle\quad+C_{F}n_{m}\Bigg[\frac{2}{\epsilon^{2}}+\frac{1}{\epsilon}\Big(\frac{19}{6}-4H_{0}(z)\Big)+\frac{635}{36}+14z^{2}+(8-18z-30z^{3}+12z^{4})\zeta_{2}

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad-2(6z^{4}+5z^{3}+3z+2)H_{-1,0}(z)+2(6z^{4}-5z^{3}-3z+2)H_{1,0}(z)

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad+8(3z^{4}+2)H_{0,0}(z)+(8z^{2}+\frac{16}{3})H_{0}(z)+\mathcal{O}(\epsilon)\Bigg]\Bigg\}

\displaystyle+\Big(\frac{\alpha_{0}}{4\pi}\Big)\Big(\frac{\alpha_{0,e}}{4\pi}\Big)n_{\epsilon}\Bigg\{+C_{F}^{2}\left[\frac{3}{2\epsilon}+\frac{47}{4}-6\zeta_{2}+\mathcal{O}(\epsilon)\right]+C_{A}C_{F}\left[-\frac{9}{4\epsilon}+\zeta_{2}-\frac{77}{8}+\mathcal{O}(\epsilon)\right]\Bigg\}

\displaystyle+\Big(\frac{\alpha_{0,e}}{4\pi}\Big)^{2}n_{\epsilon}\Bigg\{C_{F}^{2}\left[\frac{1}{\epsilon^{2}}+\frac{2}{\epsilon}+4\zeta_{2}-3+\mathcal{O}(\epsilon)\right]+C_{F}^{2}n_{\epsilon}\left[-\frac{1}{8\epsilon^{2}}-\frac{3}{16\epsilon}+\frac{91}{32}-\frac{7}{4}\zeta_{2}+\mathcal{O}(\epsilon)\right]

\displaystyle\quad+C_{A}C_{F}\left[-\frac{1}{2\epsilon^{2}}-\frac{1}{\epsilon}+\frac{3}{2}-2\zeta_{2}+\mathcal{O}(\epsilon)\right]+C_{A}C_{F}n_{\epsilon}\left[\frac{1}{4\epsilon^{2}}+\frac{1}{2\epsilon}+\zeta_{2}-\frac{3}{4}+\mathcal{O}(\epsilon)\right]

\displaystyle\quad+C_{F}n_{f}\left[\frac{1}{4\epsilon^{2}}+\frac{7}{8\epsilon}+\frac{3\zeta_{2}}{2}+\frac{21}{16}+\mathcal{O}(\epsilon)\right]+C_{F}n_{h}\left[\frac{1}{4\epsilon^{2}}+\frac{7}{8\epsilon}+\frac{\zeta_{2}}{2}-\frac{3}{16}+\mathcal{O}(\epsilon)\right]

\displaystyle\quad+C_{F}n_{m}\Bigg[\frac{1}{4\epsilon^{2}}+\frac{7}{8\epsilon}+\frac{21}{16}-\frac{3}{2}z^{2}+(\frac{3}{2}-4z^{2}+3z^{4})\zeta_{2}+(6z^{4}-8z^{2})H_{0,0}(z)-3z^{2}H_{0}(z)

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad+(-3z^{4}+4z^{2}-1)H_{-1,0}(z)+(3z^{4}-4z^{2}+1)H_{1,0}(z)+\mathcal{O}(\epsilon)\Bigg]\Bigg\}+\mathcal{O}(\alpha^{3})\,.

(B.3)

Note that the one-loop coefficients of
Z_{2}
and
Z_{m}
match. However,
this is clearly a coincidence as it ceases to be true at two-loop.

If we work in a theory with some massless and some massive flavours we
need a further renormalisation constant that renormalises diagrams
where a heavy-fermion loop is inserted in the light-fermion propagator
corrections. This constant starts at the two-loop level and
is

\displaystyle Z_{2,l}

\displaystyle=1+\Big(\frac{\alpha_{0}}{2\pi}\Big)^{2}C_{F}n_{h}\left[\frac{1}{2\epsilon}-\frac{5}{12}+\mathcal{O}(\epsilon)\right]+\Big(\frac{\alpha_{e}}{4\pi}\Big)^{2}C_{F}n_{h}n_{\epsilon}\left[-\frac{1}{4\epsilon^{2}}+\frac{3}{8\epsilon}-\frac{\zeta_{2}}{2}-\frac{13}{16}+\mathcal{O}(\epsilon)\right]

\displaystyle\quad+\mathcal{O}(\alpha^{3})\,.

(B.4)

Next, we need the renormalisation constants for the couplings. Those
will be given in the
scheme. If other schemes, such as the

scheme for the coupling is desired, this can be fixed

after
setting
\bar{\alpha}_{e}=\bar{\alpha}
and
n_{\epsilon}=2\epsilon
.

\displaystyle\begin{split}Z_{\alpha}&=1+\frac{\bar{\alpha}}{4\pi}\Big[\frac{\beta_{20}}{\epsilon}\Big]+\mathcal{O}(\bar{\alpha}_{i}^{2})\,\\
Z_{\alpha_{e}}&=1+\frac{\bar{\alpha}_{e}}{4\pi}\Big[\frac{\beta_{02}}{\epsilon}\Big]+\frac{\bar{\alpha}}{4\pi}\Big[\frac{\beta_{11}}{\epsilon}\Big]+\mathcal{O}(\bar{\alpha}_{i}^{2})\,,\end{split}

(B.5)

For obvious reasons, we give
Z_{\alpha_{i}}
in the renormalised
couplings. The
\beta
coefficients are

\displaystyle\begin{split}\beta_{20}&=\beta_{0}+n_{\epsilon}\Big(-\frac{C_{A}}{6}\Big)\,,\\
\beta_{11}&=6C_{F}\,,\\
\beta_{02}&=-4C_{F}+2C_{A}-2T_{R}N_{F}+n_{\epsilon}(C_{F}-C_{A})\,,\end{split}

(B.6)

where
\beta_{0}=11/3C_{A}-4/3T_{R}N_{F}
is the normal
\beta_{0}
of

. Here we have defined the shorthand
N_{F}=n_{h}+n_{m}+n_{f}
as the
sum of all active flavours, independent of mass. To convert these
results into the
scheme, we set
[
53
]

\displaystyle\alpha=\bar{\alpha}\ \frac{\bar{Z}_{\alpha}}{Z_{\alpha}}=\bar{\alpha}\Bigg[1-\frac{4}{3}\frac{\bar{\alpha}}{4\pi}\log\frac{\mu^{2}}{M^{2}}\Bigg]\,,

(B.7)

for each active fermion with mass
M
.

B.2
Infrared prediction in QCD

In this section we will give results for QCD with some massive and
some massless flavours. The QED limit is straightforward by setting

C_{F}\to 1
,
C_{A}\to 0
, and
n_{f}\to 0
.

As discussed in Section
2.4
, we use
to predict the

structure of an amplitude. For this we need to calculate the
process’s anomalous dimension
{\bf\Gamma}
with (
2.37
)
and determine
{\bf Z}
by solving the
(
2.30b
).
{\bf Z}

then shares the
structure with our amplitude after we have
performed the decoupling transformation
[
72
,
103
]

\displaystyle\zeta_{\alpha}

\displaystyle=1+\Big(\frac{\alpha}{4\pi}\Big)n_{h}\frac{4}{3}\log\frac{\mu^{2}}{M^{2}}+\mathcal{O}(\alpha^{2})\,,

(B.8)

\displaystyle\zeta_{\alpha_{e}}

\displaystyle=1+\Big(\frac{\alpha_{e}}{4\pi}\Big)n_{h}2\log\frac{\mu^{2}}{M^{2}}+\mathcal{O}(\alpha^{2})\,.

(B.9)

In
, the presence of the evanescent coupling makes the
more
complicated
[
70
]
. We restrict ourselves mostly to

again because three- and four-gauge vertices become very
complicated as soon as they involve
\epsilon
-scalars. We have

\displaystyle\begin{split}\log{\bf Z}&=\Big(\frac{\vec{\alpha}}{4\pi}\Big)\Bigg(\frac{\vec{\bf\Gamma}_{1}^{\prime}}{4\epsilon^{2}}+\frac{\vec{\bf\Gamma}_{1}}{2\epsilon}\Bigg)\\
&+\sum_{m+n=2}\Big(\frac{\alpha}{4\pi}\Big)^{m}\Big(\frac{\alpha_{e}}{4\pi}\Big)^{n}\Bigg(-\frac{3\vec{\beta}_{mn}\cdot\vec{\bf\Gamma}_{1}^{\prime}}{16\epsilon^{3}}-\frac{\vec{\beta}_{mn}\cdot\vec{\bf\Gamma}_{1}}{4\epsilon^{2}}+\frac{{\bf\Gamma}_{mn}^{\prime}}{16\epsilon^{2}}+\frac{{\bf\Gamma}_{mn}}{4\epsilon}\Bigg)+\mathcal{O}(\alpha^{3})\,,\end{split}

(B.10)

where we have defined the shorthand notation for terms involving only
one-loop quantities

\displaystyle\begin{split}\vec{\alpha}\cdot\vec{\bf\Gamma}_{1}&=\alpha\quad\ {\bf\Gamma}_{10}+\alpha_{e}\ \,{\bf\Gamma}_{01}\,,\\
\vec{\beta}\cdot\vec{\bf\Gamma}_{1}&=\beta_{10}\,{\bf\Gamma}_{10}+\beta_{01}\,{\bf\Gamma}_{01}\,.\end{split}

(B.11)

Here, we have used
{\bf\Gamma}_{mn}

(
\beta_{mn}
) to indicate the
\alpha^{m}\alpha_{e}^{n}
coefficient of

{\bf\Gamma}
(
\beta
).
\vec{\beta}
is given in (
B.6
) and

\bf\Gamma
is constructed as in (
2.37
)

\displaystyle\begin{split}{\bf\Gamma}(\mu)&=\sum_{i,j}{\gamma}_{\text{cusp}}\log\frac{\mu^{2}}{-{\rm sign}_{ij}2p_{i}\cdot p_{j}}+\sum_{i}{\gamma}_{i}\\
&-\sum_{I,J}{\gamma}_{\text{cusp}}(\chi_{I,J})+\sum_{I}{\gamma}_{I}\\
&+\sum_{I,j}{\gamma}_{\text{cusp}}\log\frac{m_{I}\mu}{-{\rm sign}_{Ij}2p_{I}\cdot p_{j}}\,.\end{split}

(B.12)

Let us go through the four anomalous dimensions appearing here:

•

The light-quark anomalous dimension
{\gamma}_{i}

[
70
]

\displaystyle\begin{split}{\gamma}_{i}&=\Big(\frac{\alpha}{4\pi}\Big)(-3C_{F})+\Big(\frac{\alpha_{e}}{4\pi}\Big)n_{\epsilon}\frac{C_{F}}{2}\\
&+\Big(\frac{\alpha}{4\pi}\Big)^{2}\Bigg[C_{F}^{2}\Big(-\frac{3}{2}+12\zeta_{2}-24\zeta_{3}\Big)+C_{F}n_{f}\Big(\frac{130}{27}+4\zeta_{2}\Big)\\
&\qquad+C_{A}C_{F}\Big(-\frac{961}{54}-11\zeta_{2}+26\zeta_{3}\Big)\Bigg]\\
&+\Big(\frac{\alpha}{4\pi}\Big)\Big(\frac{\alpha_{e}}{4\pi}\Big)n_{\epsilon}\Bigg[C_{A}C_{F}\frac{11}{2}-C_{F}^{2}\Big(2+2\zeta_{2}\Big)\Bigg]+\Big(\frac{\alpha_{e}}{4\pi}\Big)^{2}n_{\epsilon}\Bigg[-\frac{1}{8}n_{\epsilon}C_{F}^{2}-\frac{3}{2}n_{f}\Bigg]+\mathcal{O}(\alpha^{3})\,.\end{split}

(B.13)

•

The heavy-quark anomalous dimension
{\gamma}_{i}

[
103
]

\displaystyle\begin{split}{\gamma}_{I}&=\Big(\frac{\alpha}{4\pi}\Big)(-2C_{F})+\Big(\frac{\alpha}{4\pi}\Big)^{2}\Bigg[C_{A}C_{F}\Bigg(-\frac{98}{9}+4\zeta_{2}-4\zeta_{3}+\frac{8}{9}n_{\epsilon}\Bigg)+C_{F}n_{f}\frac{40}{9}\Bigg]+\mathcal{O}(\alpha^{3})\,.\end{split}

(B.14)

•

The normal cusp anomalous dimension
{\gamma}_{\text{cusp}}

is
[
70
]

\displaystyle\begin{split}{\gamma}_{\text{cusp}}&=\Big(\frac{\alpha}{4\pi}\Big)(4)+\Big(\frac{\alpha}{4\pi}\Big)^{2}\Bigg[C_{A}\Big(\frac{268}{9}-8\zeta_{2}\Big)-\frac{80}{9}n_{f}-n_{\epsilon}C_{A}\frac{16}{9}\Bigg]+\mathcal{O}(\alpha^{3})\,.\end{split}

(B.15)

•

Finally, we have the velocity dependent cusp anomalous dimension

{\gamma}_{\text{cusp}}(\chi)

[
103
]

\displaystyle\begin{split}{\gamma}_{\text{cusp}}(\chi)&={\gamma}_{\text{cusp}}\chi\coth\chi+\Big(\frac{\alpha}{4\pi}\Big)^{2}\ 8C_{A}\Bigg\{\chi^{2}+\zeta_{2}+\zeta_{3}\\
&\qquad+\coth\chi\Bigg[{\rm Li}_{2}\big(\mathrm{e}^{-2\chi}\big)-2\chi\log\big(1-\mathrm{e}^{-2\chi}\big)-\zeta_{2}(1+\chi)-\chi^{2}-\frac{\chi^{3}}{3}\Bigg]\\
&\qquad+\coth^{2}\chi\Bigg[{\rm Li}_{3}\big(\mathrm{e}^{-2\chi}\big)+\chi{\rm Li}_{2}\big(\mathrm{e}^{-2\chi}\big)-\zeta_{3}+\zeta_{2}\chi+\frac{\chi^{3}}{3}\Bigg]\Bigg\}+\mathcal{O}(\alpha^{3})\,.\end{split}

(B.16)

Beyond the one-loop level, these anomalous dimensions do not have

C_{F}^{2}
,
C_{F}n_{m}
, or
C_{F}n_{h}
terms. This implies that soft
singularities associated to these terms exponentiate – just as
expected.

B.3
Massification

Our discussion of massification was still missing the explicit
expression of
Z_{q}

\displaystyle\sqrt{Z_{q}}

\displaystyle=1+a_{0}C_{F}\Bigg\{\frac{1}{\epsilon^{2}}+\frac{1}{2\epsilon}+\zeta_{2}+2+\bigg(4+\frac{1}{2}\zeta_{2}\bigg)\epsilon+\bigg(8+2\zeta_{2}+\frac{7}{4}\zeta_{4}\bigg)\epsilon^{2}+\mathcal{O}(\epsilon^{3})\Bigg\}

\displaystyle\qquad+a_{0,e}C_{F}\frac{n_{\epsilon}}{4}\Bigg\{-\frac{1}{\epsilon}-1-(1+\zeta_{2})\epsilon-(1+\zeta_{2})\epsilon^{2}+\mathcal{O}(\epsilon^{3})\Bigg\}

\displaystyle\qquad+\Big(a_{0}(s)\Big)^{2}\Bigg\{C_{F}^{2}\Bigg[\frac{1}{2\epsilon^{4}}+\frac{1}{2\epsilon^{3}}+\frac{1}{\epsilon^{2}}\bigg(\frac{51}{24}+\zeta_{2}\bigg)+\frac{1}{\epsilon}\bigg(\frac{43}{8}-2\zeta_{2}+6\zeta_{3}\bigg)

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad+\frac{369}{16}+\frac{61}{4}\zeta_{2}-18\zeta_{4}-24\zeta_{2}\log 2-3\zeta_{3}\Bigg]

\displaystyle\qquad\quad+C_{F}C_{A}\Bigg[\frac{11}{12\epsilon^{3}}+\frac{1}{\epsilon^{2}}\bigg(\frac{25}{9}-\frac{1}{2}\zeta_{2}\bigg)+\frac{1}{\epsilon}\bigg(\frac{1957}{216}+\frac{13}{2}\zeta_{2}-\frac{15}{2}\zeta_{3}\bigg)

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad+\frac{31885}{1296}+\frac{38}{3}\zeta_{2}-13\zeta_{4}+12\zeta_{2}\log 2+\frac{13}{3}\zeta_{3}\Bigg]

\displaystyle\qquad\quad-C_{F}C_{A}\frac{n_{\epsilon}}{12}\Bigg[\frac{1}{2\epsilon^{3}}+\frac{11}{6\epsilon^{2}}+\frac{1}{\epsilon}\bigg(\frac{215}{36}+3\zeta_{2}\bigg)+\frac{4559}{216}+11\zeta_{2}+4\zeta_{3}\Bigg]

\displaystyle\qquad\quad+C_{F}n_{f}\frac{1}{6}\Bigg[-\frac{1}{\epsilon^{3}}-\frac{8}{3\epsilon^{2}}-\frac{1}{\epsilon}\bigg(\frac{149}{18}+6\zeta_{2}\bigg)-\frac{3269}{108}-16\zeta_{2}-8\zeta_{3}\Bigg]

\displaystyle\qquad\quad+C_{F}n_{m}\frac{2}{3}\Bigg[\frac{1}{\eta}\bigg(\frac{1}{\epsilon^{2}}-\frac{5}{3\epsilon}+\frac{28}{9}+2\zeta_{2}\bigg)-\frac{1}{\epsilon^{3}}+\frac{1}{2\epsilon^{2}}+\frac{1}{\epsilon}\bigg(-\frac{55}{24}-3\zeta_{2}\bigg)

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad+\frac{1675}{432}-2\zeta_{2}+\zeta_{3}\Bigg]\Bigg\}

\displaystyle\qquad+a_{0,e}a_{0}\Bigg\{C_{F}^{2}\frac{n_{\epsilon}}{4}\Bigg[-\frac{1}{\epsilon^{3}}-\frac{9}{2\epsilon^{2}}-\frac{15}{2\epsilon}-23\zeta_{2}-2\zeta_{3}+1\Bigg]

\displaystyle\qquad\quad+C_{A}C_{F}\frac{n_{\epsilon}}{8}\Bigg[-\frac{11}{\epsilon}-\frac{105}{2}+4\zeta_{2}+20\zeta_{3}\Bigg]\Bigg\}

\displaystyle\qquad+\Big(a_{0,e}\Big)^{2}\Bigg\{(C_{F}n_{\epsilon})^{2}\frac{1}{32}\Bigg[-\frac{3}{\epsilon^{2}}-\frac{5}{\epsilon}-30\zeta_{2}+\frac{85}{2}\Bigg]

\displaystyle\qquad\quad+C_{F}n_{f}\frac{n_{\epsilon}}{8}\Bigg[\frac{1}{\epsilon^{2}}+\frac{7}{2\epsilon}+\frac{21}{4}+6\zeta_{2}\Bigg]

\displaystyle\qquad\quad+C_{F}n_{\epsilon}\bigg(\frac{C_{F}}{2}+\frac{n_{\epsilon}C_{A}}{8}-\frac{C_{A}}{4}\bigg)\Bigg[\frac{1}{\epsilon^{2}}+\frac{2}{\epsilon}-3+4\zeta_{2}\Bigg]

\displaystyle\qquad\quad+C_{F}n_{\epsilon}n_{m}\frac{1}{8}\Bigg[\frac{1}{\epsilon^{2}}+\frac{7}{2\epsilon}-\frac{3}{4}+2\zeta_{2}\Bigg]\Bigg\}+\mathcal{O}(a_{i}^{3},\epsilon,\eta)\,.

(B.17)

Here, we have defined
a^{0}_{i}(x)
as in (
5.49
)

\displaystyle a^{0}_{i}(x)=\bigg(\frac{\alpha_{i}^{0}}{4\pi}\bigg)\bigg(\frac{\mu^{2}}{m^{2}}\bigg)^{\epsilon}(-2+{\rm i0}^{+})^{\eta/2}\bigg(\frac{-\nu^{2}}{x}\bigg)^{\eta/2}\,,\qquad i\in\{s,e\}\,,

(B.18)

through the unrenormalised coupling. The factorisation anomaly, i.e.
the pole in
1/\eta
, either cancels with the soft function or with a
similar contribution due to the anti-collinear jet
\bar{Z}_{q}
that is
identical to
Z_{q}
except for the
n_{m}
term

\displaystyle\sqrt{\bar{Z}_{q}}\Big|_{n_{m}}=\Big(a^{0}_{s}(m^{2})\Big)^{2}C_{F}n_{m}\frac{2}{3}\Bigg[

\displaystyle-\frac{1}{\eta}\bigg(\frac{1}{\epsilon^{2}}-\frac{5}{3\epsilon}+\frac{28}{9}+2\zeta_{2}\bigg)+\frac{1}{2\epsilon^{3}}-\frac{5}{6\epsilon^{2}}-\frac{253}{72\epsilon}

\displaystyle\quad+\frac{5083}{432}-\frac{14}{3}\zeta_{2}-\zeta_{3}\Bigg]+\mathcal{O}(a_{s}^{3},\epsilon,\eta)\,.

(B.19)

B.4
Conclusion

We now have all necessary constants to perform any calculation in

at the two-loop level. These results are three-loop ready
in that they contain
n_{\epsilon}
. However, before we can use even these
two-loop results in any actual three-loop calculations, we need to
expand everything up to at least
\mathcal{O}(\epsilon)
or even

\mathcal{O}(\epsilon^{2})
for
Z_{q}
. For
Z_{m}
and
Z_{2}
, this is
trivial because their exact
\epsilon
dependence is known in terms of
hypergeometric functions. This is unfortunately not true for the more
complicated
Z_{q}
.

Appendix C
Eikonal integrals
\hat{\mathcal{E}}

Here we give the explicit form of integrated eikonal required for
massive
. These expressions have been computed in

[
109
]
. As discussed in the text, we do not
need terms
\mathcal{O}(\epsilon)
or higher: terms of

\mathcal{O}(\epsilon)
in
\hat{\mathcal{E}}
have no effect since they do not
modify
\mathcal{M}_{n}^{(\ell)f}
after setting
d=4
. This means we can set them to
zero and there is no need to compute
\hat{\mathcal{E}}
beyond finite terms.

We start with defining a few auxiliary quantities:

\displaystyle\beta_{j}

\displaystyle\equiv\sqrt{1-\frac{m_{j}^{2}}{E_{j}^{2}}}\ ,

\displaystyle v_{kj}

\displaystyle\equiv\sqrt{1-\left(\frac{m_{j}m_{k}}{p_{j}\cdot p_{k}}\right)^{2}}\ ,

\displaystyle a_{kj}

\displaystyle\equiv(1+v_{kj})\frac{p_{j}\cdot p_{k}}{m_{k}^{2}}\ ,

\displaystyle\nu_{kj}

\displaystyle\equiv\frac{a_{kj}^{2}m_{k}^{2}-m_{j}^{2}}{2\,(a_{kj}\,E_{k}-E_{j})}\ .

(C.1)

Following
[
109
]
, the integrated eikonal can
then be written as

\displaystyle\hat{\mathcal{E}}_{kj}=\frac{\alpha}{2\pi}\frac{(4\pi)^{\epsilon}}{\Gamma(1-\epsilon)}\left(\frac{\xi_{c}^{2}\,s}{\mu^{2}}\right)^{-\epsilon}\bigg(

\displaystyle-\frac{1}{2\epsilon}\frac{1}{v_{kj}}\log\frac{1+v_{kj}}{1-v_{kj}}

(C.2)

\displaystyle+\frac{a_{kj}(p_{j}\cdot p_{k})}{2\,(a_{kj}^{2}m_{k}^{2}-m_{j}^{2})}\Big(J(a_{kj}E_{k},\beta_{k},\nu_{kj})-J(E_{j},\beta_{j},\nu_{kj})\Big)\bigg)+\mathcal{O}(\epsilon)\,,

where we have used the function

\displaystyle J(x,y,z)

\displaystyle=\Bigg(\log^{2}\frac{1-y}{1+y}+4\,\text{Li}_{2}\left(1-\frac{x(1+y)}{z}\right)+4\,\text{Li}_{2}\left(1-\frac{x(1-y)}{z}\right)\Bigg)

(C.3)

For the case
j=k
this expression simplifies to

\displaystyle\hat{\mathcal{E}}_{jj}

\displaystyle=\frac{\alpha}{2\pi}\frac{(4\pi)^{\epsilon}}{\Gamma(1-\epsilon)}\left(\frac{\xi_{c}^{2}\,s}{\mu^{2}}\right)^{-\epsilon}\bigg(-\frac{1}{\epsilon}-\frac{1}{\beta_{j}}\log\frac{1+\beta_{j}}{1-\beta_{j}}\bigg)+\mathcal{O}(\epsilon)\,

(C.4)

for the self-eikonals.

Appendix D
Explicit derivation of FKS
3

In Section
4.3.1
, we have skipped the detailed derivation of
FKS
3
. While we motivated that all auxiliary integrals can be
avoided by setting all
\xi_{c}
equal, we have not shown this because the
iterative eikonal subtracting and expanding is rather lengthy. In the
following we will go through all contributions and show that indeed
all auxiliary integrals cancel. One concession we will make for
simplicity is to set already those
\xi_{c}
equal that we have set equal
in
.

At
, we have four terms

\displaystyle\sigma^{(3)}=\int\mathrm{d}\Phi_{n}\mathcal{M}_{n}^{(3)}+\int\mathrm{d}\Phi_{n+1}\mathcal{M}_{n+1}^{(2)}+\int\mathrm{d}\Phi_{n+2}\mathcal{M}_{n+2}^{(1)}+\int\mathrm{d}\Phi_{n+3}\mathcal{M}_{n+3}^{(0)}\,,

(D.1)

which are separately divergent and that we will re-organise according
to the scheme’s prescription.

D.1
Real-virtual-virtual contribution

Let us begin with the real-virtual-virtual part that we split again
into a hard and soft contribution

\displaystyle\mathrm{d}\sigma^{(3)}_{rvv}=\mathrm{d}\Phi_{n+1}\mathcal{M}_{n+1}^{(2)}=\mathrm{d}\sigma^{(3)}_{s}(\xi_{c})+\mathrm{d}\sigma^{(3)}_{h}(\xi_{c})

(D.2)

as in (
4.19
). Using that even at the two-loop level

\displaystyle\mathcal{S}_{n+1}\mathcal{M}_{n+1}^{(2)}=\mathcal{E}_{n+1}\mathcal{M}_{n}^{(2)}\,,

(D.3)

the soft contribution in analogy to (
4.14
)
and (
4.21
) is given by

\displaystyle\mathrm{d}\sigma^{(3)}_{s}(\xi_{c})\to\mathrm{d}\Phi_{n}\hat{\mathcal{E}}(\xi_{c})\mathcal{M}_{n}^{(2)}\,.

(D.4)

The hard contribution is now

\displaystyle\begin{split}\mathrm{d}\sigma^{(3)}_{h}(\xi_{c})&=\mathrm{d}\Upsilon_{1}\mathrm{d}\Phi_{n,1}\mathrm{d}\xi\,\left(\frac{1}{\xi^{1+2\epsilon}}\right)_{\hskip-3.0ptc}\big(\xi^{2}\mathcal{M}_{n+1}^{(2)}\big)\\
&=\mathrm{d}\Upsilon_{1}\mathrm{d}\Phi_{n,1}\mathrm{d}\xi\,\left(\frac{1}{\xi^{1+2\epsilon}}\right)_{\hskip-3.0ptc}\xi^{2}\Big(\mathcal{M}_{n+1}^{(2)f}-\hat{\mathcal{E}}(\xi_{c})\mathcal{M}_{n+1}^{(1)}-\frac{1}{2!}\hat{\mathcal{E}}(\xi_{c})^{2}\mathcal{M}_{n+1}^{(0)}\Big)\\
&=\mathrm{d}\sigma^{(3)}_{f}(\xi_{c})+\underbrace{\mathrm{d}\sigma^{(3)}_{d1}(\xi_{c})+\mathrm{d}\sigma^{(3)}_{d0}(\xi_{c})}_{\mathrm{d}\sigma^{(3)}_{d}(\xi_{c})}\end{split}

(D.5)

where
\mathrm{d}\sigma^{(3)}_{f}
is finite and the divergent part
\mathrm{d}\sigma^{(3)}_{d}

is composed of

\displaystyle\int\mathrm{d}\sigma^{(3)}_{d1}(\xi_{c})

\displaystyle=-\int\mathrm{d}\Upsilon_{1}\mathrm{d}\Phi_{n,1}\mathrm{d}\xi\,\left(\frac{1}{\xi^{1+2\epsilon}}\right)_{\hskip-3.0ptc}\xi^{2}\Big(\hat{\mathcal{E}}(\xi_{c})\mathcal{M}_{n+1}^{(1)}\Big)\equiv-\mathcal{I}^{(1)}(\xi_{c})\,,

(D.6a)

\displaystyle\int\mathrm{d}\sigma^{(3)}_{d0}(\xi_{c})

\displaystyle=-\int\frac{1}{2!}\mathrm{d}\Upsilon_{1}\mathrm{d}\Phi_{n,1}\mathrm{d}\xi\,\left(\frac{1}{\xi^{1+2\epsilon}}\right)_{\hskip-3.0ptc}\xi^{2}\Big(\hat{\mathcal{E}}(\xi_{c})^{2}\mathcal{M}_{n+1}^{(0)}\Big)\equiv-\frac{1}{2!}\mathcal{J}(\xi_{c})\,.

(D.6b)

Above we have defined two functions
\mathcal{I}^{(1)}
and

\mathcal{J}
that are potentially tedious to compute. However, as we
will see they cancel in the final result, similar to the function

\mathcal{I}
at
.

D.2
Real-real-virtual contribution

The real-real-virtual contribution are similar to the double-real
contribution of

\displaystyle\qquad\mathrm{d}\sigma^{(3)}_{rrv}=\mathrm{d}\Phi_{n+2}\mathcal{M}_{n+2}^{(1)}=\mathrm{d}\sigma^{(3)}_{ss}(\xi_{c})+\mathrm{d}\sigma^{(3)}_{sh}(\xi_{c})+\mathrm{d}\sigma^{(3)}_{hs}(\xi_{c})+\mathrm{d}\sigma^{(3)}_{hh}(\xi_{c})\,,

(D.7a)

\displaystyle\left\{\begin{array}[]{c}\mathrm{d}\sigma^{(3)}_{ss}(\xi_{c})\\[5.0pt]
\mathrm{d}\sigma^{(3)}_{hs}(\xi_{c})\\[5.0pt]
\mathrm{d}\sigma^{(3)}_{sh}(\xi_{c})\\[5.0pt]
\mathrm{d}\sigma^{(3)}_{hh}(\xi_{c})\end{array}\right\}=\mathrm{d}\Upsilon_{1}\mathrm{d}\Upsilon_{2}\mathrm{d}\Phi_{n,2}\ \frac{1}{2!}\ \left\{\begin{array}[]{c}\frac{\xi_{c}^{-2\epsilon}}{2\epsilon}\delta(\xi_{1})\,\frac{\xi_{c}^{-2\epsilon}}{2\epsilon}\delta(\xi_{2})\\[3.0pt]
-\frac{\xi_{c}^{-2\epsilon}}{2\epsilon}\delta(\xi_{2})\,\left(\frac{1}{\xi_{1}^{1+2\epsilon}}\right)_{\hskip-3.0ptc}\\[3.0pt]
-\frac{\xi_{c}^{-2\epsilon}}{2\epsilon}\delta(\xi_{1})\,\left(\frac{1}{\xi_{2}^{1+2\epsilon}}\right)_{\hskip-3.0ptc}\\[3.0pt]
\left(\frac{1}{\xi_{1}^{1+2\epsilon}}\right)_{\hskip-3.0ptc}\,\left(\frac{1}{\xi_{2}^{1+2\epsilon}}\right)_{\hskip-3.0ptc}\end{array}\right\}\,\mathrm{d}\xi_{1}\,\mathrm{d}\xi_{2}\ \xi_{1}^{2}\xi_{2}^{2}\mathcal{M}_{n+2}^{(1)}\,.

Obviously
\int\mathrm{d}\sigma^{(3)}_{hs}=\int\mathrm{d}\sigma^{(3)}_{sh}
and

\displaystyle\int\mathrm{d}\sigma^{(3)}_{hs}(\xi_{c})

\displaystyle=\mathrm{d}\Upsilon_{1}\mathrm{d}\Upsilon_{2}\mathrm{d}\Phi_{n,2}\ \frac{1}{2!}\ \int\mathrm{d}\xi_{1}\ \left(\frac{1}{\xi_{1}^{1+2\epsilon}}\right)_{\hskip-3.0ptc}\big(\xi_{1}^{2}\mathcal{M}_{n+1}^{(1)}\big)\hat{\mathcal{E}}(\xi_{c})=\frac{1}{2!}\,\mathcal{I}^{(1)}(\xi_{c})\,.

(D.8)

Furthermore, as for (
4.41
) we find

\displaystyle\mathrm{d}\sigma^{(3)}_{ss}(\xi_{c})

\displaystyle\to\ \mathrm{d}\Phi_{n}\frac{1}{2!}\hat{\mathcal{E}}(\xi_{c})^{2}\mathcal{M}_{n}^{(1)}\,.

(D.9)

The hard contribution is not yet finite due to the
explicit
1/\epsilon
pole in
\mathcal{M}_{n+2}^{(1)}
. As is customary by now we
again perform an eikonal subtraction

\displaystyle\mathcal{M}_{n+2}^{(1)}\equiv\mathcal{M}_{n+2}^{(1)f}(\xi_{c})-\hat{\mathcal{E}}(\xi_{c})\,\mathcal{M}_{n+2}^{(0)}\,.

(D.10)

and write

\displaystyle\mathrm{d}\sigma^{(3)}_{hh}(\xi_{c})

\displaystyle=\mathrm{d}\sigma^{(3)}_{hf}(\xi_{c})+\mathrm{d}\sigma^{(3)}_{hd}(\xi_{c})\,,

(D.11a)

\displaystyle\mathrm{d}\sigma^{(3)}_{hf}(\xi_{c})

\displaystyle=\mathrm{d}\Upsilon_{1}\mathrm{d}\Upsilon_{2}\mathrm{d}\Phi_{n,2}\ \frac{1}{2!}\ \left(\frac{1}{\xi_{1}^{1+2\epsilon}}\right)_{\hskip-3.0ptc}\,\left(\frac{1}{\xi_{2}^{1+2\epsilon}}\right)_{\hskip-3.0ptc}\,\xi_{1}^{2}\xi_{2}^{2}\mathcal{M}_{n+2}^{(1)f}(\xi_{c})\,,

(D.11b)

\displaystyle\int\mathrm{d}\sigma^{(3)}_{hd}(\xi_{c})

\displaystyle=-\int\mathrm{d}\Upsilon_{1}\mathrm{d}\Upsilon_{2}\mathrm{d}\Phi_{n,2}\ \frac{1}{2!}\ \left(\frac{1}{\xi_{1}^{1+2\epsilon}}\right)_{\hskip-3.0ptc}\,\left(\frac{1}{\xi_{2}^{1+2\epsilon}}\right)_{\hskip-3.0ptc}\,\xi_{1}^{2}\xi_{2}^{2}\hat{\mathcal{E}}(\xi_{c})\,\mathcal{M}_{n+2}^{(0)}\equiv-\frac{1}{2!}\mathcal{K}(\xi_{c})\,.

(D.11c)

Here we have defined a third auxiliary function
\mathcal{K}
that
will cancel in the final result.

D.3
Triple-real contributions

The evaluation of the triple-real contributions proceeds along the
lines of the
double-real part, albeit with more (individually

\xi_{c}
dependent) terms

\displaystyle\mathrm{d}\sigma^{(3)}_{rrr}

\displaystyle=\mathrm{d}\Phi_{n+3}\mathcal{M}_{n+3}^{(0)}=\mathrm{d}\sigma^{(3)}_{hhh}+\underbrace{\mathrm{d}\sigma^{(3)}_{hhs}+\mathrm{d}\sigma^{(3)}_{hsh}+\mathrm{d}\sigma^{(3)}_{shh}}_{3\mathrm{d}\sigma^{(3)}_{hhs}}+\underbrace{\mathrm{d}\sigma^{(3)}_{hss}+\mathrm{d}\sigma^{(3)}_{shs}+\mathrm{d}\sigma^{(3)}_{ssh}}_{3\mathrm{d}\sigma^{(3)}_{hss}}+\mathrm{d}\sigma^{(3)}_{sss}\,.

(D.12)

Because we choose all
\xi_{c}
equal, it does not matter which photon is
soft, just how many. Thus, we are left with four different kinds of
contributions

\displaystyle\begin{split}\left\{\begin{array}[]{c}\mathrm{d}\sigma^{(3)}_{sss}(\xi_{c})\\[5.0pt]
\mathrm{d}\sigma^{(3)}_{hss}(\xi_{c})\\[5.0pt]
\mathrm{d}\sigma^{(3)}_{hhs}(\xi_{c})\\[5.0pt]
\mathrm{d}\sigma^{(3)}_{hhh}(\xi_{c})\end{array}\right\}&=\prod_{i=1}^{3}\Big(\mathrm{d}\Upsilon_{i}\mathrm{d}\xi_{i}\,\xi_{i}^{2}\Big)\ \mathrm{d}\Phi_{n,3}\frac{\mathcal{M}_{n+3}^{(0)}}{3!}\ \left\{\begin{array}[]{c}\frac{\xi_{c}^{-2\epsilon}}{2\epsilon}\delta(\xi_{1})\,\frac{\xi_{c}^{-2\epsilon}}{2\epsilon}\delta(\xi_{2})\,\frac{\xi_{c}^{-2\epsilon}}{2\epsilon}\delta(\xi_{3})\\[3.0pt]
\frac{\xi_{c}^{-2\epsilon}}{2\epsilon}\delta(\xi_{2})\,\frac{\xi_{c}^{-2\epsilon}}{2\epsilon}\delta(\xi_{3})\,\left(\frac{1}{\xi_{1}^{1+2\epsilon}}\right)_{\hskip-3.0ptc}\\[3.0pt]
-\frac{\xi_{c}^{-2\epsilon}}{2\epsilon}\delta(\xi_{3})\,\left(\frac{1}{\xi_{1}^{1+2\epsilon}}\right)_{\hskip-3.0ptc}\left(\frac{1}{\xi_{2}^{1+2\epsilon}}\right)_{\hskip-3.0ptc}\\[3.0pt]
\left(\frac{1}{\xi_{1}^{1+2\epsilon}}\right)_{\hskip-3.0ptc}\,\left(\frac{1}{\xi_{2}^{1+2\epsilon}}\right)_{\hskip-3.0ptc}\,\left(\frac{1}{\xi_{3}^{1+2\epsilon}}\right)_{\hskip-3.0ptc}\end{array}\right\}\,.\end{split}

(D.13)

The triple-hard
\mathrm{d}\sigma^{(3)}_{hhh}
contribution is finite and can be
integrated numerically. For the triple-soft
\mathrm{d}\sigma^{(3)}_{sss}
we get

\displaystyle\mathrm{d}\sigma^{(3)}_{sss}(\xi_{c})

\displaystyle=\mathrm{d}\Phi_{n}\,\frac{1}{3!}\hat{\mathcal{E}}^{3}\,\mathcal{M}_{n}^{(0)}\,.

(D.14)

The double-soft contribution can be expressed in terms of the function

\mathcal{J}(\xi_{c})
as

\displaystyle\int\mathrm{d}\sigma^{(3)}_{hss}(\xi_{c})

\displaystyle=\int\mathrm{d}\Upsilon_{1}\mathrm{d}\Phi_{n,1}\ \frac{1}{3!}\ \hat{\mathcal{E}}(\xi_{c})^{2}\left(\frac{1}{\xi_{1}^{1+2\epsilon}}\right)_{\hskip-3.0ptc}\mathrm{d}\xi_{1}\ \xi_{1}^{2}\mathcal{M}_{n+1}^{(0)}=\frac{1}{3!}\mathcal{J}(\xi_{c})\,.

(D.15)

Similarly, the single-soft contribution

\displaystyle\int\mathrm{d}\sigma^{(3)}_{hhs}(\xi_{c})

\displaystyle=\int\mathrm{d}\Upsilon_{1}\mathrm{d}\Upsilon_{2}\mathrm{d}\Phi_{n,2}\ \frac{1}{3!}\ \hat{\mathcal{E}}(\xi_{c})\left(\frac{1}{\xi_{1}^{1+2\epsilon}}\right)_{\hskip-3.0ptc}\left(\frac{1}{\xi_{2}^{1+2\epsilon}}\right)_{\hskip-3.0ptc}\,\mathrm{d}\xi_{1}\,\mathrm{d}\xi_{2}\ \xi_{1}^{2}\xi_{2}^{2}\mathcal{M}_{n+2}^{(0)}=\frac{1}{3!}\mathcal{K}(\xi_{c})

(D.16)

involves the auxiliary function
\mathcal{K}
.

D.4
Combination

Combining all contributions at
we need to evaluate
(
4.47
). Collecting the terms with an
n
-parton phase
space we get

\displaystyle\begin{split}\mathrm{d}\sigma^{(3)}_{n}(\xi_{c})&=\int\mathrm{d}\Phi_{n}\Big(\mathcal{M}_{n}^{(3)}+\underbrace{\hat{\mathcal{E}}(\xi_{c})\mathcal{M}_{n}^{(2)}}_{\mathrm{d}\sigma^{(3)}_{s}}+\underbrace{\frac{1}{2!}\hat{\mathcal{E}}(\xi_{c})^{2}\mathcal{M}_{n}^{(1)}}_{\mathrm{d}\sigma^{(3)}_{ss}}+\underbrace{1\times\frac{1}{3!}\hat{\mathcal{E}}(\xi_{c})^{3}\mathcal{M}_{n}^{(0)}}_{\mathrm{d}\sigma^{(3)}_{sss}}\Big)\\
&\qquad\underbrace{-\mathcal{I}(\xi_{c})-\frac{1}{2!}\mathcal{J}(\xi_{c})}_{\mathrm{d}\sigma^{(3)}_{d}}+\underbrace{\frac{1}{2!}\mathcal{I}(\xi_{c})+\frac{1}{2!}\mathcal{I}(\xi_{c})}_{\mathrm{d}\sigma^{(3)}_{hs}+\mathrm{d}\sigma^{(3)}_{sh}}\underbrace{-\frac{1}{2!}\mathcal{K}(\xi_{c})}_{\mathrm{d}\sigma^{(3)}_{hd}}+\underbrace{3\times\frac{1}{3!}\mathcal{J}(\xi_{c})}_{\mathrm{d}\sigma^{(3)}_{hss}+\cdots}+\underbrace{3\times\frac{1}{3!}\mathcal{K}(\xi_{c})}_{\mathrm{d}\sigma^{(3)}_{hhs}+\cdots}\,.\end{split}

(D.17)

The auxiliary integrals
\mathcal{I}^{(1)}
,
\mathcal{J}
and

\mathcal{K}
cancel as do the explicit
1/\epsilon
poles in the
first line. The other contributions in (
4.47
) are also
separately finite. Thus, after setting
d=4
the explicit expressions
of the separately finite parts of (
4.47
) are given by
(
4.48
) with

\displaystyle\mathrm{d}\sigma^{(3)}_{n+1}(\xi_{c})

\displaystyle=\mathrm{d}\sigma^{(3)}_{f}\,;

\displaystyle\mathrm{d}\sigma^{(3)}_{n+2}(\xi_{c})

\displaystyle=\mathrm{d}\sigma^{(3)}_{hf}\,;

\displaystyle\mathrm{d}\sigma^{(3)}_{n+3}(\xi_{c})

\displaystyle=\mathrm{d}\sigma^{(3)}_{hhh}\,.

(D.18)

Comparing (
D.18
) to (
4.44c
) and
(
4.44d
) reveals the pattern of how to extend beyond

as done in Section
4.3.2
.
